My attention has now been called to it, and I cannot now avoid seeing it appears to me that the words "City of Limerick," in No. 4, are different from the other parts of the body of the letter; and it appears to me that the figures "18" and "19" are different from the figures and date
"1839"—the "BC" I cannot judge of, because it is printed—I think the word "March," in No. 3, in the date, is in a different handwriting from the body of the letter; and also in this letter, the "18" and "19" differ from the figures in this date—the margin, I should say, is different from the body of the letter.
WILLIAM MARCH . I am secretary to the Brazilian Mining Company—Mr. George Haythorn is one of the proprietors of that company—there are a great many proprietors. On the 20th of March I received advices from Messrs. Came, of Falmouth, of the arrival of two boxes of gold dust, marked "B C 18 and 19"—the letter of advice does not mention the "B C "but the numbers—they were to come by the Sea Gull —a subsequent, advice mentioned the letters "B C" as well as the numbers—it would be known among the shipping people in London that very morning, the 20th of March, that the Sea Gull had arrived—on the 25th I received further advices from Messrs. Carne, informing me of the transmission of the boxes of gold dust by the City of Limerick —I never authorised Moss, or any body else, to go to the Dublin Steam Wharf, and make use of my name, to receive them—I did not see this letter (No. 4) before the boxes were gone—the signature to this letter bears a similarity to Mr. Carne's—the handwriting of the body does not appear to me to be the same, and it strikes me as a very informal, unmercantile sort of letter, which it is very unlikely any merchant should sign—there is nothing else which strikes me—( looking at No. 3)—I do not think the whole of this is one handwriting, and it is a letter very unlikely to be signed by Messrs. Carne—my name is wrongly spelt, which Messrs. Carne are perfectly acquainted with, as they are constantly in the habit of writing to me, and it designates me as the agent to the Brazilian Mining Company, which is not correct—my name is not correctly spelt in No. 4—my correct designation is Secretary to the Company.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. The signature te No. 4 you say it different from the body? A. It appears so, and it is a very unlikely letter for a commercial man to write—my own name is also spelt wrong—the signature of Messrs. Came in No. 3 is different from the body—I am not aware that I can make any other remark as to the handwriting.
GEORGE CARTHEW . I am servant to the Brazilian Company. I was sent on the 25th of March to the Dublin Steam Packet office, Mr. Hartley's, John-street, Crutched-friars—I saw one of the clerks—I do not know his name—I do not see any one in Court that I saw on that occasion—( looking at the prisoners)—when I looked round I certainly did not see the young man I see now in Court, the younger Caspar—I am not certain that I saw him there that day—I would not undertake to swear to him by any means.
JOHN ROB . I am an officer of the City. I was called in, in consequence of this robbery, on the 26th of March—I went to the counting-house to John-street—I did not see Lewin Caspar there—I saw him that day at the St. Paul's coffee-house—I asked him if he had two letters with him, the one purporting to come from Falmouth, and the other from March and C., of Broad-street—he said yes, and produced them to me—these are the letters (Nos. 3 and 4)—No. 4 was in the state it is now, with the back torn off—I said, "I suppose you know to whom that was addressed"—he said no, he did not—I said, "Was it in that state when it Wfis delivered to you by the gentleman?"—he said he could not say whether it was torn off when he received it, or since—he said, "All I have to look at is to see whether it
is a genuine signature or no"—that observation was not in answer to any question of mine—he said he believed it to be a genuine signature—nothing further occurred then—in consequence of information which reached me, on the 1st of April, Easter Monday, I went to a house in New-street, Whitechapel-road—there was nobody living there—I went on to Mansel-street, to Moss's lodging—Mr. Hartley was with me—I searched Moss's lodging, but found nothing—while I was there I saw the two Caspars in Mansel-street—Mr. Hartley asked young Caspar what he did in that street—he said, "I understood that you had searched a house, and found the gold, and I came to satisfy myself about that"—I went to the counting-house in John-street on the Tuesday, and saw Lewin Caspar—I asked him if he knew a person of the name of Moss—he said, "Yes, two or three"—I said, "Where do they live?"—he said, "One was my father's foreman. once"—I said, "Where does he live?"—he said, "I don't know"—I said, "When did you see him last?"—he said, "About seven months ago"—I said, "What was your business with him?"—he said, "I merely met him by accident, and asked him How he did"—he was then given into my charge by Mr. Hartley, Lea the officer was with me—he declared that he was innocent of the charge—I then took him to Giltspur-street Counter, where I searched him, and found these two documents on him—( producing them)—after leaving him in custody, I went to the house of the elder Caspar, in Finsbury-pavement—I found him there, and asked if he knew a person of the name of Moss—he said, "Yes, I do, three or four"—I said, "Where do they live?" he said, One was my foreman once"—I said, "Where does he live?" he said, o' He lives the second, third or fourth turning down the Commercial-road"—I said,. "What is the name of the street?" he said, "I don't know"—I said, "What is the number of the house?" he said, "I don't know"—I said, "When did you see him last?" he said, "Last Saturday"—I said., "What was your business with him?" he said, "I went to him respecting two watches he had to repair for me"—I said, "To whom did they belong?" he said, "To myself, one was a gold watch, which was my own wear"—I said, "When did you see him previous to that?" he said, "On the Monday"—I said, "What was your business with him then?" he said, "Respecting the same two watches"—I said, "When did you see him previous to that?" he said, "About a fortnight or three weeks previous"—Mr. Hartley was with me, and he gave him into my custody—he declared he knew nothing about it—it was stated what he was taken into custody for—we wished to do it as quietly as we could, on account of his wife—Moss surrendered himself into my custody about the 17th of April—I received some bank notes and a memorandum from him—( looking at them)—this is the I O U, I have no doubt, and these are the notes—there are thirteen £10 notes, and two £5 notes.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. By whose direction was it that you subjected these two men to this inquisition of yours? A. By my own—I thought it right to ask the questions—I thought it was fair—I did not give them notice that I intended to make their answers evidence—I do not think that unfair—I put the questions fairly to them—I think it is my. duty to ask questions.
Q. Have you not heard such questions constantly animadverted upon by the Judges in this Court? A. Possibly I might by some—I have.
Q. If a prisoner said to you in answer to a question, "Possibly I
might," and then said, "I have not a doubt about it," would not you consider that an equivocation? A. It would depend on the question I put—no doubt I might consider it an equivocation—I gave that answer to you because I thought the question very improper—how have I equivocated?
Q. You have sworn if another gave you the same answer you gave me, you would consider it an equivocation, don't you then consider it equivocation in yourself? A. I have nothing to say upon that—perhaps I do not adopt the same line with respect to my own answers that I do with the answers of another—that is my answer—I have adopted every thing that is fair—I make no distinction between myself and others—I have nothing to say about it.
Q. As you admit the learned Judges have said over and over again, that it is improper to cross-examine prisoners, why did you do it? A. I do not call it cross-examining—I have heard some Judges blamed for putting questions, and some I have heard the reverse—I don't remember the name of any Judge who has recommended that course, but I do say I have heard it—it was given publicly in Court—I can't name any Judge who has done so—I thought it fair to put the questions I did—if I had done wrong I should like it done towards myself—I did not tell them I had come to take them into custody—I put the questions for the purpose of getting at the truth of the matter.
Q. On your oath, was it not for the purpose of making evidence of their answers? A. I have given you an answer—I could not tell whether their answers would be brought in evidence here or no—no doubt some part of it would be evidence—I put the questions to come at the truth, and to see whether they told the same story I had heard from other persons as to the knowledge of Moss—no question their answers would be evidence.
Q. Did you intend it? A. Well then, I say, Yes—it is a very serious robbery—a reward of £500 has been offered—it is not on conviction—I have heard that £500 reward has been offered from hundreds of people, oo conviction of the offenders and restoration of the property.
Cross-examined by MR. SEEGEANT BOMPAS. Q. What time on the first of April did you search the house in Mansel-street? A. It was in the afternoon part, about two or three o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. When did you first see Alice Abrahams in the course of this transaction? A. I cannot tell the day of: the month, but it was about a week before Moss surrendered—it was not in consequence of any communication she made to me that I was able to trace out the matter—the communication was made to me by another person—she did not make a communication to me—I have no doubt the. communication I received was obtained from her—I saw her in company with a person, but she never gave me any information—the communication made to me was before I saw Mrs. Abrahams at all—there was no communication, that I am aware of made tome in her presence—I believe she was sworn at the police-office.
COURT. Q. Did she make any communication to you.? A. No, she asked me relative to Moss, whether Moss could be made an evidence of.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you not go with her yourself to Mr. De Mole? A. I did—it was not for the purpose of hearing what disclosure she would make—she was at Lambeth-street office the day previous, and she was to go to prison or be under my charge—I was at her house all night, and felt anxious of being rid of being there, and I called on Mr. Clarkson
and Mr. Bodkin to ask if she might be let out on her own recognizance or bail—they did not like to interfere, and I went to Mr. De Mole—Mr. De Mole and Lea were two hours in a private room with her, but I was not present.
MR. BODKIN. Q. How long have you been an officer of the City? A. About fourteen years—I was called upon to investigate about the robbery, and under those circumstances I thought it right to put questions to discover it.
JAMES HARTLEY . I live in John-street, Crutched Friars. I have more than one partner—I am proprietor of the Iron-gate wharf—the steam vessel called the City of Limerick belongs to us—it goes from Dublin to London, calling at Falmouth—Messrs. Carne are our agents and correspondents at Falmouth—I was in Dublin on the 25th March—I arrived in London on the 28th—Lewin Caspar was in my service as confidential clerk, and in my absence he was to superintend and manage my business—this letter (No. 6) I produce from among my own papers—I see the words "Mr. Bristow, Please attend to this, L. CASPAR "—that is Lewin Caspar's handwriting—( read)—"Messrs. James Hartley and Co., 16, John-street, Crutched Friars. Falmoutn, Oct. 8, 1838. Gentlemen, We are receipt of your note of the 6th instant, and in reply we beg to say the turtle is for Mr. Haggard, at the Bullion Office, Bank of England. The Duke of Cambridge arrived at noon; all well. We ate, gentlemen, your obedient servants, W. and E. C. CARNE. Pet George Hugoe. P.S. We wrote you last night, per box, informing you we received the chest tea, which shall be returned. "Mr. Bristow, Please attend to this, L. CASPAR."
HENRY MOSS re-examined. Q. Is that the letter about the turtle, which Lewin Caspar gave you to imitate? A. Yes.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Have you ever seen that letter before? look at it carefully. A. I never saw this letter, not with this writing at the bottom—there is no doubt I have seen this letter before—if this is not the letter, it is precisely like the letter which was lent to me—to the best of my belief this is the very letter that was left with me more than three months before this transaction—this is the letter, I do believe, that I copied the handwriting from.
COURT. Q. You say so positively that is the letter you copied the handwriting from? A. To the best of my belief that is the handwriting, and that was the contents of it.
MR. JONES. Q. What made you say just now, not with that writing at the bottom of it? A. I do not recollect the writing at the bottom of it—I do not recollect to nave noticed it—it may have been there—I have no recollection of saying just now that I had never teen the letter before with that writing at the bottom—I will not swear I did not say so, but what I mean is, I have no recollection of having seen this writing at the bottom—it may have been there, but I have no recollection of having noticed it—I have looked at the back of the letter—I said I had seen it before, after looking at the direction and looking at it all over—there is nothing particular at the back which enables me to identify it, only the direction, because I was to imitate this sort of direction—I see a figure 6 on the back—on my oath it was not that which enabled me to say it was the letter—I do not remember seeing it—I will swear I did not notice the No. 6 at the back—I may have seen it, but I did not notice it—I will not swear that I did see it—I looked all over the letter, but I was looking at the direction—I cannot swear that I did or did not see the figure 6
—I have great reason to doubt that I did, because I did not know where it was till I looked for it when you spoke.
Q. While you were going out of Court, did you not hear somebody say, it is marked No. 6? A. I did not—nothing of the kind—I never copied the words of this letter—it was the handwriting I was to imitate—I was to look at the style of handwriting—I never made a copy of the letter—it was given me to imitate the handwriting—I was to copy the handwriting, not the words—I never copied the words—I took it frequently to copy the style of writing—I wrote two forms, which I saw yesterday—I returned it on 24th March last, when Lewin Caspar was at my house, and he took it with him—I do not think any person ever saw that letter in my possession.
MR. HARTLEY. re-examined. I was in Dublin on the 21st—I had occasion to write to Lewin Caspar, finding fault with him.
HENRY MOSS re-examined (looking at No. 7.) Lewin Caspar showed me a letter to this effect precisely, one Sunday that he was at my house—I cannot swear that I ever saw this letter before, but I saw one exactly like it—I only saw the top part of it—I know nothing more about it, only that he showed to me a letter very similar to this, if this is not it—I read the top part of it—( looking over it)—this was the contents of the letter he showed me—I believe it is the same he showed me, but I cannot positively take on myself to swear it—he told me at the time he showed it to me that he had received a letter from a person in Dublin, who complained of his not having acknowledged the receipt of 300 l.—I do not exactly remember the remark he made—he merely said he wrote a very angry answer, but he did not tell me the nature of it.
MR. HARTLEY re-examined. While I was in Dublin I wrote a letter on the 21st to Lewin Caspar—that letter would reach London in due course of post on the 23rd—this letter (No. 5) is Lewin Caspar's handwriting.—( read.)
"London, 23rd March, 1839.—Messrs. W. and E. C. Carne.—Dear Sirs, A most diabolical robbery has been committed here through the means of forging your signatures to two letters; on the other side I send a copy of them, and I beg that you will use your utmost exertion towards discovering the perpetrator. The impression is that it must have been done by persons who have had access to your office, or knew of all the particulars as early as yourselves. The letters were posted at Falmouth, 23rd, and arrived with this morning's manifest. Shortly after a gentleman called requesting the delivery of two boxes, marked B C 18 and 19; he showed me a letter, purported to be Mr. Marsh, or from him. I had not the slightest suspicion, and referred him to the wharf, writing an order for their delivery. Unfortunately they were handed to him, and it only remains to use every exertion to obtain a clue. Let me beg and entreat of you to show your usual zeal in this matter. Believe me, dear sirs, I am almost heart-broken. Yours, very truly, L. CASPAR."
"The man who called at my office appeared about 50 to 60 years of age, grey whiskers, middling stature, very dark complexion, swarthy or very sunburnt, (as an old traveller would appear), has a country dialect, as if from your parts. I think he was lame, as he walked very slow, or perhaps limped."
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. You are one of the proprietors of the Limerick Steamer, are you not? A. Yes—there are several other owners—we did not insure this gold dust—I am one of the prosecutors
of this indictment—myself and partners in the wharf have paid the amount of the loss—the steam-company have paid no part of it—I have two partners with me in the wharf—it is we who have offered the reward.
Q. When had you first any communication about Solomon? A. The first communication we had about Solomon was from Messrs. Bult—it was some time at the latter end of April or the beginning of May, I am not sure which—it was before he was examined as a witness—I remember his being examined at the police-office—he was first summoned to the police-office to give an account of the property he had sold to Messrs. Bult—he was examined as a witness and sworn—that was very early in April, I do not remember the day—I believe it was on the 6th, it was on a Saturday I know—I think Solomon's house was searched about a fortnight or three weeks after the examination—it was not searched more than once to my knowledge—I was not there when it was searched at any time—Solomon went about his business after he was first examined—he was taken up, I should say, nearly three weeks after—it was after that that the first negotiation took place about his being admitted as a witness—I never spoke to Solomon in my life—I never spoke to my attorney about his being admitted as a witness—I was not aware that any proposition was made by Solomon—I was not aware that any offer was made of his being made Queen's evidence—I only knew it about three weeks or a month ago, I think about a month ago—I knew it before he was examined the second time as a witness—a few days before—now I recollect it was at the commencement of May, the first three or four, or five or six days of May—I learnt it from Lea the officer.
Q. Was it then agreed on with your consent that he should be admitted? A. I gave no consent about it, I left the matter to the discretion of the magistrates—I gave no assent or dissent about it—I left it to the magistrates and my legal advisers—I heard that he was to deliver up a large portion of the property which he had got possession of—I mean the five bare of gold which he had—they were worth about 2400 l., we understood at the time it was near 3000 l.—I believe his house had been searched before that by the officers—I do not know that it was arranged that it should be found in any given part of the house, nothing of the kind was said to me—I know nothing at all about it—I did not even know in what part of London it was to be delivered up, nor any thing about it—at the time Lea communicated to me about Solomon being admitted as Queen's evidence, he did not say any thing about his house being searched again—I am sure of that—I do not recollect it—Solomon was taken into custody some time in April, he was examined as a witness the second time—I do not recollect that he was sworn more than once—he was examined more than once or twice—he was first summoned and sworn when the first conversation took place about Messrs. Bult—he was afterwards in custody and examined as a prisoner, and afterwards he was examined as a witness—to the best of my recollection, that was at the latter end of May, but I am not certain about dates—it was some time after the conversation between myself and Lea—I do not recollect that any one was present when Lea mentioned it to me.
COURT. Q. Lea was the first person that mentioned to you that Solomon meant to give up part of the property? A. Yes.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Where was it Lea mentioned this to you? A. I really cannot charge my memory with it, we have been so much together
from the time of the robbery, that I really do not know where it was—it might probably be at my own office in John-street, or it might be at Mr. De Mole's—it made no impression upon my mind—I expected to get the money which Solomon proposed to give up—I do not recollect where it was Lea told me—I never saw Mr. Abrahams, Solomon's attorney, except at the police-office—I never spoke to him on the subject—my attorney communicated with him—that was after my conversation with Lea—we had several conversations about the gold being given up—not about Solomon's evidence—I authorised Mr. De Mole to communicate with Solomon's solicitor—I did not authorise him to make the best terms he could with Solomon—I authorised him to make no terms whatever—I authorised him to hear what Mr. Abrahams had to say, but to make Do stipulation whatever about any portion of the property—I did not know of the officers going to search a second time—I have not got the gold dust into my possession yet, nor has my attorney—the officer got possession of it about the first week in May—Lea brought it to the police office—that was not almost immediately after the conversation with Lea—there was considerable time before the property was given up—it was the first week in May I saw it at the police-office for the first time—J did not at all personally interfere, I left it to my professional adviser to do what he thought best—I was under their advice.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. In the course of all these transactions, did you see any thing of Alice Abrahams, the prisoner? A. I saw her once, for a short time, at the New Inn opposite, the day Moss surrendered himself—I saw her several times afterwards at the police-office—I saw her once or twice at my attorney's office—that was before the surrender of Moss—I never heard her give any information—I do not know what she was there about—she came with Robinson and Moss—she was not examined by Mr. De Mole, Mr. Humphreys, or any one else to my knowledge—she made no communication then, to my knowledge—I knew she had brought Moss to the New Inn—Moss never was at Mr. De Mole's office, it was at the New Inn that she was in company with him—Moss, Robinson, Mrs. Abrahams, Mr. De Mole, myself, and the two officers were there—that was about the middle of April—nothing was then said about the share Solomon had in this transaction—yes, there was, I beg pardon, when Moss gave his statement he stated that the property was sold to Mr. Solomon—Alice Abrahams was not present then—she was upstairs—Moss then communicated about the property being sold to Solomon, and the whole transaction of his having taken it away from the wharf, and the whole circumstances connected with the robbery—at that time Solomon was not in custody—he was taken very soon after—Solomon was a considerable time in custody before any proposition came from him to turn Queen's evidence—I do not know How long—I forget dates very much.
Q. Pray have you indicted Mr. Moss for this felony—have you not been before the Grand Jury on a second bill? A. I have not been before the Grand Jury at all—I do not know that I have preferred an indictment against Moss—I did not know there were two indictments—Mrs. Abrahams was at liberty when we were at the New Inn, and Moss also—he surrendered himself that day at the New Inn—what then passed implicated Solomon very strongly in the transaction—it was from information which we got from Mrs. Abrahams that we took up Solomon.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe Moss made several statements? A. He did, affecting in each to tell the truth—upon the faith
of those statements we were about to make him a witness for the Crown—it was considered then, that having made different statements, he could not be fully depended on—the principal part of the statement he made to us at the New Inn was false.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. That was when Mrs. Abrahams was there? A. Yes.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was it not proposed on the part of Mr. Solomons's solicitor, or himself, that he should refund such of the property as he had in his possession, and be admitted for the Crown? A. Yes—this was stated openly and publicly before the Magistrate in the police-office, and his consent requested before it was done—the two Magistrates, on the representation of the counsel for the prosecution, gave their full consent to it—at that time Moss was a prisoner, and had not been examined as a witness—it was stated to the Magistrate that the best test that Mr. Solomon was about to tell the truth, was his delivering up the property, so that they could depend upon what he said—it was also stated by the counsel for the prosecution that it was utterly impossible they could make out any case without making some of the prisoners witnesses.
Q. And was it not under a full conviction on all your parts that Moss was not to be depended on, having given such contradictory statements, that Solomon was admitted as a witness? A. Yes, but nothing whatever was done until every thing had been stated to the Magistrates, and their consent asked and given, in the presence of the prisoners—the value of the property given up by Solomon is about 2400 l.—I am quite satisfied we could not have gone to trial on the evidence of Moss, unless we had strong corroborative testimony—I think it ought to be strong.
Q. How long has Lewin Caspar been a clerk in your house? A. I only became connected with the establishment in October, 1837—I found him. in the office then—very large quantities of money have passed through his hands in the course of business, frequently from 3000 l. to 4000 l. a month,—I had the highest opinion of his honesty till this transaction—large quantities of property, as well as money, have passed through his hands, bullion and specie.
Q. You have not been able to find Allen? A. We have not—I have not been on the look-out for him, nor directed any search for him—I have had no occasion to do so—I do not think it worth while—he absconded without giving me any notice, five or six weeks after the robbery—this transaction had been put in the train of legal investigation long before he absconded—Solomon had been taken up about three weeks before, to the best of my recollection—Allen absconded about a month before Solomon was first summoned to give an account of the property—it was, of course, talked of in the office that Solomon was taken up—on one occasion Lewin, Caspar had specie to the amount of from 130, 000 l. to 150, 000 l. in his care—that was on the 15th of December last—it was a consignment from Falmouth—I was in London then, and he went with it to the Bank—Allen was our collecting clerk—he had access to the different offices—we trusted him, or we should not have kept him—I was apprised of this robbery on the 27th, while in Dublin—I came to town on Thursday, and Lewin Caspar was taken up on the following Tuesday—I have no doubt it was publicly talked of in the office before I came, as information was given the very day it happened—I found Lewin Caspar in his situation when I returned from Dublin, and he remained there till he was taken—he went to his own home
as usual—he was allowed to spend the Sunday as he liked—he could have gone off if he pleased—Good Friday also intervened—he had that day himself also—he might have absconded if he liked.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was Lewin Caspar a clever clerk, and did he understand mercantile business well? A. Particularly well—I have seen the two forged letters—they are such as would decidedly have excited my suspicion—I have neither directly or indirectly received money or money worth on this transaction, except the five bars of gold restored by Solomon.
Q. You have been asked whether you found Lewin Caspar at his post when you returned from Dublin—did he ever, before or after inquiries had commenced on this subject, suggest or hint to you that there was a person in the world of the name of Moss? A. Never—the same course was pursued with respect to Moss becoming a witness as was pursued with regard to Solomon—it was the proposition of his attorney, adopted by the prosecution, and acquiesced in by the Magistrates—it was at the latter end of the examination of one day that Mrs. Abrahams was examined—she was examined from about ten to fifteen minutes, to the best of my recollectioin and she was then handed over to the care of the officer, upon her begging she might not be put into custody.
( Adjourned.)
Wednesday, 26th June, 1839.
JONAS COOTER . I am one of the letter-carriers of the General Post Office. Mr. Hartley's office in John-street is in my district—I have my book in which I keep an account of the letters delivered to all those persons who have an account with me—Messrs. Hartley had an account with me—I used to be paid every Saturday—( turning to his book)—the amount of Messrs. Hartley's postage for the week in which the 25th of March was included, was 1 l. 5 s. 2 d.—the amount on the 25th of March was 7 s. 10 d. only—only one unpaid letter came by the General Post from Falmouth to Messrs. Hartley, that day—I do not take an account of paid letters.
WILLIAM DAVIS . I am one of the inland messengers of the General Post Office in London—all unpaid letters have a circular stamp placed on them, which is kept at the Post Office—the whole of the bags are divided into eight divisions, called tables, and each division is stamped by a particular letter—there are four stamps for each division—I have them here.
Q. Look at the London post-mark, with which this letter (No. 3) appears to be stamped? A. This is not the London post-mark—it is a forgery—the post-mark to this letter (No. 2) is genuine—this letter (No. 3) could not have come through the General Post Office—the post-mark to this letter (No. 4) is also a forgery—that did not come through the Post Office.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. You say these are not genuine stamps, How do they differ? A. This (No. 3) differs particularly in the letter E—it is smaller—that letter denotes a division—the E division of the table—a man named Hatton affixes the mark—we have his name in the book—it is one man alone who has the duty for probably a year—( producing the stamp)—this is the true mark, with the exception of the dates, which are moveable—this stamp has been used since 1st January 1838—the letter is a fixture, but the figures shift daily—my attention was first
called to the stamps at the examination at Lambeth-street—this stamp was used last year—the previous stamps are always broken up—I am confident the stamp used before this was broken up—the person who makes the stamps takes the old ones—we have nothing of the kind left in the office.
THOMAS HARLAND . On the 25th of March, I was on the stand in Corn-hill, with my cab —I remember about twelve o'clock, Moss got into the tab—( Moss was here produced to the witness)—that is the man—he desired me to go to the Dublin Steam Wharf, Irongate—he told me to drive fast—I went there—Moss sat in the cab, and showed a piece of paper to one of the porters (Kingston)—the porter introduced him to the clerk, I believe—he fetched the clerk to him—I have since seen the clerk who was fetched to him—he asked Moss to step in, which he did—I pulled the cab over to the other side of the street, and waited—he then came out, and one of the porters with him, carrying two boxes—I do not recollect whether the clerk came out again—the boxes were about that length and height—they were deal boxes—I noticed red sealing-wax on them, and I think a pink tape, but I am not quite certain—I think Moss had a blue bag with him when he went to the wharf, but I am not certain—the boxes were put into the bag, and Moss got in—I gave one of the porters a four-penny piece by Moss's direction, and Moss gave one of the porters a shilling—he told me to go to the Cross Keys, Wood-street, Cheap side, and to drive as hard as I could—when I got there the boxes were taken in, and Moss got out of the cab —a lad, I think, took the boxes out—I believe both the boxes were taken to the Cross Keys office—he kept me waiting a little while, while he went with a lad to purchase a blue bag, but he could not get one, he returned and discharged me.
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. By what means do you know the man who came with you in the cab that day? A. I know him by sight—we had a little bit of a dispute about the fare—I know him as I should know any one else—I remarked at the time that he was dressed in black—he had a broad-brimmed hat, and rather largish whiskers—not such as he has to-day, they were larger than he has to-day rather—they were black.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Look at Moss, have you any doubt of that being the man? A. Yes, that is the man.
JOHN MACBETH . On the 25th of March I was looking out for a fare in Cheapside with my cab, and was called to the Cross Keys in Wood-street, by a man—( looking at Moss)—that is the person—it was about twelve o'clock, as near as I can guess—I pulled in at the corner of Wood-street, within a few yards of the Cross Keys—Moss got into my cab —he brought one box in a blue bag out of the Cross Keys, and a boy brought another box which was not covered—it seemed like foreign deal wood—he desired me to drive to the London Hospital, and asked whether I knew it—I drove there, and was going into the hospital—he stopped me—I stopped at the corner of the street going down by the side of the hospital—I do not know exactly the name of the street—he took the box out which was in the blue bag, and went down the street by the side of the hospital on the right hand side—the street on the right hand as you stand facing the hospital—I remained at the corner—he was gone about three quarters of an hour—when he returned, he brought the bag back empty—he then got into the cab, and desired me to drive to the iron bridge, down the Commercial-road—when I got there he stopped me—he then had the other
box out, and put it in the bag on the pavement, after he took it out of the cab —I held the bag.
Q. As you held the bag, could you form any opinion whether the box was heavy? A. Yes—very heavy and very clumsy—in getting it into the bag it burst the bag—I noticed some marks, and pink tape, and red sealing-wax on the box—the marks on it were numbers, and two letters in ink—he paid me and I was discharged—he went down towards Blackwall way—omnibuses frequently run backwards and forwards in that road—he took the box under his arm.
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. Did you see both the boxes? A. I did not see the colour of the one in the bag—we did not meet any one when we got to the London Hospital—I did not see either of the prisoners there.
EDWARD HENRY . I live in Bread-street, Cheapside, and am in partnership with my brother. I know the witness Moss perfectly well as being in the employment of Mr. Hyams—I have dealings with Mr. Hyams—on the 25th of March, he came to our house—I do not exactly remember the time, but it was in the morning—he came to bring some watches for his employer, and asked for some money for Mrs. Hyams, as Mr. Hyams was out of town—I paid him this £50 draft—( produced)—this is his handwriting on the back—he wrote that in my presence—looking at the date of the cheque I recollect it was on the 25th of March—I gave it him on account of Mr. Hyams, for Mrs. Hyams.
JANE BRADLEY . On the 25th of March, I was living in the service of Mr. and Mrs. Moss—since I was before the Magistrate I have been under the care of Lea, the officer—I had lived with them two years, but used to sleep at home with my friends. On the 25th of March, he lived at No. 12, New-street, which is by the side of the London Hospital—Mr. Moss generally went out to work of a morning in a kind of long rough working dress—he used to go out between eight and nine o'clock, or about nine o'clock—on the morning of the 25th of March he went out about half-past eight o'clock—he had not his working dress on then, but a better suit of clothes—he returned between one and two o'clock—he usually gave a double knock, but when he returned this day, only one knock came to the door—my mistress opened the door.
Q. When you were before the Magistrate do you remember what you said on that subject—did you say your mistress opened the door? A. Yes, there was a mistake in my deposition when it was read over, and I named it to Mr. Humphreys—it said I had opened the door, but I did not open the door—I went to Mr. Humphreys' office and told him of it—my mistress opened the door—I heard my master's voice while I was in the kitchen—Moss did not stay but a very few minutes—he did not stay to dinner—I made some observations about it at the time—after my master had been gone some time I went out—my master returned about eight o'clock in the evening—he washed himself, and then went to the Earl St. Vincent public-house—about half-an-hour after he was gone, another knock came at the door—it was the elder Mr. Caspar—I let him in—he asked me where Mr. Moss was—I said he was round at the Earl St. Vincent, and I would go for him, I should not be gone a minute—I fetched him home—I staid till half-past eleven o'clock, and left master and the elder Caspar there—Mr. Caspar had been there all that time—I used to come to work in the morning about half-past six o'clock—I came on the Tuesday morning
about half-past six o'clock—it was my duty to light the fires—I did not light any fire that morning, because it looked as if it had been burning a long while, and they were all white ashes—the fire was then alight—that was in the back parlour—Mr. Caspar was gone then—I did not see him—I examined the ashes—I know wood ashes—it appeared as if wood ashes had been burning there, and one of the hobs was over red sealing wax—there were some nails among the ashes underneath the grate, and some little lumps that looked to me like glass—I do not know what it was—my master was in bed when I went that morning—my mistress went out directly I came in, she had a bundle in one hand and a basket in the other—when I went away the night before, there was a brass-plate on the door with my master's name on it, but when I went next morning it was taken off—I know master's sister, Mrs. Levy—my master did not go out on Tuesday till late in the afternoon—he was walking about the place—Mrs. Levy came that Tuesday morning, and in the afternoon a truck came—when my master went out in the afternoon, he did not return any more to New-street—a truck of things went away that day to Mansel-street, and the rest of the things next day—only master moved on the Tuesday—my mistress returned about one o'clock on Tuesday afternoon—she went out again directly she had had her dinner, and returned about five o'clock—on Wednesday morning a van came and took the rest of the things away—I remained there all Tuesday night, helping them to pack up—my master has a daughter named Rosetta—she kept her clothes in a deal box—she told me something about that box, when I came there on the Tuesday morning, and in consequence of what she said I looked at the box, and her clothes were in a drawer and not in the box—that is the box they used to be kept in—( looking at one produced)—that box was underneath the bed in her father's room on Tuesday morning—I looked at it—it was tied up with a cord—from what the girl said to me I tried to lift it, but could not, it was very heavy—that box was taken away in the truck by the carman—I know Mr. Reed—he is the landlord of the house in New-street—they told me about some things going to be pledged, and Mrs. Moss gave me five sovereigns, which I gave to Mr. Reed, the landlord, by her direction—I do not know what rent my master paid a year—I think the five sovereigns was for the quarter's rent—I went to the lodgings in Mansel-street on the Wednesday—I saw Moss there on Wednesday evening—he was at home all day on Wednesday in Mansel-street, and he went out in the evening—I was at Mansel-street all day—I know Isaac Davis—Mrs. Moss and Mrs. Levy assisted me in packing up the goods on the Tuesday night, and on the Wednesday morning Davis assisted—I went to the lodging in Mansel-street about seven o'clock on Thursday morning, and saw Moss there—I saw old Caspar there that morning—I opened the door to him about a quarter to eight o'clock—he asked for my master, and master went and spoke to him just in the passage—after speaking together old Caspar ran down stairs, and master went out in a very few minutes after—he did not come home to breakfast that morning—I was kept in-doors, and not permitted to leave the house from that Thursday morning until the Saturday evening—I slept there—that was not usual with me—on the Sunday before the robbery was committed, old Mr. Caspar came to the house in New-street about dinner-time, and staid about half an hour—I had frequently seen him at my master's house before that—on the Saturday after the robbery he came to Mansel-street about dinnertime, one o'clock, and on that occasion my mistress sent me out of the room
—master was not at home—master had two rooms and a very small dressing-room at Mansel-street—the girl's box that has been produced was put into that little room—it is like a cupboard—my master slept in the room next to the cupboard on the Tuesday night—Mrs. Levy lives in Petticoat-lane—Davis has a sister, she lives next door to Mrs. Levy.
Cross-examined by MR. BOMPAS. Q. You have been under the care of Lea, the officer? A. Yes—I have not been living at his house—I hare teen living at the Flying Horse, in Lambeth-street—it is a public-house close to the police-office—I was under Lea's care—I do not know whether he provided my food for me—I suppose he did—my deposition was read over to me—I did not say at first that I went and let my master in—I could not, because I was in the kitchen with my mistress—I did not say so—it was a mistake.
Q. It was taken down that you said you heard your master's voice say, "Where is the girl?" and you knew it was your master's voice, and you went and let him in? A. No, I never let him in—it was perhaps taken down so, but I did not say so—it was read over to me as if I opened the door, but I did not—I said I knew it was my master's voice, but I did not open the door—I did not let him in—it was taken down that I went and let him in.
Q. When was that read over to you? A. About a week ago, I think—it was read over by Mr. Humphreys, at his office—there were some other gentlemen there—it was not read over to me more than once—I was sworn to it a great while ago, but they must have made that mistake at Lambeth-street, because I never let him in—I swore to it when I was first taken to Lambeth-street by the officer—I was not taken up as a prisoner; I was taken before the Magistrate as a witness—that was about two months ago—it was read over to me then, and I swore to it, but the Clerk read so fast I did not notice that when he read it—it was first taken down from my mouth, and read over to me about a fortnight after—I put my cross to it, and swore to it—it was read over to me again by Mr. Humphreys, about a week ago—Lea was present—I wanted to correct it then, as I did not open the door—I have been under Lea's care about three months, from a week after the robbery was done.
Q. You say the deal box was under the bed; did you pull it out? A. I just knelt down and lifted it, I did not pull it out—I cannot say whether it was far under the bed—it was afterwards put into the small room—Moss went to Mansel-street on Tuesday—he went away on the Thursday after he got to Mansel-street, soon after Mr. Caspar, and did not return till Monday—I did not go into the little room till the Thursday afternoon, the first Thursday I was there—I saw the box then—I was at Mansel-street on the Tuesday morning in the next week—I was not there on the Wednesday—I first saw Mrs. Levy there on the Wednesday morning—she came with me—I saw her there on the Tuesday evening, when the truck went—I saw her go away once on Wednesday, and about three times on Thursday.
Q. Did she carry any thing away from the box? A. I do not know whether it was from the box, but it was out of that room—she went out, and was out about an hour, and then came back again—I saw the box again on Thursday afternoon, and it remained there—it was there on the Tuesday in the next week—I went into the room, and saw it there, on that Tuesday morning—I really do not know whether any body was with
me—the box remained there all along, and I left it there—I was cleaning the room on the Thursday, and I looked into it, and there was nothing in it then but one dirty muslin frock—the box remained there then—I was not there after Tuesday morning.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. I believe, when I first saw you at Lambeth-street, Mrs. Moss and Mrs. Levy were in custody. A. Yes—I do not recollect whether Mr. Moss was in custody or not then—Mrs. Moss and Mrs. Levy were not in custody the first time I was there—I had been examined two or three times then—I cannot say How long the last examination was after the first—it might have been a fortnight—I think my deposition was read over to me at the last examination—I do not know when it was taken down—Lea did not tell me there was a mistake—he never named it.
Q. How came you to go to Mr. Humphreys. A. Why, it was so long, and I was afraid I should forget it—the time being so long, I wished to have it read over again—it was only read over to me once at the office—I dare say that was a month before I went to Mr. Humphreys—I was afraid something might escape my memory—I really do not know whether Mr. Moss was in custody when I was examined—I saw him in the dock once—I saw him at Lambeth-street—I did not see him anywhere else—I have seen him, since I was examined, at the Flying Horse—I really do not know whether Lea was with him at the time or not—I do not know whether it was once or twice I saw Mr. Moss at the Flying Horse—I do not know whether Lea was in his company on any of those occasions—I think he was by—I cannot say he was there, hut I do not know whether he was in the room with Lea—the Flying Horse is the public-house close to the police-office, where the policemen go.
Q. Where are you when you are there, in a private room, or with the guests? A. Very generally with the two nieces, helping them to do about the place—I am in the kitchen when I am not with the nieces about the house—I am never in the tap-room—I see Lea and Roe sometimes go into the parlour—I am never talking to them, nor hearing what they say.
Q. I suppose, from the time you first went there, you have had no conversation with Lea or Roe about your evidence. A. Lea came in one morning, and I said, "Mr. Lea, I am so frightened I shan't keep it in my memory," and asked him if he would take me to have it read over—I was not at the office when my master gave his evidence—I have never been in the office only when I had to give my evidence—my evidence was given as against my mistress and Mrs. Levy.
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. You have spoken to Mr. Moss once or twice, you think, at the Flying Horse? A. Yes; he asked me How I was—I am certain it was after I was examined that I saw him—Mr. Caspar was frequently in the habit of coming to my master's house—on Sunday, the 24th of March, I am quite sure I saw him there.
GEORGE SHEEN . I am a carman. On the 26th of March I was employed by Mrs. Levy to remove some things from New-street—I moved the things from No. 10, I think—it was Mr. Moss's house—I took them to Mansel-street—I took some things in a truck—I did not remove any boxes, to my knowledge—I am in the habit of moving so many people's things—( looking at the box produced)—that is the box, to the best of my
knowledge, that I moved—I believe I moved that—I had some one to help me, and that person helped me to lift that box into the truck—Mrs. Levy told me to bring a man with me—the man helped me to lift the box, because she told me to be careful of it—it was rather heavy.
GEORGE REED . I am a tailor, and live in Northumberland-place. The house, No. 12, New-street, where Moss lived, belongs to me—New-street goes down by the side of the London Hospital—on the 25th of March, Moss owed me two quarters' rent—he left on the 26th—he had given no notice whatever—Bradley, the servant-maid, brought me round 5 l. at ten o'clock at night—it was unusual for him to send the servant with it—he generally paid it himself—the rent was 20 l. a year—there was 10 l. due.
JOSEPH JEPSON . I live at No. 29, Mansel-street. Moss took a lodging at my house on the 26th of March—they moved in some things the same day—a truck came, and next day they moved in their furniture.
LUCY MARSHALL . I am a widow, and live at No. 13, New-street, Whitechapel-road, opposite the house Moss lived in. I knew Moss very well—on Monday, the 25th of March, I saw him go to his house about three o'clock in the afternoon—he had something very weighty with him, I cannot tell what—he came in a direction from Stepney way—that would not be from Whitechapel-road, but from Commercial-road—he could scarcely get what he had, up the steps—he went into the house with it—next morning I missed the plate off his door—about five o'clock that Tuesday evening, two men went with a truck, and took away some things—there were three or four boxes, a bed and bedstead, and a few brooms—one of the boxes was corded, and appeared very heavy—it was the shape of that box—( the one produced.)
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You Say it looked heavy—I suppose he staggered under the weight of it? A. It was as much as two men could put into the truck, it was so heavy—it was very weighty—I have no judgment what weight it would be—I should think it was full two cwt.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. But you have no judgment what weight it would be? A. No.
HARRIET SMART . I am the wife of George Smart, a tailor, living in New-street. I know the house where Moss lived—I saw some moving from there on Tuesday, the 26th of March—I know Ellis Caspar—I saw him on the Sunday before this moving, at Moss's door, and I saw him on the Monday—I saw the younger Caspar there one Sunday, to the best of my recollection, but I could not swear to him.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS." Q. Did you ever see that before? A. No—I did not lodge in the same house with Moss, but at No. 16, opposite—I had never seen the person I say was Caspar, before that Sunday—I only had a view of him while he stood at the door to be let in—that might be about a minute or two minutes—I cannot say what time of the day it was—it was towards the evening, getting dusk—it is not a wide street—it is as wide as the length of this court—I cannot speak positively to the width—I was at my first floor window—it was in the dusk of the evening I saw him the second time, and from my first floor window—he had his hat on—I cannot say positively whether he was going away when I saw him, or whether he was going in—I had a view of his face—I saw him conversing at the door—the door was open.
Q. Who was it first asked you about this? A. Roe, the officer—he came to me—he did not give me a description of any person—he came to me some time after the robbery—to the best of my belief, it was the week following—I had not a long conversation with him—I should think it was five minutes—I afterwards went to Lambeth-street, and saw both the Caspars at the bar—it was not the same day I was examined that Roe came to me—he came to me several days before—I had objected to go to Lambeth-street, but I had a summons—I cannot say who took me into the office—Roe was there at the time—I know Lea—I did not see him go and speak to Caspar, before my attention was called to him—Lea did not talk to me in the office.
CHARLES CALLAWAY . In March last, I was in the service of Fiestell, of 68, Great Tower-street—I know Moss—I have seen him at my master's coffee-house, three or four times—I think it was in March last—sometimes alone and sometimes in company—I have seen the young man, Lewin Caspar, in his company—I have seen him there two or three times with Moss—I do not exactly know the times—I have seen Lewin Caspar there very frequently by himself—I found a red handkerchief in the coffee-room at one time—( looking at one)—it was something similar to that, but I cannot say whether that is it—I gave it up to Mrs. Fiestell, who is now confined in child-bed.
Q. Do you remember whether Moss had been at the house on the day it was found? A. I think I could almost swear that he and Lewin Caspar came in together that day, and staid late at night, but I should not like to swear that Moss was one of them—I am satisfied that Lewin Caspar was there—I afterwards saw the handkerchief in my master's possession—be had it in his pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Where is this coffee-house? A. The second door from Mark-lane—it is a coffee-house and eating-house—Fiestell has kept it four or five months—nobody attends the customers but myself—Mrs. Fiestell occasionally attended when I was engaged down below, but very seldom—that did not happen once in a week—Mr. and Mrs. Fiestell live there—my master was out of the way at that time—there is no name up at the shop—Mr. Fiestell used to come home late at night, and go out early in the morning, between five and six o'clock, sometimes before—I had seen the person I call Moss two or three times before alone—I almost think I saw him twice with the elderly gentleman, now I come to recollect, they came to see Mr. Fiestell—I do not know whether Mr. Fiestell is here—I have seen him to-day, and I saw him yesterday—Moss came to see Mr. Fiestell—they saw him up in the coffee-room—that was before he was out of the way—I should think it was in November, but I cannot exactly say—there was a case he was on at Guildhall about a bill of exchange, I think about the 24th or 25th of November—that was when he was out of the way—he returned on the Saturday night—he was away two or three days, and came back again—he kept out to avoid being arrested, but I do not think he ever stopped away above three days at a time—he came home on Saturday night once or twice—I found the handkerchief between ten and eleven o'clock at night, and gave it to Mrs. Fiestell next morning—I afterwards saw it in Mr. Fiestell's hands, but I did not know it was the same handkerchief—I do not know whether Fiestell has been keeping out of the way up to the present time—I have not been living with him since April—he had not ceased keeping out of the
way when I left—he was very seldom at home, except at night—sometimes he has got in and out—he certainly had some object in keeping out of the way—I know a person named Davis—he used to come to the coffee-shop—I have not seen him since I left there—he used to come to see Mr. Fiestell—I saw him there last in April—Mr. Fiestell keeps the shop now.
Q. Does he keep any thing else? A. Allow me to explain—I am his brother-in-law—Mrs. Fiestell is my own sister, unfortunately—I say unfortunately, because he has brought me into so many scrapes—he has brought me here to-day to lose my day's work—I have lost my situation in consequence of coming here—I lived at Stiles's, a ginger beer shop—I suppose I had seen Moss at the coffee-shop three or four times altogether before I saw him with Lewin Caspar—I never knew him to take coffee but once—I think he came to see Fiestell—he went up stairs—Fiestell was there sometimes—one night I recollect he came late—that was the night of the handkerchief—it was about eleven o'clock—he might be there four or five times—I think he was in Fiestell's company once when he had coffee—I think that was in March.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What Davis did you see him with? A. A wine-merchant on Tower-hill, I believe—he is a red-faced man.
THOMAS HARLAND re-examined, (Looking at the witness Kingston)— That is the porter—( looking at Mr. Bristow) and that is the gentleman that came to Mr. Moss in the cab.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You drove Moss in that cab? A. Yes—I took the same notice of him as I should of any other person—I have seen him to-day—I should say his whiskers are not so large as when I saw him—they appear to have been shaved off.
SARAH LEVY . I am a widow, and sister to Moss, I live at 111, Petticoat-lane. I remember hearing of the robbery which took place on the 25th of March—I was in Mansel-street on Tuesday the 26th of March—I saw Moss there in the evening—I saw him in New-street on the morning of Tuesday, and in Mansel-street in the evening—on the Thursday following he gave me some directions—I was in Mansel-street on the Thursday morning—he gave me some directions—in consequence of those directions I went to his apartment in Mansel-street, to a little, back-room—I found in that room two parcels wrapped in coarse brown paper tied with packthread—I took them to my own lodging by his direction—I did not take them both at once, they were too heavy—I first took one, then the other—I know a person of the name of Davis, he lived in Coventry-court—I saw him on that day about twelve o'clock—I had some conversation with him, and then he came to me again in the evening—I gave him the parcels, and he tied them one at a time in a handkerchief—he took them one at a time, and then came back afterwards and took the other—I was present at Moss's wedding—Old Caspar was there.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Moss is a relation of yours is he not? A. Yes, a brother—he has been a watchmaker—I never lived with him at all—I visited at his house—I did not know Ikey Solomons—I never spoke to him—I am quite sure of that—I have heard of him—I cannot tell whether Moss knew him—I keep no such acquaintance as Ikey Solomons—Davis is no relation of mine, he is of my brother's by marriage—I have known Davis a long time—I do not know How he gets
his living—I swear that—I never went to his house in Coventry-court—I never went to a house of his in my life—he may get his bread by keeping brothels for what I know—I have no belief about it—I know none of his business—I never heard of his keeping a house of that description—he may keep six brothels for aught I know—he may keep brothels for aught I know—I never inquired his business—I do not know that he keeps four brothels—I cannot tell what he keeps—he may keep brothels for what I know—perhaps he may—I believe he does—I have a brother named Barney Moss, he lives in William-street—he has a house one end of the street in Waterloo-road, and one in Cornwall-road—it is a brothel—my sister's husband was named Abrahams—he is dead—his wife is alive, and lives in Gulston-street, Whitechapel—he kept a brothel in my sister's time—she was married to him.
Q. Were you taken up on suspicion of being one of the thieves? A. I was taken up—I do not know whether I was accused as a thief—I was taken up on suspicion, for what I cannot tell—I suppose it was on suspicion of being concerned in this business—I cannot tell How many examinations there were at Lambeth-street—when Moss was made a witness Mr. Adolphus had me and Mrs. Moss let out.
MARIA SEWELL . J live at No. 33, Charlotte-street, Whitechapel. I am aunt to Mrs. Moss, the wife of Henry Moss—I lodged with Moss for seven years—I lived with him in New-street, to the best of my recollection about six years—I know both Lewin and Ellis Caspar—during the time I was in New-street at Moss's I saw them there, not very frequently—I saw old Caspar more frequently than the younger—I have not lived with Moss for the last four years, therefore I have not seen them there—I have not seen them for the last four years.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Are you a married woman? A. A widow—my maiden name was Solomon—I am no relation to Mr. Solomon in the Strand.
Q. Are you any relation to the gentleman who is called Ikey Solomons? A. I do not know who you mean—I have heard of him in the public newspapers—I know him from public report and no more—I have been a widow these fifteen years, no, let me correct myself, about seventeen years—I was married in 1804—I parted from my husband before he died—no one else ever passed for my husband—I never lived with any body else after I separated from my husband—there is no captain whom I know.
Q. How came you to go away from Moss's house? A. Miss Moss was getting a young woman, and the room I occupied was wanted for her—I was not there when Moss's house was searched—I heard from Miss Moss that it had been searched—I never heard it from him—I never saw him after the occurrence till I saw him at Lambeth-street—I was divorced from my husband—I do not know on what account—perhaps many more females have been injured—I know I was divorced—I believe it was for false accusations brought against me, that I was untrue to my husband—I was not caught in the fact—I really do not knew who the party was with whom I was said to be untrue.
Q. Do you mean to say you do not know the name of the party with whom you were accused—( no answer)—do you decline to answer?—was it untrue when you said you did not know the name? A. I did not say so—I did not answer—I do not know the name—I do not know that I said just now I did not know the party if I have said so I did not know that
I said so—he is a man in business, a pencil-maker—I went to live with my brother after I was divorced from my husband—the pencil-maker was the only person with whom there was any charge to my knowledge—the pencil-maker never came to visit me—I knew him as being a friend of my husband's, who dealt with him—it was at Liverpool I was said to be false to my husband—I lived there—I do not recollect any thing about a captain of a ship—the pencil-maker lived in London—this was said to have happened in Liverpool—my husband was away in the country—I get my living by tailoring-work, and a trifling income which my brother allows me—I am related to Davis—Mr. Davis is my sister's husband—it is about fifteen years since I was divorced from my husband.
ADOLPHUS FIESTELL . I keep a coffee-shop in Great Tower-street. I do not know Moss—I know Lewin Caspar—I remember his coming to my coffee-shop in February or March—I cannot say the date—some one was with him—they had some coffee—when they were gone I found a handkerchief—my waiter was not there nor my brother-in-law—it was me—I gave particular instructions, knowing only Mr. Caspar, Jun. when he was a boy, and finding it was the first time he came to my coffee-shop, and being glad to see people who wish to come to my shop, I told the waiter to serve them in a different manner than he serves others—I remember finding the handkerchief—I gave it to my wife, who is now confined—I have it now—it is in my possession—my wife's possession is my possession—I received it afterwards from her and kept it—I afterwards saw Roe, the officer, and gave him the handkerchief in the office, when it was required by Mr. Humphreys.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Do you act as waiter sometimes? A. I do.
WILLIAM FISHWICK . I am a carpenter, and live at No. 3, Meeting-house-lane, Peckham. I have known Alice Abrahams many years—she came to my house last Easter Wednesday, in the forenoon, and wished to know whether I could make it convenient for a person to come and lodge with me, whether I could accommodate them with a bed and lodging—she did not say who it was—she asked me if I could accommodate a person there, a particular friend of hers—I said, "Is it a male or female?"—she said, "It is a male"—I asked her if every thing was honourable—she said I might depend on it, that it was—I told her if my wife was as agreeable as I was I had no objection—I had no lodger at the time—I asked if it was any person I knew, knowing all her family so many years—she said, "No, it is not"—she said his circumstances were rather embarrassed, and he wished to be there two or three days, until his creditors could meet, and compromise the business—my wife was at that time attending a lady in the village in her accouchement—I told her she might see my wife, it was on her road home, and I would take her down there, and she might speak to her—I went with her to my wife, but what passed between them I do not know—it was at the lady's door—I was not exactly near enough to hear, but my wife came over and said, if the person could make it convenient with me till she returned home, she had no objection—Mrs. Abrahams said, "None of my family know it, and I don't wish them to know it"—she went away, and returned again the same evening, in company with two gentlemen—one was Mr. Moss, and the other a gentleman—I do not know his name—( Moss produced)—that was one of the persons—he was left behind, and slept at my house that night—Mrs. Abrahams and the
other person might stop half an hour or three quarters that night—Moss remained at my house until the Wednesday fortnight—it was Wednesday evening, about ten o'clock, that he came to my house, and a fortnight after he left, in the forenoon—during the time he was at my house Alice Abrahams came to see him I should say three times, or it might be four—when she came she was always in company with the same person who came with Most when he first came—I do not know the name of that person, nor who it is—she generally came in the early part of the morning—she used to see Moss in the apartment where he used to sleep, the front-room, one pair—she stopped one time nearly an hour, but generally about half an hour—I did not hear what pasted between them—they went out together, while Most was staying there—she was always accompanied by the tame man—they went out together three different times—when he went away the same persons came for him that brought him.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How long have you been a housekeeper there? A. Six years, and in the parish seventeen years—there has never been any thing against me in my life—I have known Alice Abrahams almost from a child—my opinion of her was such that I readily received a lodger at her recommendation—I never knew any thing of her but an honest, industrious young woman—Moss conducted himself as a gentleman, and I treated him at such—he had only one room—it was my own bed-room, which I gave up to him—that was the only room he had the command of—Mrs. Abrahams always went up stairs to that room with the man who accompanied her—there was always tares of them together in the room—not the slightest thought of any thing improper their occurred to me—when Moss's time was out they all three went away together—they generally went out about ten or eleven o'clock in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Did you tee Mott almost every hour in the day during the fortnight? A. Yes—I did not see him after he left my house till I was at Lambeth-street—I saw him there—I see no difference in him now to when he first came to me, only in hit dress, and that is only in the coat.
GEORGE ROBINSON . In April last I was clerk to Mr. Yardley, an attorney in Christopher-street, Hatton-garden. On Sun day morning, the 14th of April, I was at my own house, which was then in Wharton-street, Lloyd-square—the prisoner, Alice Abrahams, came there with Mow and Davis, the man charged in this indictment—I received these thirteen £10 notes and two £5 notes on that occasion—I have also an I O U.
MR. PRENDERGAST? Q. Were not you professionally concerned for Mrs. Abrahams? A. For her and Moss—Mr. Yardley was acting as their attorney at that time, and had been previously.
COURT. Q. Was the meeting you had together relating to the subject matter of the defence of this prosecution? A. Most assuredly.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you say you were attorney for Most at well? A. I was—when these were given to me I considered I was acting for him as well as Mrs. Abrahams.
JAMES LEA . I am an officer of Lambeth-street office. On the 25th of March the prisoner, Lewin Caspar, came to me at that office, at near three o'clock, to inform me of a robbery which had taken place at Messrs. Hartley's—he showed me these two letters ( Nos. 3 and 4)and described to me the way in which the robbery had been effected—by desire of Mr. Hardwick I went with him to the Dublin steam-wharf—he staid with me there
a short time—he then said he thought he could be of no more assistance to me—I then went with him to the office in John-street—he there produced four or five letters out of a drawer, and asked me my opinion of the writing—he had them in his hand—he said, "Do you not think the writing is very much like these?"—I said, "No, certainly not"—he then asked me what I thought of the signature of Messrs. Carne, and on comparing it with the other letters, I said they appeared very much alike, certainly—he said it was principally on the faith of that signature that he had given the order to the person who brought the letter in the morning—I asked him the description of the person—he said he was about sixty yean of age he thought, and a little taller than himself, dressed in black, and grey hair and whiskers—he said it was a person that came from Mr. March, but whether it was Mr. March or not he did not know—nothing more passed at that time—I went away shortly after—about eight o'clock that night I was at the Swan with Two Necks, making inquiries about this matter, and the two Caspars came there together in a coach—the mail leaves the Swan with Two Necks a few minutes before eight o'clock, I believe—it was gone when they arrived—I spoke to them, and told them they might perhaps overtake it at the railroad station, and I went with them in a coach, but we arrived too late—that was with a view to Lewin Caspar's going down to Falmouth about this business—he stated to me himself, in his father's presence, that he meant to go to Falmouth to look after it—I think his father was not to have gone with him—his father went all the way to the railroad station—we returned in the same coach when we found we were too late—as we returned the elder Caspar left the coach, to the best of my belief and recollection somewhere about Charging-cross—I cannot say exactly, for I did not notice.
Q. Was it said for what purpose he should leave the coach? A. Young Caspar said some bills were to be printed on the following morning about this matter, offering a reward of 500 l.—he said Mr. Allen and Mr. De Mole were to have them printed—I said I should like to see Mr. De Mole and Mr. Allen before they were printed, and upon that Lewin Caspar told his father to go to Mr. De Mole about it—it was for that reason he left the coach—Lewin Caspar only went with me to the steam-wharf that day to take inquiries—in the evening he went with me to St. Paul's coffee-house, to inquire if any person had come by the Falmouth mail—I had been there before in the early part of the day—we went to see if a person had come, answering the description Lewin Caspar gave of the man, sixty years of age, with grey hair and whiskers—we heard of a person who appeared to answer the description—Lewin Caspar said he thought the description given by the proprietor of the coffee-house, resembled the person very much—he described the person as having grey hair, and about the height and age—there were one or two waiters, and the landlord—three persons had arrived that morning from Falmouth, and this description was given of one of them—Lewin Caspar said it appeared to correspond—he said nothing to me about that house at that time; but a day or two after he said he thought I could not do better than stick to the persons at that coffee-house—I saw him on two or three occasions after, and he asked How I was getting on—three or four days after, I succeeded in tracing the cabs to New-street, and was led to a house in which the witness Moss was stated to have resided—I think it was No. 1—it was the first house on the left side—I have never been there with Moss—I saw
the landlord, Mr. Reed—I never saw Jane Bradley, the servant, there—I searched that house, and found a quantity of nails and burnt wood in the fire-place, and some in the dust-hole—( producing them)—I have three separate parcels, one from the dust-heap, one from inside the grate, and one from under the grate—there was scarcely any in the dust-hole, and very few in the grate—there are between forty and fifty nails—after this I was with Mr. Hartley at the office—I saw him almost every day—on one occasion I was with Mr. Hartley in Mansel-street, and saw the two Caspars at the end of Somerset-street, which leads into Mansel-street—one is a continuation of the other—I was afterwards present at the counting-house, where Roe put some questions to Lewin Caspar—I do not remember Mr. Hartley putting any questions to him—Roe asked him if be knew a person named Moss—he said "Yes, three or four"—after some other questions had been put, and answered, he was given into my custody by Mr. Hartley—I afterwards took Mrs. Moss and Mrs. Levy into custody—I have produced this box, which I got out of a little room in the back bed-room in Mansel-street—I produce the gold bars which are now in this box—I received them from a person of the name of John Dean, at Mr. Solomon's house, 53, Strand, on the 7th of May—I received these two moulds from him at the same time ( producing them)and half a dozen Russian copper coins, which I have not got with me—I searched the house of Davis, No. 8, Oxendon-street, and I went with Roe to the house in Coventry-court—he searched it while I remained down stairs—I did not see either of the prisoners at the house in Oxendon-street, when I searched it—I saw Mrs. Abrahams in Coventry-court—she was sitting in the room when I went in—this was on Easter Tuesday, the 2nd of April, when Roe went to search the house—I did not know Davis till I saw him that evening—I did see him—that evening—I saw him at the office during the examination, and I saw him in company with Mrs. Abrahams in a hackney-coach in Thread needle-street—I have also seen him at the house of the prisoner Moses—Moses keeps a public-house in Vinegar-yard, Drury-lane—I did not know who Mrs. Abrahams was at the time I saw her at Davis's—she called me by my name, but I did not know her—she told me she lived at her father's (Moses) house—I produce a £300 bank note, ( marked No. 15,) which I received from Mr. De Mole, and two £5 notes which I got out of the Bank of England.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. What time on the 2nd of April was it that you saw Mrs. Abrahams at Coventry-court? A. About eight o'clock in the evening, as near as I can recollect—seven or eight o'clock—I searched part of Solomon's house—Roe searched the upper part the same day—I first searched it on the 16th of April—I consider that I searched it thoroughly, as far as I had any thing to do with it—I found nothing there then—I went there again on the 7th of May—I did not go with Solomon—I did not make any arrangement with him.
COURT. Q. Had you made any arrangement with Solomon to go there? A. No—I never saw Solomon.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Your arrangement was with Mr. Abrahams, his attorney? A. Yes—I do not know what relation he is to Solomon—I do not know that he is his brother-in-law, not of my own knowledge—I think I have heard it mentioned that they are related.
COURT. Q. Had you made an arrangement with Mr. Abrahams to go to Solomon's house? A. Yes, on the morning of the 7th of May.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. What were you to get from Solomon by that arrangement? A. I was to go for these five bars of gold—I was not to go and find them there—I was to see John Dean, and John Dean was to give them to me—I went with him up to the top of the house—he undid a large box, which was corded up—he unlocked it, took the bars out, and gave them to me—it was not arranged that I was to go and find them in that box—there was no arrangement of the kind—all the arrangement made with me was that I was to receive them from John Dean—I did not know where they were till he took me up stairs, and gave them to me—Mr. Abrahams was with me, nobody else—Solomon was in custody at the time.
Q. And part of the arrangement was that you were to let him turn Queen's evidence if he gave it up? A. No, it was not arranged by me—I believe it was part of the arrangement made—all I know is I was to go there and receive these bars—I believe he was to give up the gold, and then to be let out as Queen's evidence—I have not the slightest doubt about it—it was in order to bring other people to justice as well—I do not know that it was part of the arrangement that he was to convict somebody else—I cannot say about convicting them—I did not say convict, I said to bring them to justice—I did not mean that he was to convict right or wrong—he was in jail at that time—I did not know where his sons were—I knew they were gone off—they had absconded—he has two sons.
Q. Was it not part of the arrangement that the two sons were to perform a part in convicting somebody, or bringing them to justice? A. I never heard any thing of the kind named—the first I saw of them was at the office at Lambeth-street—I believe that was the same day as the gold was given up, and Solomon admitted as a witness—I believe Dean had been off too—Dean turned Queen's evidence, and told the same story.
Q. I should like to know whether, before they were allowed to be Queen's evidence, and the gold was given up, you had got down what they were to swear to? A.
