No, I knew nothing at all about it—I had not got down what they were to say—I did not know that there was a statement made by Solomon of what he would say if they would let him out—I did not know he had made the statement till it was produced at the office—I did not know from Abrahams what the statement was—I did not know any thing about it till I was at Lambeth-street—I heard that a statement would be made—I did not hear that it had been made—a statement was produced at the office which he swore to—I suppose that was the statement he had made before—I had nothing to do with it—I saw his sons and Dean at the office, and heard them give their statements—I did not manage the matter with Mr. Abrahams—I do not know who did—Mr. Abrahams sent for me that evening to go to Solomon's—I had been in communication with him before—he told me about Solomon being evidence, and giving up the gold—I was in communication with him from time to time—I had seen him twice before—I did not learn from him what Solomon would swear—the object of my communication with Mr. Abrahams was about the restoration of the property, and also about bringing other parties to justice—I said very little to Mr. Abrahams on the subject—I referred him to the solicitors for the prosecution—I thought he had better speak to them—I do not know that he went to them—I afterwards learnt from him that I was to get this gold, and that Solomon was to be admitted as a witness—I have not seen Davis since about the 20th of April, I should say—I think the last time I saw him was at the police-office
—I had seen him at Moses' house previously—I have not been drinking with Davis at the Flying Horse since the 20th of April—I might have done so before that, I cannot tell—the last time I saw him was when the gold was taken away—it is possible I drank with him—Mrs. Abrahams was there at the time—she was there with him—Moss was not there—not in the same room—I do not recollect drinking at all with Davis at the Flying Horse—I said it was possible I might, but I do not recollect it—I will not swear I did not—I did not drink with him till he got drunk—I never saw him drunk—he went with me in a coach, with Moss, to the prison, about seven or eight o'clock in the evening—it was as near the 20th of April as I can recollect—I cannot tell to a day.
COURT. Q. Did you drink with Davis? A. I dare say I might—I cannot say whether I did or not.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Do you mean to say yon doubt whether you did or not? A. Yes, I do—I am sure I do not know whether I did or not—I did not sit down in his company, for I was very busy in the office, backwards and forwards, at the time—I was not on duty at the office that day—I left business about seven o'clock—Moss was in custody that day—I partly had the care of him, but there were some policemen there to take charge of him also.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. As soon as this robbery was discovered, you were applied to, and consulted about it—is that so? A. Yes—I was applied to on the 25th of March—for a few days all my transactions were with the two Caspars—it was on the 16th of April, Roe and I went to search Solomon's house—Dean was there at the time—he did not then show me any gold in a trunk up-stairs, nor give me any information about it—I think it was two or three days after that Dean and the two young Solomons disappeared—I "searched Davis's house in Coventry-court, on the 2nd of April, and found Alice Abrahams there—it is a house of ill fame—a house of accommodation—the door is kept open—Mrs. Davis was sitting with Alice Abrahams in the parlour—I asked her no questions, and she told me nothing—I think it was about a fortnight after, that she made some communication—I think it was somewhere about the 13th or 14th, she made some communication, in consequence of which she was examined at the office on the subject—her communication to me partly referred to Solomon—it was on her communication that I pursued the scent with relation to Solomon—I saw her I think about twice or three times between that time and my going after Solomon —I saw her on the Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, and then I went to Solomon with Roe and made the search."
Q. After that did you see her examined before the Magistrates at Lambeth-street? A. I remember one day she was asked some questions by Mr. Clarkson before Mr. Hardwick—it was a few words—I should not like to say whether she was sworn or not—to the best of my belief and recollection she was not—I may be wrong—I will not swear about it—I was very busy with the case that day, running in and out—she might have been sworn in my absence—she went away under Roe's protection—not as a prisoner, but to be brought up again as a witness when wanted—she was brought up again next day, found bail, and entered into sureties in the sum of 1500 l., to appear as witness if wanted—she came op again when required—I took Solomon into custody on the 16th of April—I should say
Solomon was present, and heard what she said before the Magistrate—I have no doubt about it—the clerk read it.
Q. Had Solomon before that, said any thing to you against Mrs. Abrahams, or Mr. Moses, her father? A. No—I saw nothing of his sons till the day they were brought up and examined, when the gold was given up—Mrs. Abrahams was afterwards made a prisoner, and Solomon admitted as a witness for the crown—a man named Rees was also taken into custody in consequence of something she said—he is brother-in-law to Solomon-Moss, his wife, and sister were also taken into custody.
Q. How soon did Moss begin to give information, and try to be made a witness? A. I should say about the 10th of April—there were some suspicions about it for two or three, or three or four days—I was not in any private room, when Moss was examined before Mr. Hardwick, the counsel and attorneys—he was taken out of the office into a room in front of the house, but what was going on there I do not know—I cannot say who was there—I was in the office.
Q. Well, who disappeared from the office? A. Mr. Hardwick left the office—I do not recollect whether the counsel and attorneys left—Mrs. Abrahams was not there—that occurred only once—I should say they were absent from the office about an hour or an hour and a half—Mrs. Abrahams was then committed as a prisoner—the servant, Jane Bradley, has been in my care ever since—I placed her at the Flying Horse in Lambeth-street—it is a very respectable house—she has been acting as servant there—she has been there ever since—I have seen her there many times—I have not conversed with her—it is about ten weeks ago since she was examined at the police-office—she has taken means to refresh her memory as to what she said—that was not by my advice—she went to Mr. Humphreys to have her statement read—she requested to go—I did not put it into her head at all—she said it was so long ago she would like to have it read over.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Was Lewin Caspar the first person from whom you received information of the robbery? A. Yes, he came to the office about three o'clock as near as I can recollect—I went with him immediately to the wharf—I am sure I went to the wharf first—it was not to the office in John-street—I do not think we called at the office in our way to the wharf—I am quite sure we did not—I recollect we went straight from our office to the wharf, and John-street would be out of our way a good deal—we went along Swan-street, through the Minories and Tower-hill—we did not go down John-street, George-street, and across America-square—I have not the slightest recollection of calling at the office—I would not swear we did not—I know Mr. Blundell—I saw him at the office that morning—Lewin Caspar gave me a description of the man who had taken the boxes away, at the office in John-street, and he also gave some description when he came to the police-office—he appeared to be very much agitated, and put himself in a variety of attitudes—I could hardly make him out at all—he was knocking his head about—he appeared very much hurt about it—I do not know whether it was put on or not—he appeared very different that evening and the next day—he expressed great anxiety that I should go with him immediately, and render every possible assistance—I did not see Mr. Blundell at the wharf before I went to the office—I should say I went to the wharf before I saw Mr. Blundell—I will not swear I did not see him before I went to the wharf.
Q. Did Blundell give you any description of the man who took away the boxes? A. To the best my recollection he could give very little description of him—I believe he did give some description—I remember he could speak very slightly of him, in fact I asked every one of the clerks—as for as my recollection serves me, Blundell gave a very slight description of the man, but he did give a description that he was dark, tall, and so on—I had seven or eight descriptions from different clerks there—some described him as one sort of man, and some as another sort—I had seven or eight different descriptions of him from persons, the greater part of whom were connected with Mr. Hartley's wharf and office—I think Blundell said he thought the man had grey whiskers, but he would not speak distinctly on it—I particularly asked him that—he certainly said he thought the man had grey whiskers—he did not give me that description of the man before Lewin Caspar gave me any description at all—that I swear positively—Lewin Caspar had mentioned about the grey whiskers to roe before I saw Blundell at the office—Blundell's description corresponded with Lewin Caspar's as to the whiskers, but not as to the person—Blundell did not describe the man's age at all—I did not ask him—I do not recollect that any thing passed between us respecting the man's age—I do not recollect what he said about the man's age at all—I will not swear he did not describe him as about sixty years of age—I spoke to half a dozen clerks about it, and I cannot recollect—I spoke very little to Blundell about it at all.
Q. At what o'clock did Lewin Caspar leave you the night you met him and his father at the Swan with Two Necks? A. I think about half-past ten, or between ten and eleven o'clock—it was Monday night—while we were in the coach, I expressed a wish to see Mr. De Mole before the bills were issued—he immediately desired his father to inform Mr. De Mole of it, and he got out of the coach for that purpose.
MR. BODKIN. Q. With respect to Bradley, did you take care of her at the express desire of the Magistrate? A. Yes, and Mr. Hartley also—I have been an officer nearly twenty-one years—on my solemn oath I did not in any way suggest to her to go to Mr. Humphreys to hear her evidence read over—Mrs. Abrahams attended as a witness at the police-office on the 14th of April—it was very near the end of that day's examination—there were only one or two questions asked her—her examination was postponed—Solomon was not made a witness until public application had been made to the Magistrate, and their sanction obtained, on a statement of the circumstances—that was after the interview which took place between the counsel for the prosecution and the Magistrate in another room.
COURT. Q. This communication was in a private room was it? A. Yes, it was with regard to Moss—I think Solomon was admitted as a witness two or three days after.
MR. JONES. Q. About what time was it that Ellis Caspar got out of the coach on your return from the railway? A. As near as I can recollect about ten minutes or a quarter past nine o'clock—it might have been something later—it might be half-past nine o'clock, but it was not earlier than a quarter past.
Q. What is the distance from Charing Cross where he got out to New-street, where Moss lived? A. About two miles and a half I believe—it is that at least—I saw him again in the course of that night—I was with him from the time of getting into the coach at the Swan with Two Necks,
to the time of getting out at Charing Cross—I never lost sight of him—I can undertake to swear it was not as late as ten o'clock when we got out of the coach.
GEORGE FREDERICK BULT . I am in partnership with my brother as goldsmiths and dealers in bullion, in Cheapside. Before this occurrence, we had been in the habit of purchasing bullion and dealing with Solomon, a refiner, in the Strand, to the amount of from about 7000 l. to 10, 000 l. a year, for the last five or six years—I was present when young Selim Solomon received a cheque of 1200 l. from my brother, on Messrs. Robarts, on account of two bars of gold, which we had bought of him on the 2nd of April—we had bought the gold of Solomon, but his eldest son Selim brought it to us, as he had been in the habit of doing—the weight was 408oz. gross—we have a correspondent named Emerique, of Paris—in purchasing bullion the object of the party is to turn it into money directly—we seldom keep it above a few hours if we can dispose of it—it depends on the state of foreign exchange—we shipped these bars of gold to Emerique, of Paris, I think on the 5th of April—I had in the meantime made a communication to Mr. De Mole, in consequence of what. I had heard of the loss of the gold-dust—I made the communication the same day I received the gold, before I sent it away—when gold is once melted, if it is amalgamated with copper or any thing, it will entirely alter the colour, which would prevent it being identified—the alloy will alter the colour—the colour depends on what it is alloyed with—I have two assay papers—( marked 12 and 13)—they came from Mr. Johnson, the assayer, in the ordinary course of business—it is the custom to take a little piece off to assay gold—I did not pay the balance for the gold on the same day the 1200 l. cheque was paid—we had an order not to pay the balance—Edward Clark, our clerk, had paid some money to Solomon on the 2nd of April on account, but not in my presence—there were two payments made on the 2nd of April to Solomon—I do not exactly know whether it was on the 2nd without my books, which my clerk can produce to-morrow—the payments were all made by my clerk—he can prove these things to-morrow—he is not here—this is the cheque for 1200 l.—( marked No. 11.)
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did not the ingot that was brought to you appear the result of smeltings of different sorts of gold? A. I am not a smelter, therefore I cannot say—it must have been melted of different materials, because of the alloy in it—I do not understand what you mean by smeltings of different kinds of gold—if it was old jewellery and coins I should call them different—it did appear the result of smeltings of that description of material, as far as my judgment goes—it might be old coins, jewellery, and gold-dust all mixed together—there is so much disguise in gold, it is impossible to tell unless you see it.
COURT. Q. Is it so much altered by alloy it is difficult to say what it is? A. Yes.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. That was your judgment at the time, that it was different kinds of gold, old coins and jewellery, and the setting of diamonds? A. No, there would be silver in that—it might be watch-cases, snuff-boxes, and such articles.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you any judgment of melting gold. A. I have frequently seen gold melted, but I never melted it myself—it was not possible for a man like myself to distinguish of what the bars, which I received from Solomons, were composed—two or three penny pieces put
among it would alter the complexion of the gold—nobody can tell what a bar is composed of without melting it—whether it was composed of chains, watch-cases, or gold in the ore, melted with copper, I could not tell.
( Adjourned.)
Thursday, June 27 th, 1839.
GEORGE ROBINSON re-examined. Q. Look at these notes. A. I have seen them—they have been in my possession—they came into my possession for the first time on 14th April—I parted with them on the 17th at the New Inn in the Old Bailey—I delivered them to Moss—none of the prisoners were present when I gave them up to him—they are the same I received from him—this IOU was among them—I received them at my own house, in Moss's presence, with this I O U among them—they were all received at one time, and all parted with at one time.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You were concerned for Mrs. Abrahams? A. Yes, for her alone, on the first occasion, and afterwards for Moss—he sent me several statements in writing—he made several statements to me—I never saw the letters—he mentioned to me respecting his forging the word Falmouth.
HENRY SOLOMON . I am a goldsmith and refiner—on the 25th of March I carried on business at 53, Strand—I had another shop at No. 9, Coventry-street—I know the prisoner Emanuel Moses—I have known him eight or nine years—it might be ten years—I have also known Alice Abrahams for some few years back—I have a son named Isaac, and a daughter Mary—on Easter Tuesday last I was in my shop in the Strand—Emanuel Moses came to my house, and said he wanted to speak to me—after some few minutes he said again he wished to speak to me, and intimated a wish to be private, and walked towards the end of the shop—I followed him, and we went up stairs into the sitting room together—my daughter Mary was in the sitting room when we came in, and seeing somebody with me, she got up and walked out—when she had left, Moses asked me if I would purchase some gold-dust, and what would I give an ounce for it?—I said gold-dust varied from 3 l. 15 s. to 3 l. an ounce—he then said he had a quantity of it—I said I would not take more than thirty ounces of it till I had tried it to see what it was—he said "Very well," and we agreed about the price—I was to give him 60 s. or 3 l. an ounce—I said one or the other—I do not know which, but the amount is the same—he said, "Very well, I will sand my daughter Alice with it in about half an hour"—he said, "Remember, you don't know me, and I don't know you," or, "I don't you and you don't know me"—those were the words he said before he left me—he then went away, to the best of my recollection—I do not remember his saying any thing more—about half or three quarters of an hour afterwards his daughter, the prisoner Abrahams, came into the shop—she said, "I have brought what my father spoke to you about"—I understood what she meant—she walked on towards the workshop, and 'I followed her—my workshop is at the back of my front shop—I have materials there for, melting gold and silver—there are furnaces there—I put a crucible in the fire, and she produced some lumps of gold from her bosom, and from her pocket—I put them into the crucible as she gave them to me—I perceived that she had brought more than I told her father to send, and I made some
observation—she said, "There is nothing to fear," and produced a bunch of keys, and said it was all locked up safe, and ought to be melted that day, or some observation of that kind which I do not exactly remember—I felt intimidated at the time—I do not recollect any other observation she made—after I had melted what she brought, I poured it out into two skillets, and took it into the front shop—Alice Abrahams followed me into the front shop, and I weighed it there—to the best of my recollection it weighed one hundred and ten ounces, it might be a little more or less—I said there was a great deal more than I anticipated, and I felt a very great deal of alarm indeed, and she took hold of my hand, and swore a Hebrew oath that she would never divulge that I had melted it—after having weighed it, I gave her a memorandum—I recollect what I wrote upon that memorandum.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Did you ever have that memorandum back? A. No.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What was on that memorandum? A. I wrote down the weight and the price, 60 s. or 3 l. an ounce—I also wrote the amount that it came to, and if I recollect right, the monies paid also—I paid her 330 l. in notes and silver—to the best of my recollection that was what I paid her—she went away with an understanding to bring more—she returned again in about a half or three quarters of an hour, and brought some more gold—I melted that also in the same way—after I had done so I put it down on the memorandum paper—I took it into the front shop and weighed it the same as the other, and put it down on the same memorandum paper—I do not remember what the amount of the weight of that was—I gave her a memorandum of it—I did not pay her at that time—I told her I should have to send into the city for some money—she said she might as well go and fetch some more, and she went away—to the best of my recollection after the second melting I re-melted some of the gold and made a bar of it—I re-melted it with some silver-gilt lace—I gave the bar to my son Isaac, and told him to take it to Cock and Johnson's, who are refiners in Hatton-garden—while my son Isaac was gone, Mrs. Abrahams came back with some more gold—I melted that in the same way—I took it into the back shop, melted it, and took it into the front shop, weighed it, and put it down on the same memorandum paper—to the best of my recollection it was on the same paper—she brought gold in this way five or six times, to the best of my recollection, and each time I melted it and put it down on a memorandum paper—she waited for my son Isaac's return from Cock and Johnson's—she waited in the drawing-room, while is the front room on the first floor—not the room I saw Moses in—that was the back sitting-room—while she was waiting in the drawing-room, her mother, Mrs. Moses, came—I was in the drawing room at the time, and she came up stairs where Mrs. Abrahams was—she said her husband had sent her to see why Alice had stopped, and that he was uneasy—I told her I had sent into the city for some money—she seemed satisfied, and went away—my son Isaac brought me 400 l. in bank notes and sovereigns to the best of my recollection.
Q. Did he, in the presence or hearing of Mrs. Abrahams, state what the money had come from? A. Yes, he told me he had got a cheque from Messrs. Johnson, on Williams, Deacon, and Labouchere, the bankers, and that he had got from them, to the best of my recollection, a 300 l. and a 100 l. note, and he went to the Bank of England and changed them for
small notes and sovereigns—I gave them to Mrs. Abrahams as I received them from him, and put down the amount on the memorandum paper—after paying her the 400 l. the amount left me a debtor to them between 2 and 3000 l.—that was after paying the 400 l. and 330 l. also—to the best of my recollection it was something about 2500 l., some few pounds under or over—it was all at the rate of 3 l. an ounce—in that state of things I gave her an I O U for the amount—that I O U was afterwards returned to me by her, and I gave her another—I destroyed the first I O U when I gave her the second.
Q. Do you remember in the course of the transaction, whether there was any portion of gold that did not come into the account? A. Yes, after all the gold was brought, there were some small pieces of gold which Mrs. Abrahams produced from her pocket, some was out of a little leather bag, not much bigger than a watch-pocket—she gave that to my servant Dean to melt for her, and I weighed it—she said I was not to say any thing to her father about that—to the best of my recollection that weighed between four and five ounces—I paid her 13 l. for it, and that was not put down in the account—she took it out of her pocket, and said she had got this out of the shakings out of the bag, or something of that description—I do not exactly recollect what, but not to say any thing to her father—she particularly pressed that on me—I put the gold in my iron chest for that evening—I re-melted it on the Wednesday morning—my servant Dean assisted me—I put some Russian copper coin with it, but I had previously re-melted on the Tuesday evening one of the bars with about seventy ounces of silver—I put the Russian coin in that I melted on the Wednesday to alter the colour—that is the truth—it is no use to disguise it, and I put the silver in what I melted on the Tuesday for the same purpose—that would have the effect of altering the colour and preventing detection—after I had alloyed the gold with the copper coin on the Wednesday, it was melted into bars—I sent two of those bars to Messrs. Bult, of Cheapside, by my son Selim—on his return he gave me eleven 100 l. notes and one hundred sovereigns—Mrs. Abrahams came in soon after, and I gave her six of the 100 l. notes and one hundred sovereigns—I put that payment down on the memorandum—that reduced my debt with them to 1800/. and some odd pounds—the I O U I had first given her was then destroyed, and I gave her this one—( read —I O U 1807 l., H. S.)—my son Selim was present at the time I paid the £600 notes and one hundred sovereigns, to the best of my recollection—upon my giving Mrs. Abrahams the last I O U she begged of me not to destroy, the memorandum, for her father would be very angry with her if she did not return it—she said, "Pray do not destroy it, sir, for if you do, my father will be very angry with me"—I gave it her back—some part of it was destroyed—I was in the act of doing it at the time, and part of it was torn off—to the best of my recollection, the amounts remained entire, and so did the sums paid her—there was a balance due to me from Bult and Co. for the two bars I sent them—I have not received it—I had dealings before with Messrs. Bult for some years.
Q. On the same day you sent them the two bars, had you sent them any thing else before? A. Not that morning—on the Tuesday morning I had sent them some Irish and Scotch notes and some foreign coins to about the amount of 140 l.—I do not recollect sending any thing else on Wednesday morning but the two bars—I do not recollect what might be sent from Coventry-street,
because my son attends there—I was endeavouring to get money at that time for the purpose of making payments.
Q. Do you recollect whether in the course of the day on which you sent the two bars to Messrs. Bult they made any communication to you on the subject of them? A. Yes, I do—I was not in a condition, after settling with Mrs. Abrahams, to return Messrs. Bult their money.
COURT. Q. Do you mean the 1200 l? A. Yes.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. At what rate did they buy of you? A. At the rate of 77 s. 9 d. standard per ounce—I cannot form any judgment what would be the difference with Russian copper and silver—the quantity of copper was put in accidentally—I no doubt made a good profit—Mrs. Abrahams did not call on me for three, four, or five days after I paid her the six £100 notes and one hundred sovereigns—she then said that her father was very anxious for more money—at that time there had been some inquiries made about London—I told her there had been a great stir made about it, and I could not part with any more money or sell another ounce of gold at present—I told her not to call at present for any more money, I could not sell another ounce of gold—I felt very uneasy about it.
Q. After your son Isaac had taken what you sent to Cock and Johnson, did you receive a communication also from them? A. I saw Mr. Cock myself—he made a communication to me about the gold on the Thursday—I afterwards sent my young man (Dean) to them—I think that was on the Friday—I was afterwards summoned to give an account of this matter at Lambeth-street—I received a summons on the Friday night to appear on the Saturday, to the best of my recollection—that was the Friday in Easter-week—I had my attorney with me when I attended—it was Mr. Plumtree on that day—you put some questions to me, and after some answers which I gave I declined answering any more questions—the magistrate let me go—on my receiving the summons on the Friday, before I went to the magistrate, I sent my son Isaac to the prisoner Moses' house—he brought me back a message from Moses—on the Saturday when I went before the Magistrate I gave directions to my son Selim, and Dean, about money, and in consequence of those directions I got back six £100 notes, and 75 l. in £5 notes, of the money I had paid Mrs. Abrahams, the self-same notes—I got them changed for sovereigns—I got rid of them—I paid some in to my bankers, and got change for them as well as I could—I had given my son Isaac directions on Easter Tuesday to get money for me—I sent to Cock and Johnson's the bars—I sent in other directions for money, to more than one, two, or three places—I know Mr. Woolf, a tailor in Regent-street—he is a nephew of my wife's—I sent to him among others—I told my son Isaac to go to him, and he brought me 120 l. in notes and sovereigns.
Q. Among the money you paid Mrs. Abrahams did you pay any of the notes your son brought you from Woolf? A. Yes, no doubt I did, to the best of my recollection—I did not change them after they were brought to me before I gave them to her—it was on Easter Tuesday I sent to Woolf for money—on the following Wednesday, the next day, I returned him in this cheque—( looking at one)—the money I had borrowed.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Well, Mr. Solomon, you know this Court before, do you not? A. I never was in this Court to my knowledge—I was a witness in the other Court—I do not know How long ago that is—I believe it is about six months ago—it was about some
stolen plate—I was told then that I ought to have been in the dock, but allow me to explain: a regular dealer I had known for years by the name of Laurence, well known in the trade, came to my shop one morning, and produced some plate—he asked me for that plate 9 s. 6 d. peroz.—I offered him for that plate 9 s. an ounce—he would not take it, and was going with it to the next neighbour of mine, Mr. Warner, a silversmith in the Strand—I sent for him back again, and offered him 9 s. 3 d. for it—he would not take that—there being some articles among them which I wanted, (I should not have given that price at any other time, but there being two Or three articles I wanted) I gave him 9 s. 6 d. an ounce for it—some few days after, to the best of my recollection, my young man came home and said he had seen a bill at Messrs. Bult's with a description of this plate, saying it was stolen by a town-traveller of Mr. Lias; that it had been absconded with by a town-traveller of Mr. Lias, the same plate that I had purchased—he discovered that it had been stolen—we sent immediately to Mr. Laurence to give him information of it—he, in his turn, went to Mr. Lias, and gave the information himself who he had bought it of, and sold it to me—we gave the information from our house to Mr. Laurence, and he went and told Mr. Lias—we had no business to inform Mr. Lias—we told the man we bought it of—I gave evidence in the other Court when the case was tried there—I was told by the Judge that I ought to have been placed in the dock, and tried—he told me I was a lucky man not to be placed in the dock—that was in answer to a question I asked—I asked who the plate was to be given to, to the best of my recollection—I did not ask if I was to have it, to the best of my recollection—be did not say I was lucky in not being tried for receiving stolen goods—he said, "You are a lucky man not to be placed in the dock;" but I beg to tell you I was then going to give the explanation to the Judge of our having given information in the first instance of it; and he said, "There is no imputation on you, Mr. Solomon"—it was Mr. Sergeant Arabin, I think, tried the case—I do not know that I was complimented by the Counsel—the case was tried at night, and, in the confusion, asking about the plate, that observation did pass in the Court, and it was the general impression in the Court that the Judge mistook me for Mr. Laurence—it was night before the case was finished—I do not mean to swear it was night—I know there were lamps alight—since then Mr. Laurence has paid me back at the rate of 9 s. 6 d. an ounce, in bills at two, six, and nine months, which bills I have sent, with some others, to Ransom's, the bankers—he gave me the bills with the rate of five per cent, interest.
Q. What time in the day on the 2nd of April, Easter Tuesday, was it that Mrs. Abrahams came to you? A. To the best of my recollection, it must have been about a quarter before twelve o'clock—I cannot exactly remember the time—I have known her some few years, not by dealing with her, or her with me—perhaps I have very seldom, but I have been to the bar, and taken some sherry—I do not suppose it was a dozen times—it was at her father's house, where she served—I was very much alarmed when she brought me the 110 ounces, when I saw there was a quantity of it, because I had previously told her father only to send thirty ounces—I do not know that I never intended to buy more than thirty ounces—I certainly did not expect more than thirty ounces when I told him at first—I did not expect to have more than the thirty ounces at first; but when she
brought it me I was very much alarmed—I found I had got into it, and I suspected this was the gold that was lost—the gold I heard of that was stolen—the gold dust.
Q. And then you desired her to bring you some more? A. She went away to bring more—I did not desire her to bring more—it was when I saw the 110 ounces that I was alarmed.
Q. Then you thought it was the gold dust that was stolen, and after that paid her the money, and agreed that she should bring more? A. She said she would bring more—she swore an Hebrew oath, that she would never divulge it—I did not swear too—she did not take an oath from me that I would not divulge it—I had no idea of taking an oath to her—we did not take each other by the hand—she seized my hand—she swore, I did not—it was after that, all the rest of the gold came.
Q. Had you then any doubt whatever that you were buying the gold that was stolen? A. I suspected it was the gold—I suspected it was that gold—I believed it was that gold—I went on receiving it—she brought me the rest—I did not become quiet and easy about it—unfortunately I have not been out of a state of agitation since.
Q. When you say you were in for it, why did not you give her back the gold before you paid her, if you believed it was stolen? A. Because I had done so—I was unfortunately in it, and felt unhappy all the time—the had previously sworn to me in a very pitiful manner that she would new divulge that I had melted it for her—I do not generally keep a furnace alight in my back-room—I do not recollect whether I lit it on purpose for her—the furnace was burning, and I put the crucible on—the fire was already burning—I paid her 330 l. at first—I paid her the 400 l. in the course of the afternoon—I cannot tell exactly the time—I did not pay attention to the time—to the best of my recollection it must have been between three and four o'clock—it was about that time to the best of my belief—I gave her the 330 l. in the morning—it must have been before twelve o'clock to the best of my belief—I should say it would take about half-an-hour to turn the gold into a bar in my furnace—I had not received all the gold from Mrs. Abrahams before I paid her the 400 l.—it was after I had received the third lot that I paid her, to the best of my recollection—the 400 l. was not paid on the second occasion, it was after the third melting—I melted two or three times after that—I do not know whether my son Isaac was there all day—he was not in the back shop—he attends to the front-shop—he was not in the back-shop that day to my recollection—I do not recollect, upon my honour—I do not mean to say he did not see what was going on—I do not recollect his coming into the back-shop at the time—I do not remember his coming into the back-shop at all—I will not swear he was not there, but I do not remember his coming in there at all—my son Selim was certainly not there, for he attends to the shop in Coventry-street—he was not in the Strand at all on Easter Tuesday to my knowledge, to the best of my recollection he was not—I do not mean to swear it, I say to the best of my recollection—I saw my son Isaac up in the drawing-room that day, when he brought me the 400 l. from the banker's—to the best of my belief he was present when the 400 l. was paid—yes, he was—I recollect it.
Q. Of course he knew what it was paid for, and all about it? A. That I do not know, I am sure—nothing took place in his presence about it—he only told me he had been to the Bank of England and got the 300 l. note
and 100 l. note changed, which he had received from Williams, Deacon, and Co.—he told me that in the presence of Mrs. Abrahams—I did not direct him to get the notes changed when he received them—he got them changed at the Bank of England himself—he thought perhaps it might be more convenient to me—he had no other reason for going—if he had thought I was doing wrong, perhaps he would have brought all sovereigns—I told him to go to Cock and Johnson's and get the money, but did not tell him what for or for whom, to my recollection—I did not to my recollection tell him what for—I swear to my recollection I did not—I merely told him to go and get me this money—that is all I remember—I do remember telling him that, but not for whom—to the best of my recollection he was in the front shop at the time I told him to go to Cock and Johnson's with the bar—to the best of my recollection I went into the front shop and gave him the bar to take—I cannot state it in any other way—I gave it to him in the front shop to the best of my belief—I am almost certain I did—I did to the best of my recollection or belief, I can say no 'more—I will swear to nothing but what I am positive downright to—to the best of my firm belief he was in the front-shop when I gave it to him—I cannot give any other answer—there was nobody present that I recollect when I gave it to him—I do not remember seeing any one present—I was so agitated in the business that I did not notice whether any body was present or not.
Q. As I understand you, you did not at all suspect, till you saw the 110oz. but that it was perfectly right? A. Yes, I did previously suspect it, before she came I suspected it was all wrong, that there was something wrong about it, when Moses said, "Remember, you do not know me and I do not know you"—I do not remember saying that I did not suspect any thing till I saw the 1100oz. instead of 30oz.—I felt alarmed at its being that, it might have been a further confirmation in my mind, but I had a previous suspicion of it—I suspected that it was the gold that was lost.
Q. Did you not ascribe your suspicion to its being 110 oz. instead of 30? A. Yes, very likely I did; but I previously-suspected it—I suspected before she brought the gold that it was the gold that was stolen—I suspected it—I can say no more.
Q. Did you believe before Mrs. Abrahams brought it, that she was going to bring the gold that was stolen? A. I suspected it was—I cannot answer in any other way—I suspected it was the gold lost—I was in great alarm when it came—I was alarmed before she came at all—I did not give it a thought before she came the first time.
Q. Why, you say you suspected it before she came at all? A. Yes—I suspected it, but I was under no alarm, because I had done nothing yet—I felt no alarm as I had had nothing to do with it—she went into the back shop with me when she came, pulled out the gold, and we melted it there at once—there was no one in the back shop with me at first—my servant, Dean, came in at the latter end of the melting of it—I melted it myself the first time or two—I put it into the crucible with my own hands—to the best of my recollection, Dean came in at the latter end of the melting—I have no doubt at all of his being there at the time I was melting the first parcel of gold—it was at the latter end of the first parcel, to the best of my belief—he assisted me in melting the first 110oz.—it was at the latter end of the melting, because I had sent him previously into the City—he was there when the 110 oz. were being melted—to the best of my recollection, he came at in the latter end, just as I had nearly finished—I
had sent him to Cock and Johnson's, and Messrs. Bult's—he was gone an hour or two—he left after eleven o'clock—I received all the gold the same day—all that I received—all that I had to do with, was melted—I do not know whether I bad all which is the cause of the pending inquiry—all the gold that I received I received that day, between five and six o'clock in the evening to the best of my recollection—it must have been about that time—after Dean returned, he and I melted it together.
COURT. Q. Then did Dean assist in all the meltings? A. Yes.
Mr. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Did you put the rest into the crucible, or did he? A. We did it together—my son Isaac was attending in the front shop—I do not know whether he was there all the time or not—all the gold was melted in Mrs. Abrahams's presence—each melting took from half to three quarters of an hour—about half an hour to the best of my recollection—as soon as it was melted she went away to fetch some more at each time—the last parcel was brought, I think, between five and six o'clock, to the best of my recollection, but I did not notice the time—the time it was finished, was between five and six o'clock, the time she went away last—it was early in the afternoon that I sent Isaac out—I do not remember sending any body else out that afternoon—I have no recollection of it—I do not know whether I did or not—it was about three or four o'clock in the afternoon that I paid the 400 l., and after the third melting—there were two or three meltings afterwards—Mrs. Abrahams came next morning (Wednesday)—I think it must have been between twelve and one o'clock—I cannot say exactly the time, because I did not pay attention to it, but it was between twelve and one o'clock, or about one o'clock—the bars which I sent to Messrs. Bult were melted on the Wednesday morning—I am certain of that—it was not on the Tuesday—I mean the re-melting—Mrs. Abrahams saw the original melting, but this was the re-melting—the gold was all re-melted on the Wednesday morning, except the bar sent to Cock and Johnson's, and a bar of 70 oz. melted on Tuesday—I sent two bars to Messrs. Bult, which I melted on the Wednesday morning—that is what I put some copper into—that was all I put in, to the best of my recollection—the copper I put in myself and my servant Dean—I was afterwards summoned and went before the Magistrate.
Q. Was what you swore then true or false? A. I did not swear there true—it was false—I think it was to some question about some 100 l. notes that I objected to answer' any thing more to, to the best of ray recollection—I was not thinking all the time, but I swore falsely to protect myself certainly—I was not thinking all the time I was questioned—I swore falsely to screen myself.
Q. Have you come here now to screen yourself? A. I have come here to give evidence of the truth now.
Q. Are you come to swear to what you say, in order to get yourself off? A. All I have stated here to-day is the truth—Yes, certainly it is to screen myself, I tell the truth—I would not tell a falsehood—I wish to make every reparation in my power—I feel very great remorse for what I did before the Magistrate—I feel very great sorrow—I was taken into custody after that—I do not know, I am sure, what became of my sons—I was in prison—I did not see them at all—I did not know whether they were gone off—since I have been out I have heard of it—I knew nothing of it before—I did not know whether Dean was off too—I knew nothing about
their being out of the way—I was in prison, and knew of nothing that went forward—Rees, my brother-in-law, was in prison also.
Q. Did you know that Mrs. Abrahams was examined as a witness? A. I saw her from the bar standing there, but I paid no attention to what passed—I was in such a fright that I paid no attention, and that is the truth—I was panic-struck—I was panic-struck at the very first communication—when I was taken into custody, on the 16th of April, I hardly knew what was going forward—I saw Mrs. Abrahams there—I did not hear her give evidence, to my knowledge—I heard that she was to give some evidence, if I recollect correctly, but I was in such a situation that I paid no attention to any body else—I saw her there giving some kind of evidence, but I paid no kind of attention to it.
Q. Did it occur to you that it was against you, or in relation to you? A. It did when I saw her there—I paid no attention to what passed—to the best of my recollection, I think I heard my name mentioned; but I do not recollect what passed—to the best of my recollection, I mean to swear, I did hear it—I cannot say positively whether I did or not—I was in such a state of dilemma that I did not see or hear any thing of what passed—I did not pay any attention—she might have mentioned my name—Mr. Abrahams, my attorney, is no relation to me or my wife—neither him or his wife—his wife is not my niece—Abrahams's wife and Mrs. Woolf are sisters, and Woolf is a nephew of my wife's—to the best of my recollection I heard Mrs. Abrahams mention my name—I will not swear she did not mention it—I rather think, to the best of my belief she did—I hardly have a doubt about it—my solicitor came to me while I was in prison, and I made a communication to him—he took a statement from me in writing—I suspected it was for the purpose of being communicated to the attorney for the prosecution—I believed that it was for that purpose—he suggested it to me first, to state the whole of the truth—I gave my statement to Mr. Abrahams, and told him I wished to make every reparation which laid in my power—he suggested it to me, and I agreed to it—I was very happy to do so, to make every reparation in my power, and to tell the whole truth—it was impressed upon my mind to tell the truth—I sent that statement before any communication was made to me that I was to be Deceived as a witness—Mr. Abrahams came and took my statement, and I said that statement—I do not know what day it was—it was a long while before I was examined as a witness—I cannot at all tell How long—it was longer than a week, I will swear that; and, to the best of my recollection, it must have been a fortnight too—I knew where the gold was at that time—I had no communication with Dean from the tune I made that statement till I was admitted as a witness, neither directly nor indirectly—I was in prison, and had no communication with any one but my solicitor—I sent no message to him, nor to my sons—I had nothing but through my solicitor—he talked to me about the business of the lease of my house, and I desired him to attend to it for me—I got rid of the lease of my house in the Strand—that was not done in case I should not be a witness, for this has ruined me altogether—I do not recollect whether it was before I was admitted as a witness that I got rid of my house in the Strand—I do not remember—I got rid of it, because I never could live in it any longer after this transaction—not that I might be prepared for my trial, and that nobody should take it—I believe it is now sold to a person in King William-street—I have not troubled my head with my own affairs—
my wife carries on business now in Coventry-street—where I did before—no where else.
Q. This would prevent your carrying on business in the Strand, but not in Coventry-street? A. Yes, because we lived in the Strand.
Q. Did you give any directions to any body to give up the gold? A. I told my solicitor I would make every reparation in my power—I gave directions to my solicitor to cause the gold to be given up, and that I was ready to make every reparation in my power—I gave up all the gold which remained after this unfortunate transaction—I did not give up any notes—there was 50 l. taken out of my cash-box, in Coventry-street, by Roe—I have not undertaken to give over any money of any kind, nothing but the gold—I paid for the gold 330 l., 400 l., 700 l. and 13 l., besides—the greater part of the gold I think I have not paid for—I cannot tell exactly what money I have received for the gold I got from Mrs. Abrahams, because I received money for silver bars, and Scotch and Irish notes and gold coin at the same time—I can swear I received 1200 l. from Messrs. Bult for the gold, and there is a balance in their hands, and 400 l. from Cock and Johnson's—I do not recollect what the balance is in Messrs. Bult's hands—he was to pay about 60 l. balance for the gold, to the best of my recollection, but I do not recollect—there was no arrangement for my handing over any more money of any kind—I do not know a man of the name of Davis—I have heard of his name—I should not know him if he stood before me—I do not know him, not the man who is indicted—I never saw him to my recollection in my life—I might have seen him passing the street, and not know who he was—my sons were not off before I was taken up—I left them at home when I was taken—I was at liberty above a week after I was first examined—it was above a week after I was sworn and examined at Lambeth-street that I was taken up—it was on a Tuesday I know, and I think it must have been on the 16th of April I was taken up.
Q. You had the gold in your house at that time? A. I gave it to my servant Dean to take care of—I gave it him the morning after we melted it, when I received the message from Messrs. Bult—I gave it to him to take care of—to put away—I either gave it him, or told him to take it out of the iron chest—I was agitated and alarmed about it—I gave to him to put away—to put it out of the way—I did not use the word conceal—I gave it him to take it out of my sight—I did not see where he took it—my house was not searched before I was taken up—I do not know whether it was after I was taken up—I did not ask Dean where the gold was before I went to gaol—I only gave it him to take care of to put it out of the way—I believe he is my servant still—the gold was in my iron chest—I kept the key of it at the time—my son Isaac had nothing at all to do with taking care of any part of the property, only Dean—I only sent Isaac with a bar to Cock and Johnson's—he attended in the front shop.
Q. Was he trusted in respect of your property generally? A. He is my son—he did all things I wished him to do—Dean only had the care of this property—before that I locked it in my iron chest myself—I kept my papers and deeds and coin in that iron chest—I put bowls of coin there of a night—there was no other melted gold there, to the best of my recollection—I am a general dealer in gold and silver, a bullion dealer generally—my son Selim had nothing to do with the shop in the Strand at that time—I sent for him on the Wednesday morning—he had
very little indeed to do with that shop—he might come over occasionally, but very little indeed—he had the entire management of the shop in Coventry-street—my daughter had nothing at all to do with the business—she never interfered in it at all.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Let us understand you rightly, you had two shops at the time of the transaction, one in the Strand and one in Coventry-street—the shop in the Strand is where the fire is ready, and the crucibles prepared? A. Where the gold was melted—it is the place of business—the crucible was ready, unfortunately, at that moment.
Q. It was merely accidental that the crucible was ready, it was never so before? A. Never, to my knowledge—I have been twenty-five years in business—Moses came to me in the morning, and said he had gold dust to sell—I said I would not receive more than thirty ounces—I had not dealt with Moses before for gold—I had dealt with him for spirits—I did not ask where he got the gold—I got 3 l. 17 s. 9 d. an ounce for the gold—that is standard—it contained silver and sulphur at first—I cannot tell what I got for it, because it was mixed with other gold—the gold contains silver and sulphur.
Q. Is not the gold dust coming from the Brazils in the highest state of perfection? A. No, not in the highest perfection, it requires a process to make it pure—I do not know whether it is in the purest state when it comes from abroad—it comes in different prices—it varies in price, and cannot be quite pure—alloy is used to it afterwards to make it workable—I had beard there was gold dust lost before Moses came to me—I heard that it had been stolen—I asked no questions of Moses—I was astonished when Mrs. Abrahams came with the 110 ounces instead of thirty, and I was sorry I had got into it—I had melted it and weighed it before J discovered there was 110 ounces—I could tell, without melting it, that there was more than thirty, ounces, because I made the observation that she had brought more than her father intended—I did not say, "Take it away," nor did I say, "Come again with as much as you please."
Q. Pray did either of your sons see any thing of the transaction of that day, when Mrs. Abrahams was there? A. My son Isaac saw her come in, to the best of my recollection, in the morning, the first time she came with the gold—he did not go with her into the back shop, he remained in the front shop—I do not recollect whether any body else was in the front shop—when Mr. Moses came in I was attending to two or three persons—I do not know who they were—in a public shop I cannot tell people—none of them are here—I received the summons to attend before the Magistrate on the Friday night, three days after I bought the gold—I attended, and was examined—what I then swore was false—I was not thinking at the time, and did it to screen myself—I knew it was wrong—I signed my name to it—I did not consider it willful and corrupt perjury—I did not swear to injure any body, but to screen myself—I was not thinking at the moment—I am very sorry for it—I feel great remorse and sorrow for it—I am satisfied I swore wrong, but I did not think it was wilful corrupt perjury at the time—I regret it very much—my conscience has told me I ought to regret it—I saw Mrs. Abrahams at the office, while I was at the bar in custody—I am sure I do not know How far I was from her when she was being examined—I should think I was as far as I am from the foreman of the Jury now—but I cannot remember such trifling things—I was as far from her as I am now.
Q. Upon your oath was she not interrogated by the Magistrate in a clear
voice, and did not she give her evidence in a clear tone? A. I did not pay attention to it, I was too agitated—the magistrate did not ask me if I had any questions to put to her to my recollection—I was attended by counsel, and I left it to my counsel—Mr. Plum tree attended as my attorney in the first instance, and Mr. Abrahams next—that was when Mrs. Abrahams was examined, but I do not know what passed—I have been detained in custody ever since till now—I have never been out—neither of my sons or Dean have been to see me while I have been in custody—I did not know where they were—I did not know whether they were in England—I have no reason to believe or suppose that they went out of the way and went abroad—I did not write to them and desire them to come back and be witnesses with me at the police-office—I left it in the hands of my solicitor—I believe Dean is still living in the service of my family, carrying on my business in Coventry-street—I have not taken any other house to carry on business in—they have, I believe.
Q. Who are they? A. I do not know I am sure—I do not know where the house is—I do not know whether it is in Hanway-yard—I have heard that some one has taken a house for them—I do not recollect the person's name—he does not act for me—I have been in prison ever since, and have had no communication—I do not know who has taken the house or where it is—I have been in prison, and have not troubled my head about it—I do not know that it was my brother-in-law, Mr. Rees, that took it—I have every reason to believe it is not, or I should have heard it—I do not know who is the owner of the house in Hanway-yard—it is not my brother-in-law—he has no houses there—he did live in Hanway-yard before I went to prison—he lived there in a house of his own—my wife and family have not taken his house—they have taken a house there, but not his house.
Q. Pray How long was it after Alice Abrahams was examined that the remorse struck you, and your conscience began to work? A. Directly after I had bought the first gold—I would have made reparation and told the truth if I could have got out of it then—I did not like to expose myself that I was in it—it was about a week or ten days after Mrs. Abrahams was examined that I made the disclosure—it might have been a fortnight—my conscience had begun to work before that—I only sent one statement—I was examined privately before the Magistrate once, and then. Mr. Hardwick told me I must consider that statement was a voluntary statement, and that was the way I gave it—it was drawn up in writing by my solicitor, and my signature was attached to it—it was the substance of the truth that I was to swear to—I swore to it afterwards before the grand jury, not before the Magistrate—I do not remember swearing to it before the Magistrate—I gave it in—they considered it a voluntary statement—I did not swear to that statement before the Magistrate—I was examined by the Magistrate after I gave that statement in—I was examined afterwards on oath before the Magistrate.
Q. Was that statement of yours put in and sworn to, or did you swear to some other matter which was taken down by the clerk? A. I was sworn to what I am stating now—I swore to all the questions the counsel put to me, and that was the truth—my solicitor wrote down the statement as I stated it to him—he drew it up from my dictation—he wrote it down, and at the same time told me not to add a word more or less, and to tell nothing but the truth and the whole truth—I cannot say whether that same paper was read before the Magistrate, or whether he copied
it before he gave it to the Magistrate, but I know it was read before the Magistrate, and brought to me to sign—I believe a gentleman now on the bench, Mr. Hardwick, and Mr. Clarkson were present in the private room when I made this statement—I do not recollect seeing Lee and Roe there—Mrs. Abrahams was not there—I should think it was nearly a fortnight after that before I was examined as a witness—my sons had been examined before that, and my servant Dean—I remember him being examined—he is in my service now—he attends at the shop in Coventry-street—he has been a great many years in my service—he is a confidential servant—I left him in care of my whole house—I do not know whether he went out of the way when I was in trouble—I was in prison from the 16th of April, and cannot tell you of any thing that took place.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Now, upon the subject of this private examination, was the clerk to the Magistrate present who read over the paper which you had signed? A. Yes—it was purely a voluntary statement made by me to my attorney to be forwarded to the Magistrate.
Q. Did the Magistrate give you any caution, or make any observation to you on the subject of your statement before it was read? A. To the best of my recollection he said it might be used against me, or it might be destroyed—he said if the statement proved untrue I should be put in the bar, and it would be used against me—at that time no promise was made me that I should be a witness—I did not know I was to be received and examined for the Crown till long after.
ISAAC SOLOMONS . I am the son of Henry Solomons. My father had a shop in the Strand, and another shop at No. 9, Coventry-street—I assisted him in the business at the shop in the Strand—I was at that shop on Easter Tuesday last—the prisoners, Mrs. Abrahams and Mr. Moses came there—Moses came first—I was in the front shop next the street when he came—my father was there—Moses spoke to him—he merely said he wished to speak to him, and they went to the passage, and went up stairs—they remained up stairs about ten minutes—Moses then left—about three-quarters of an hour after he was gone Mrs. Abrahams came—she went into the back shop—my father was there with her—there are furnaces in the back shop—I do not know whether there was any fire in them at that time, I believe there was—I did not remain at home all the time Mrs. Abrahams was there—my father directed me to go out—in consequence of directions from him I went to Ransom's, his banker—I returned and told ray father what had happened there—I went out again, and took a cheque for 80 l. with me, drawn by my father on Ransom's—I took the change I got for it to my father—I know Woolf, a tailor in Regent-street—in consequence of my father's directions I went to Woolf, and received a cheque of 100 l. from him on Ransom's—I had also received three£5 notes and five sovereigns from Woolf—I went to Ransom's with the 100 l. cheque of Woolf's, and received the amount of it—I do not remember what I received from Ransom's in payment of that cheque—I delivered it to my father in the same state I received it—I took a bar of gold to Cock and Johnson's the same day, and they gave me a cheque for 400 l. on Williams and Labouchere's bank on account of that bar—I presented it for payment, and received a£300 note and a£100 note—this is the£300 note I received ( looking at the one marked No. 15)—I took it to the Bank of England and got for it twenty £10 notes, and twenty£5 notes—I changed the£100 note for a hundred sovereigns—I carried them back to ray father—I found Mrs. Abrahams
there when I returned—she was in the drawing-room with my father—I gave the notes and sovereigns to my father—I told him I had received them from the Bank of England, that I had got a cheque from Cock and Johnson's on Labouchere's, and the£300 note and£100 note that I got there I had changed at the Bank of England—I remember the day my father was summoned to attend as a witness at Lambeth-street—I had before that taken some French bank notes to Messrs. Bult, and had received some money for them—I do not know the exact amount—it was about 150 l. I should think—I gave that to John Dean—the French bank notes belonged to the shop in Coventry-street—on the evening that my father received the summons I saw the prisoner Moses at his house in Vinegar-yard—I went there by my father's direction—I said to Moses that I wished to speak to him—he said, "What do you want?"—I said I wished to speak privately to him, and I walked up stairs with him—there was nobody in the room besides ourselves—I showed him the summons, and asked him for the bank notes which he had—I believe he had read the summons when I put that question to him—I said if he would let my father have the bank notes he should have sovereigns as soon as possible—he said, "Don't make yourself a child"—he promised me that he would not part with the bank notes, and I then came away—I was aware of the contents of the summons when I went there—when I showed him the paper I said, "I hope the bank notes are perfectly safe, and if you will let me have them back you shall have sovereigns for them"—I said my father had received a summons, and that he had to appear at Lambeth-street.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. How old are you? A. Twenty years of age—I was employed in my father's workshop sometimes, sometimes melting, and doing whatever was necessary in the workshop—my father and myself were in the shop when Moses came in—that is all that I remember—I think there was a stranger there betides—I will not swear that there was any body but myself and my father—I cannot positively say whether any one else was there or not—I cannot swear whether one stranger was there or any body—I will not swear there were not many people in the shop—I am positive the shop was not half full—I only know my father was there—I said I would not be certain whether there was a stranger there—I am not certain there was only one, if there was one, I am positive there were not half-a-dozen people there—I do not remember any body being there except my father—Moses went into the passage and up stairs—he staid about ten minutes—it was about eleven o'clock in the morning—I was present when Mrs. Abrahams came—my father was in the front-shop when she came in, and they went into the back-shop together—I do not remember whether any thing was said—there might have been something said—I do not know whether any thing was said or not—I cannot swear whether I heard any thing said or not—my father was behind the counter with me when she came in—you can not see the furnace from behind the counter of the front-shop—you can see the door of the front-shop from the back-shop, not from all parts—there is a desk in the front-shop—there is the front shop, then a passage, then a back parlour, and then the back-shop—a window divides the passage from the shop—there is a screen before that window, between the desk and the counter—there is no screen between the window and counter—there is a screen before the desk—it is at the side of the counter—it is not between the counter and window—there is the screen, then the desk, and then the window
—the window is behind the screen—the counter runs up behind the shop—the counter runs up by the side of the screen—( looking at a plan of the shop)—this is the counter—the screen is between the counter and the window—this plan is correct—I should think the screen is about six feet high—the window is low and rather long—the screen is higher than the window a foot or two—there are doors between the back parlour, the back shop, and the passage—I do not think the shop is quite 19 feet long—I do not know the measurement of the shop—I cannot say whether the shop is about 19 feet, the passage about 11 feet, and the back parlour about 15 feet—the furnace is in that situation, and the fire place is in that situation, and the staircase also—( pointing them out on the plan)—Mrs. Abrahams came about three quarters of an hour after Moses—I was not there the whole time—I cannot tell the time I left—Mrs. Abrahams was there when I left—she had not gone out at all before I left—I left more than once—she had not gone out at all when I left first—I cannot say whether she was there when I returned the first time—I did not see her—I saw my father in the shop—I staid there about five minutes that time, and then went out again—I staid out about a quarter of an hour the second time—I did not see Mrs. Abrahams when I returned that time—I believe I saw my father—I cannot positively say whether I did or not—I went out a third time, and staid about twenty minutes or half an hour—I do not think I saw Mrs. Abrahams when I returned then—I went out again and stopped out about two hours, I think, I cannot say exactly—I was not out after that, I think—I cannot say what time I returned the last time—I should think it was about four o'clock, or between four and five o'clock—I saw Mrs. Abrahams when I returned the last time—I did not see her go—I cannot recollect whether I remained in the shop all the time afterwards—I do not know whether I did or not—I have no recollection what became of me afterwards all that evening—that I swear—I will not swear I was not in the shop for two or three hours after—I might have been in the shop, but I have no recollection what became of me—I saw Mrs. Abrahams the last time I returned, and gave the notes.—to my father—I remained in the room about a minute or two—what became of me afterwards I have not the slightest recollection—I merely told my father I had received the notes—I do not recollect that any thing else was said when I was there—I left Mrs. Abrahams and my father there—nothing else was said in my presence—my father gave the notes over to Mrs. Abrahams, and the bag of sovereigns—I got the 100 l. cheque changed at Ransom's.
Q. And gave the change, which was in sovereigns, to your father f A. I have since heard that these notes—
Q. What do you mean by since? A. Since I made the statement at Lambeth-street that I had received one hundred sovereigns for the cheque.
Q. Is this the first time you have said you got notes for it? A. I do not know that I did get notes for it—I forget what I did get for it—it is my firm belief that it was sovereigns—it was my firm belief, when I made my statement at Lambeth-street, that it was one hundred sovereigns, but I forgot what I got—it is my belief it was one hundred sovereigns.
Q. Who has told you about their not being sovereigns since? A. My father's solicitor, Mr. Abrahams—I forget on what day he told me so—it is about a week ago—I had my deposition read over to roe at the office—I did not have it read over to me about a week ago—he had given me a copy of my deposition—he told me it was part in sovereigns and part in notes
that I got for the£100 cheque—I should say it is about a week ago he told me so—I do not know whether he gave my brother a copy of his deposition—I have been living with my brother ever since—I do not know whether he was present when I had this conversation with Mr. Abrahams—he might have been—I do not know whether he was present or not—it was in our shop in Coventry-street—Mr. Abrahams came to me—I had not had a copy of my deposition before—I believe he brought it to me then—I received it from him—I do not know whether it was that day—I have not been speaking to Mr. Abrahams several times about this—I do not know whether he gave me the copy of my deposition that day or before—I have seen him several times about this—I had not spoken to him about these notes before that time, nor about the cheque.
Q. Had you any previous conversation with Mr. Abrahams about it? A. He only came and took our statements—that is about three weeks ago—my brother was present then—I had not made a deposition then—it was before we went to Lambeth-street—I do not know the day I was first before the Magistrate—I should think it is seven weeks ago—Mr. Abrahams took our statements before we went before the Magistrate the first time—we were ordered to go before the Magistrate by our solicitor—my father was not examined at that time, he was in prison—he was at Lambeth-street in custody—it was a copy of the same statement I made at Lambeth-street, which Mr. Abrahams gave me, not what I made to him before, but it was the same statement—the statement I made to him before was what I swore to before the Magistrate—the paper Mr. Abrahams gave me was a copy of the statement I made at Lambeth-street—I know it was before I went to Lambeth-street that he took down my statement.
Q. Did you have the charge of any of the keys at your father's shop? A. Occasionally, not always—I did not generally have them, only sometimes—my father kept his gold, and bullion, and things of that kind, in the iron chest—I never had charge of that key—my brother Selim manages my father's business now in Coventry-street—we have no business any where else at present—we have not taken any business any where else—I live in Coventry-street—my father lives in Charles-street—we have taken some premises in Hanway-yard—we have had them about three weeks or a month, I believe—it is a shop—it is not opened—I do not know whether it is taken for me or my father, or who—I do not know at all—I do not know whether my brother has any thing to do with it—I live in the same house with my brother—as far as I know he has nothing to do with the house in Hanway-yard—it is Mrs. Rees's house—she is a widow lady—I do not know much of her—I do know her, but am not acquainted with her—I have an uncle named Rees—I do not know that he is any relation to Mrs. Rees—he never lived with Mrs. Rees that I know of—he lives in the same street—Mrs. Rees is not my grandmother, nor any relation to me that I know of—she may be a relation to Rees—I do not know whether she is his mother—my mother's name was Rees—my mother lives with Mrs. Benjamin, in Church-street, Soho, now—Mrs. Benjamin is not the daughter of this widow, Mrs. Rees—she is an aunt of mine.
Q. Now, you say when you went to Moses, you gave him the paper to read, and he read it? A. Yes—I will not swear he can read—he looked at the paper—I never knew Mrs. Abrahams before I saw her at our house, nor did my father that I know of—he knew Moses I believe—I had seen
Moses before at our house in the Strand about two or three times—I did not know him, but had seen him occasionally—my father has seen him—I suppose he knew him by sight, coming to our house—my father did know him—I do not know whether he knew Mrs. Abrahams—I did not know her by sight—I only know her by seeing her at the office, and at our house—I heard afterwards that it was Mrs. Abrahams I had seen at our house—I did not know it at the time—I did not know where she lived—I do not remember having been to Moses' house before—I could not swear I have not, but I have no recollection of being there before I went that day—I cannot say that I had ever spoken to him in my life.
Q. Had your father communicated to you that he had been buying the gold dust, knowing it to be stolen? A. No; I had no suspicion at all that he had, till he sent me with the summons to Mr. Moses—I had not the slightest suspicion before that—I never spoke to Moses before, to my knowledge—I knew where he lived before my father sent me there—I did not know who Mrs. Abrahams was before I saw her—I believed he had a daughter.
COURT. Q. How did you know, then, it was Mrs. Abrahams at all? A. I saw her at the office.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Did any body tell you at the office that that was the person? A. I heard a person say, "That is Mrs. Abrahams," when they were pointed out—that is the only way I know her.
Q. You had some gold to take out that morning; did you know where that gold came from? A. I knew my father had been melting some—I thought he had been melting it—Mrs. Abrahams had been melting—I thought they had been melting—I was at Boulogne last year—I have not been there since—I did not remain in the Strand till the house was disposed of—I have been away—I absconded at the time my father was taken—I went out of the way—I took a lodging in the Haymarket, at No. 32—my brother Selim, and Dean, were with me, nobody else—we remained there secreted—Mr. Abrahams, the attorney, came to us there—he only came to take our statements—I do not know that that was when my father was to be made a witness—I did not say the statements were taken in Coventry-street—he brought us the depositions in Coventry-street—he came to us in the Haymarket, and took our statements to give to the attorney for the prosecution—mine, my brother's, and Dean's.
Q. Did you believe, when you gave your statement, that you should assist in getting your father out by it? A. I imagined so—I do not know whether my statement went to the attorney for the prosecution first—Mr. Abrahams did not say it was to be sent there first—it was on his advice I went before the Magistrate.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You are brought up to your father's business? A. Yes, and in his religion—your Easter is our Pass-over—at that time the Jews sometimes call and see one another, and take a piece of Passover cake—it is common for us to go and see our friends—I had seen Moses two or three times, but had never spoken to him—I do not remember ever being in his public-house in Vinegar-yard—I am sure of that—I do not know whether my brother Selim goes there sometimes—I have told the truth to-day—I have told to-day what I have told on all occasions—I have said exactly now what I did before—I told Mr. Abrahams what I have told to-day—Mr. Abrahams came to me, and told me I must tell the whole truth, and the whole of the statement, what I
knew about it—that was in the Haymarket, after I had left home, while I was concealed at No. 32.
Q. Did you see any thing that Alice Abrahams brought to your house that day? A. No—I did not see the gold melted—I saw it after it was melted—it had been melted in my absence—I had heard of the gold dust robbery two or three days before—it did not occur to me at that time that that gold was part of the gold stolen—it did at the time I had the summons—I was in the Strand at that time—I do not know where the great mass of the gold was at that time—I never knew my father melt large quantities of gold, and put it into Dean's clothes-box—I never knew where these five masses of gold were—I never saw it till I saw it at Lambeth-street—I did not suppose it was the gold dust until I was summoned to Lambeth-street—it did not strike me as strange getting the notes and handing them over to Mrs. Abrahams—when I came back from the City Mrs. Abrahams was in the drawing-room—I do not know what she did with the Bank-notes and gold—I left the room—Mrs. Abrahams and my father were in the room when I left it.
Q. You heard a good deal of this affair while concealed in the Hay-market, I suppose? A. I did not hear any thing—I only saw Mr. Abrahams one evening—we had money with us to live there—it was my brother's money, not my father's—I never wrote to my father or communicated with him in any way while I was in the Haymarket—my sister was in the Strand at that time.
Q. Had you any manner of doubt, when that gold came into your father's house, that it was part of the stolen gold dust? A. No; I did not think at all about it—I never knew any body bring such a parcel as that at once and have it melted—I did not see any more brought in that day—I have known persons bring considerable quantities of gold to melt at once—not 100 oz.—perhaps a person in the trade has brought it merely to be melted and have an assay—I did not see this gold—I only saw one bar—I never knew 500 l. worth of gold brought to my father's premises, and left there—I have been living in Coventry-street lately—I eat, drink, and sleep there always—my mother is living in Charles-street—I do not live with her—a servant lives in the new place in Hanway-yard—I have seen the place—it is not fitted up with furnaces—there is no place for them—there are no furnaces in Coventry-street—I have never known people come in and put things into the crucibles immediately—I never knew such a thing before—it is the first thing that ever occurred to my knowledge, with the exception of dealers who come to sell—I mean by dealers, shop-keepers who purchase broken gold and silver—I never knew of any body else—not gentlemen that walk about in the evening—my father melts up watch-cases and seals and things—he is in the habit of doing that—he has melted up such a quantity as would nearly make one of these wedges—I do not think there was such a stock in the shop the day Mrs. Abrahams came as would make up such a mass as that.
Q. Therefore if any man has sworn that the quantity of gold then made up was made out of watches and things in the shop, that man is wickedly perjured, is he not? A. Yes; I should say so.
Q. You would say so, even if he were your own father? A. That is according under what circumstances—a man is perjured if he swears false—I never saw my father to have any private communication with him from the time he was in confinement till now—I have seen Mr. Abrahams, the attorney,
four or five times since I gave him instructions for my deposition—I did not see him to-day before I came into court—I saw him yesterday—he did not tell me what was saying or what I had to say—he did not tell me to stick to my deposition—he only said they wanted the truth and nothing but the truth.
Q. Have you seen Mr. Abrahams's son about this matter? A. Only this morning—that was not when my father was under examination—I did not see him afterwards, till I saw him here in court—I cannot say whether he is taking notes—my brother Selim has been walking about, sometimes alone, and sometimes in the passage—I have not seen any of the Abrahams with him—my sister has not been walking about—she it waiting outside—I have not seen Mr. Abrahams, or any one else, to tell me what was going on in Court to-day.
