MR. BODKIN. Q. Is that the 300 l. note you took to the Bank? A. Yes—that is my writing on it—I wrote that on it when I got it changed—I have written on it, "H. Solomon, Foreign Money Exchange Office, 53, Strand"—( this was No. 15.)
Q. You have been asked How many times you went out that day, and How long you were gone each time; did you look to any clock or watch, or are you speaking to the best of your memory? A. To the best of my memory—when Mrs. Abrahams came in, she went straight on to the back shop—a person coming in the way she did, could see before them the room in which the furnaces were—I did not know her name then, but I noticed her person—I was told her name at Lambeth-street—persons pointed her out—I knew her person when I saw her there—I did not see any thing she brought—I saw nothing in her hands—Moses looked at the summons when I showed it to him—he did not tell me he could not read or understand it—Mr. Abrahams told me there was a mistake in my deposition about the sovereigns for the cheque—he told me that he had ascertained at the bankers' that it was part sovereigns and part notes.
SELIM SOLOMON . I am the eldest son of Henry Solomon, who has been examined to-day—I manage my father's business in Coventry-street—I used to sleep in the Strand—I remember Easter Wednesday, I believe it was the 3rd of April—my brother Isaac came for me that day, and I went to my father's, in the Strand—I found my father there—he gave me two bars of gold, and gave me directions where to take them—I took them to Messrs. Bult, the gold refiners in Cheapside—I saw Messrs. Bult when I got there—the bars were weighed—the weight was upwards of 400 ounces, to the best of my recollection—it was to be 77 s. 9 d. per oz. standard—I received 1200 l. in the whole on those two bars—I had a cheque on Messrs. Robarts—I went there with the cheque, and received eleven£100 notes and 100 sovereigns—I returned to the Strand and found my father there—I gave him that money—to the best of my belief my father was in the shop when I went back—I went into the parlour—while I was there the prisoner Alice Abrahams came there—I was sitting in the parlour when she came up the stairs—the parlour is up stairs, and is a back room—Mrs. Abrahams went into the drawing-room, which is the front room on the same story as the parlour—my father followed her into the room, and I saw my father give her six£100 notes and 100 sovereigns—they were the same notes I had given my father—to the best of my recollection it was about ten minutes after I gave them to him that he gave them to Mrs. Abrahams—
I remember the Saturday when my father appeared to a summons—on the Thursday before that I heard of some one being taken up on this robbery, and discharged—in consequence of hearing that, I went, between ten and eleven o'clock at night, to the prisoner Money Moses' house—I went without my father's knowledge—I found the prisoner Money Moses there—I told him I came in consequence of feeling very uneasy, having in the course of that day heard that the Caspars were taken, and I then asked him whether I could have these notes back—he said, "Don't make yourself a child, for neither my name nor your father's name will be brought in question"—I then asked him whether I could have those notes back that he had—he said I could—I then told him I had come without the knowledge of my father, and with that I left—that is all that transpired—on the Saturday before my father went to Lambeth-street, he gave me a£100 note—I do not know what£100 note it was—he gave me some directions about it, in consequence of which I took it and gave it to John Dean, to get it changed for sovereigns—I received 200 sovereigns from Dean that night, not at two distinct times, I received 100 sovereigns with another 100 sovereigns—I took those 200 sovereigns to Money Moses' house, in Vinegar-yard, that Saturday night, between ten and eleven o'clock—I saw Moses, and gave him the 200 sovereigns—he gave me two£100 notes—to the best of my belief they were the same notes as had been given to Alice Abrahams—Moses told me they were the same notes—when I gave him the 200 sovereigns I asked him for the two£100 notes—I gave those two notes which I got from Moses to my father—I went to Moses' house again at different times in the course of the following week—I was there two or three times—I went to receive the notes, by my father's directions—I saw Moses when I got there, and told him what I had come for, to receive those notes back, and I would give him sovereigns for them—I went to him by my father's directions, and received of him these notes—I received in all, six£100 notes, and 75 l. In£5 notes—I gave Moses sovereigns in return—after I got these notes I went again to Moses' house—he was not at home—I saw Alice Abrahams—I asked her whether I could see her father, she said he was out—we then got into conversation respecting the Caspars, and in the course of conversation I asked her whether the I O U was safe—she said yes it was, she had given it to her sister in a piece of paper, with some other notes, sealed up—I then asked her whether she was sure she was not watched, when she brought the gold dust to our house—she said, "No, I am sure I was not, and I will tell you the reason for why; I have an old sweetheart of the name of Jessop; I asked him to lend me a cupboard, to put some things in, which I intended to send to my sister in the country; and it was there that I put the gold dust," and that she brought it to our house from there—during our conversation the name of Davis was mentioned—she mentioned his name—she said she was in the house when the officers were there to search it, when some jokes passed with them, and she gave them a glass of rum—on Monday, the 15th of April, I received a message from John Dean, my father's servant—in consequence of the message I received from him, I went, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, to Money Moses' house—I found Mrs. Abrahams there, standing before the bar down stairs—she took me up stairs into a room, and told me she must have those bars back—I said, "No, you can't"—she then said that if she had them back she thought she could settle it with the prosecutors—I then asked her whether her father would give up
the money that he had received—she said, "No, I must have the bars, and nothing but the bars," or something to that effect—she said nothing more, and I left.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Do you still carry on your father's business in Coventry-street? A. I do—I was not in the Strand on Monday, the 2nd of April, to my recollection, nor on Tuesday, to my recollection—I always sleep there—I go there between nine and ten o'clock at night—I have nothing at all to do with the business there—I have no share in the business there or in Coventry-street—I took the two bars to Messrs. Bult's on the Wednesday—my father gave them to me—he did not tell me that he suspected or believed they were stolen—he told me they were gold dust—he gave me no reason to suppose he believed they were stolen at that time—I have no recollection of any thing of the kind—I do not recollect his intimating that they were stolen—I recollect his stating to me that these bars contained gold dust.
Q. Did you at once believe that it was the gold dust stolen? A. I had my suspicions, not that my father believed it to be stolen, but I had my suspicions—I do not know whether my father suspected it or not—I did not ask him any questions at all about it.
Q. You believed they were the stolen gold dust when your father said they contained gold dust? A. I had my suspicions—I cannot say I believed it—I had my suspicions, that is the short of the matter—my father merely said they contained gold dust—I immediately suspected it was the stolen gold dust—I had no such suspicion before my father said they were gold dust—no further conversation took place between me and my father about it, that I recollect, at any time—I do not recollect that he told me he had disposed of a bar or two before—I think I heard something of the affair of a bar being melted from my father—I do not know whether he told me so—I did not say I heard it from my father—I did not hear it from my father—I heard something about it in the house—I am not sure whether it was my father, my brother, or John Dean that told me about this other bar—I have not sworn it was not my father who told me—I am not certain who it was I heard it from—it might have been from my father—that was all that I heard from any body, that there had been a bar of gold sold—I heard nothing further—it was on Wednesday that I took the bars to Messrs. Bult's—to the best of my recollection, it must have been about eleven or twelve o'clock in the day—it was usual for my father to send for me when he had any important transaction, instead of employing my brother Isaac—I did not know Moses more than I might a common stranger, that is all, and Mrs. Abrahams the same—he has never been to our house before—I knew his name—I knew the man, but never spoke to him—he was no acquaintance— Money Moses is the name he generally passes by—I knew him by that name—I did not know him well—I do not know whether my father and brother had any transaction with him—I do not know that my father dealt with him—I do not know that he did not—I only slept in the house, and had my supper there—I do not know whether my father had any dealings at all with him, or whether he had known him for years—I did not know Mrs. Abrahams' name, when I saw her first I knew afterwards, but I had not the slightest idea who she was then—I did not know who Davis was—she talked to me about him—I did not mention a word about him myself—I never saw the Caspars in my life, to my knowledge—I knew their names, by hearing that two persons named Caspar were
apprehended—I did not know their names at all before—I was very much alarmed when I heard they were taken—I was not present when my father was examined first at Lambeth-street—I cannot recollect what day I was examined—I recollect that I was summoned and was examined—my father was then in custody, and was at the bar at the time.
Q. Had you given an account of the transaction before that to any body? A. My father's solicitor came to me, and desired me to tell him all the facts that were in my knowledge—my brother was present at the time—it was at our apartment in the Haymarket—we had taken apartments there to be there—we certainly had reasons for doing so—we left our home by the advice of our solicitor, and went to the Haymarket, in order to be out of the way—I made the statement to the solicitor before I went before the Magistrate—I do not know whether it was for the purpose of being handed to the solicitor for the prosecution—I was not told that at all—he told me he had hopes perhaps of my father being admitted as a witness, and then I made a statement which was taken down—I suppose that was for the purpose of seeing whether my father could be made as a witness—I believe it was—my brother and John Dean were present when I made my statement—we had all kept out of the way together—I and my brother have lived in Coventry-street since we went before the Magistrate, and have carried on business there.
Q. Did you receive, about a week ago, a copy of your deposition? A. I have had my deposition—I had it about a fortnight or three weeks ago—my father's solicitor gave it to me—I really do not know who was present—I have no recollection—I have had it ever since—perhaps I have looked at it—I do not know, I am sure.
COURT. Q. Do you know whether you have looked at it or not? A. I think I did once or——
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Finish your sentence. A. Once—I am not sure whether I looked at it once or twice—it was the statement that I made to Mr. Abrahams—it is just the same as my deposition before the Magistrate—just the same as I have got—it is a copy of the deposition, but it is exactly word for word the same as the statement—I am not sure that I looked at it more than once—I am positive that I looked at it once—that was the day he gave it me—I am not positive sure that I have looked at it from that time to this—I might have looked at it once after that—I have not looked at it to-day—I have not looked at it within this week—I do not know whether it is a fortnight or three weeks ago—I am sure I have not looked at it this week—I have not got it in my pocket now, nor have I left it over at the New Inn—I have not looked at it to notice whether the date of my examination was put there.
Q. Did Mr. Abrahams tell you why he gave it to you? A. He told me to keep in my recollection what I had stated before the Magistrate—my father did not tell me where Moses lived when I went there—I knew the house myself—I do not know whether it was always ten or eleven o'clock at night when I went there—it was generally at night that I went, but I do not know what time exactly—I spoke to Moses at the bar—the house was generally empty when I was there—he never spoke to me while persons were there—he never addressed me while any one was there—it seems he knew me—I knew him by seeing him about—the house was generally empty when I was there—now and then there may have been a person there—it is right opposite Drury Lane theatre—I saw his wile at the house, but that is all.
Q. You say Mrs. Abrahams said the gold was at the house of a man named Jessop—did she say Jessop kept his bed all day there? A. She did—I do not know whether that was was to take care of the gold—she merely stated to me, that it was singular that he kept his bed all that day.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHOS. Q. Now when you went to this house in Vinegar-yard, you found Mr. Moses and his daughter, Mrs. Abrahams, there? A. I do not know whether she was there—I do not recollect having seen her there—she was alone at the house when I had this conversation with her—I did not know her at all before this—she entered into this conversation with me of her own accord, and told me all I have stated—we got into conversation, and that was How it occurred—this was long before I went as a witness to Lambeth-street—I have no recollection when I was first examined at Lambeth-street—I was only examined there once, and my deposition was taken before the Magistrate in a private room—I was examined publicly and privately—I was not examined publicly twice.
Q. Who examined you privately? A. I went into a private room, and my deposition was read over to me, I suppose by the clerk—there were some gentlemen there—I do not know who they were—I suppose they were the Magistrates—I saw them afterwards sitting as Magistrates—that was the way in which I was examined—a written paper was produced and read over to me as my statement, and I signed it—I did not swear to it there, it was taken into the office afterwards, and I swore to it there on the same day—I do not know whether it was drawn up by Mr. Abrahams—Mr. Abrahams took my statement as to facts, and that was the statement that was read to me—I signed it at the private examination, not before—I am sure, to the best of my recollection, that I was not examined a second time at the office.
Q. Did you, in the first statement you made in the private room, include every thing you have said to-day? A. No, I did not—I have no recollection of the date on which I was examined—I have now a recollection that I was examined a second time publicly, respecting my having gone to Money Moses's on the Monday evening—I had not said any thing about that at the private examination—I had mentioned about my going to his house before, but not about my going on Monday evening the last time—I did not mention before my second examination about Mrs. Abrahams saying she must have the bars, and nothing but the bars—that had taken place before I was examined at all—it was while I was in the Strand, before I had absconded—I did not say any thing about baring seen Mrs. Abrahams on Monday evening, before my second examination—I do not think my second examination was so long as twelve or thirteen days after the first—I do not remember How long it was—it was not the next day—I do not know whether it was within a week—I have no recollection at all—whether one or two Sabbaths passed in the mean time I do not know—I might have seen Mr. Abrahams two or three times in the mean time—I did not see him every day—I was not doing any thing while living in the Haymarket—I lived there about three weeks—I took money of my own there with me—I am in my father's employment—my brother and Dean had money likewise—I had to pay various expenses—I never inquired what they had—I paid my share—I have no recollection that I paid all—it was all paid—I did not pay it—it was sent to the parties—I do not know who paid it—I did not pay any—I did not pay my share—I
do not know whether Mr. Abrahams the attorney paid it—he came to us while we were there—I do not know that he read over our depositions to us—the first time he came he took our statements—he gave us copies of our statements afterwards.
Q. Did you ever write to your father, or see him in the whole of this time? A. I wrote to him once—I do not recollect the time—it was before he was examined—it was before my statement was taken, or any thing occurred—I do not know whether my brother, or Dean, wrote to him—I never went to see him, or wrote to him by Mr. Abrahams, nor did any of us to my knowledge—I am now living in Coventry-street with my brother—I believe my brother, John Dean, and Mr. Abrahams, were present when my statement was taken—whether he examined me for the purpose of getting my father admitted as a witness I do not know—he told me he had hopes of my father being admitted as a witness.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Now, before you went into the Magistrate's private room, had you stated to your attorney, or your father's attorney, all that you knew? A. All except my going to Moses the last day, on Monday evening, when Mrs. Abrahams spoke to me about the bars—he took down from my mouth what I had to say—he told me to state all that I knew, for he had hopes that my father would be admitted as a witness, if all he had told was fully corroborated.
Q. At that time, had you seen or heard the statement that your father had either made or was to make? A. Never—I never had any communication directly or indirectly with my father on the subject—I detailed to Mr. Abrahams all the facts and circumstances that I then recollected—I did not at that time recollect the fact of my going on the last Monday to Moses's house—Mr. Abrahams read over the statement to me which he had taken down, before he took it away—when I went before the Magistrate I was not accompanied by my attorney—Mr. Abrahams was in the Magistrate's room with me—I am not sure that I saw him hand my statement to the Magistrate—I suppose it was the clerk read the statement to me—when I was examined publicly in the office it was by questions put to me by you—my examination was taken down by the clerk at the desk—after I had finished my examination, it was read over to me by the clerk, and I signed it.
( Adjourned.)
Friday, June 21 th, 1839.
JOHN DEAN . Easter Tuesday last I was in the service of Henry Solomons, of the Strand. I remember going on that day for ray master to Messrs. Cock and Johnson's—I returned about one o'clock, as nearly as I can recollect—when I returned, my master was in the workshop—the prisoner Mrs. Abrahams was with him—there were two furnaces alight, and a crucible in each—I saw Mrs. Abrahams give him some lumps of gold—he put them into a crucible—when it was melted, Mrs. Abrahams went out into the front shop—I did not observe any thing done between her and my master in the front shop, if any thing was done—I do not know How long she staid in the front shop before she left—she came back in about half or three-quarters of an hour, and brought more gold, which was melted in the same way as the other—Mrs. Abrahams was present in the workshop at the time of its being melted—after it was melted she went into the front shop, and went away again—she came, in the course of that day, four or
five times, I am not certain—after the larger pieces were melted, she produced some smaller ones from a bag or pocket which she had fastened round her waist—she said that was for herself—I melted that—after it was melted I gave it to her, and she took it into the front shop—my master was there with her—she gave me half-a-sovereign—after she was gone we remelted some of the gold with about seventy ounces of silver that night, and made it into a bar—it was put into an iron chest—previous to this, one bar had been melted, alloyed with some silver-gilt lace—I think I should know the bar into which the seventy ounces of silver was put, if I was to see it—( one produced)—yes, this is the bar, but it has been marked and cut since I gave it to Mr. Lea—before this bar was melted with the seventy ounces of silver it weighed about 270 ounces—the bar which was remelted with the silver-gilt lace was sent to Messrs. Cock and Johnson by my master's directions—Isaac Solomon took it—next morning, Wednesday, we melted six more bars—I mean Mr. Solomon and myself—some large pieces of Russian copper coin were put into them, which would alter the colour of the gold—Selim Solomon was afterwards sent with two of them to Bult's, in Cheapside—the other four were put into an iron chest, the same as the bar of silver was put into—that would make live bars—in consequence of something which passed between me and my roaster, I took charge of that gold—it was taken from the iron chest, and I put it into a chest belonging to myself—I placed the chest in the top room of the house, and left it there—I went away about a week or ten days after—when I went away, as far as I know, I left it in that place—I remember, after the two bars were sent to Cock and Johnson, Mr. Clark came to my master—he is a clerk to Messrs. Bult—on the Friday following Mr. Solomon sent me to Cock and Johnson's with a message—I delivered the message, and received an answer—I went to Messrs. Bult two days after the bar was sent there, if I recollect right; no, I believe it was on the next day, Thursday, I was sent there with a message by my master.
Q. Now, upon that day did you see Mrs. Abrahams? A. That is a mistake in the depositions, and I wish to have it corrected—I will tell you what I said before the Magistrate—that upon the day on which Selim went to Messrs. Bult, upon his return I saw Mrs. Abrahams in the shop—when I was summoned before the Magistrate, the deposition was read over to me—I mentioned to Mr. Gunter that it was not when I returned from Messrs. Bult's, but when Selim returned from Bult's, that I saw Mrs. Abrahams—Selim is the person who took the two bars—that was the occasion on which I saw her—it was in my master's shop on Easter Wednesday—she asked for Mr. Solomon—I told her she would find him up stairs—she went up—I remember the Saturday on which my master went to the police office—a summons had been left for him on that Saturday—I received a £100 note from Selim Solomon on that Saturday—I received no directions what to do with it, only to get it changed—when he gave it to me he told me what to do with it—in consequence of those directions I went to Messrs. Ransom's to get sovereigns for it—I sent it by George Wood, one of my master's servants—Wood brought me back one hundred sovereigns for it—I put them into a bag, and gave them to Selim Solomon with another 100 the same evening—in the course of the next week I changed several other notes for gold by Mr. Solomon's direction—I know the person of the prisoner Moses—I saw him at my master's shop on
Easter Tuesday—it was in the forenoon, about 11 o'clock—that was before I went to Cock and Johnson's—I was in the workshop when I saw him—on Sunday, the 14th of April, I saw Mrs. Abrahams in master's shop in the Strand between 11 and 12 o'clock at night—she asked to see Mr. Solomon—I told her that he and all the family were gone to bed, but if she wished to see him particularly I would call him up—she said, "No"—I asked her if any thing was the matter—she said, "No"—she then told me to tell young Solomon to be at her house between seven and eight o'clock next morning—I delivered that message accordingly to Selim Solomon—I saw him go out next morning (Monday) between seven and eight o'clock—I know these two assay papers—( Nos. 12 and 13 produced by Mr. Bult)—the top writing on them is mine—the other is Mr. Johnson's, of Maiden-lane, the assayer—he is a different person to Messrs. Cock and Johnson—( read, "H.S., No. 2, 2nd April, 1839, double gold assay"—that is my writing as far as that—this "W 4 1 5/8" means "worse 4 carats 15/8"—that is Mr. Johnson's writing—that means less standard, or worse than standard 4 carats 1 gr. 5/8—this "No. 1 H.S., 3rd April, 1839, double gold," on the other assay paper is my writing—the "W 4 2-1/8" means worse 4 ounces 2 grains, and 1/8—they have each Mr. Johnson's signature to them—On Monday, the 8th of April, I remember obtaining some sovereigns from Messrs. Bult's—there were from one hundred to one hundred and twenty, as near as I can recollect—I got some from Messrs. Ransom's on the same day—I do not recollect getting any more at any other place that day—during that week I got some from the shop in Coventry-street—I do not recollect How much gold I got from Ransom's—I got it from various cheques—I do not recollect what I got from Coventry-street—it was by Mr. Solomon's directions that I got this gold—I changed notes with the trades people in that week—with three or four people—I remember taking a £100 note to Ransom's during the week beginning Monday, the 8th—I took one there to get change, and one to pay in to Mr. Solcmon's account—one was changed for notes and sovereigns.
Q. I do not know whether you are able to speak positively to the bars or not, but are these other four bars such as you took from your roaster's? A. Yes—I will not undertake to swear to them, as they have been out of my possession—they varied in size as these do, but I made no mark on them—I believe them to be the same.
COURT. Q. Do you say the same of the bar alloyed with silver? A. Yes.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You speak positively to the silver bar? A. No, I will not undertake to swear positively to either bar, as they have been out of my possession.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Where were you last night? A. After I left the court I went home with Selim and Isaac Solomon—not a word was said about what transpired yesterday—I continue in their employ—I was the general melter to Mr. Solomon—he melted a great quantity of watch cases, outsides and insides—it is safer to melt than to sell them—Mr. Solomon had a great deal to do in melting—not to the amount of 10,000 l. a year—we used to send what we melted to Messrs. Bult's, and Cock and Johnson's—I do not know to what amount we sent them—it was not 8 or 10,000 l. a year—I should think about half that.
Q. Did he send any thing to Messrs. Bult's except what he melted? A.
Certainly—the business we do with Bult's is notes and coin and a few bars—it was quite an extraordinary thing to send two or three bars to this extent at one time to Bult's—when I returned from Messrs. Bult's, on 2nd of April, Mrs. Abrahams was in the workshop—Mr. Solomon put the gold in in my presence—not in both the crucibles, only in one—I saw that it was gold dust—I had heard of the loss—I did not suspect what it was at first—I did not suspect till after it was all brought, when I saw the quantity—I did not know what weight there was—I could see it was large bars—I could tell there was more than thirty ounces when I saw it continually coming—when I saw there was such a quantity I suspected it was the gold that was lost, when I saw it come so many times and there was such a large bulk—I had no suspicion when I saw Mrs. Abrahams with the one hundred and ten ounces—I thought it was the ordinary course of business—I had never seen Mrs. Abrahams before—I staid in the workshop all the day from time to time when she came—I saw her come from time to time—I went out of the workshop after it was all done—it might be about six or half-past six o'clock—Mr. Solomon was with me all the afternoon—he was in and out of the workshop all the afternoon—he was not in the workshop all the afternoon—I do not recollect seeing Isaac in the workshop—I saw him in the front shop—he generally attended in the front shop, but he went into the city, and Mr. Solomon was obliged to attend when he was in the city.
Q. How often was Mr. Solomon in the workshop with you? A. Every time Mrs. Abrahams came Mr. Solomon came into the workshop and put the gold into the crucible—he did not always do that—he did that afternoon—I never melted any thing of the kind before—I never melted gold dust—I never melt any thing but for respectable dealers whom I know—when I saw Moses in the shop, he was standing with his back against the wainscot, looking across the counter—I was at the furnace at the time—there was a crucible in the fire, but I was not looking in the furnace—I was looking towards the door—it was my business to attend to the furnace at that time—I had seen him once before—he was not known to Mr. Solomon—he came there with a bottle of spirits once—Mr. Solomon did not deal with him that I know of—I do not think I saw him in the shop more than half a minute or a minute at the outside—I do not mean when he came with the spirits, but when he came on the Tuesday—there were two doors between me and where he was standing—there is a door to the workshop, and between the workshop and passage there is a little parlour—there is a door to that pars lour—there were three doors between us, but neither of them were shut—there were two doors, and one besides—if I had told you there was only one and there were two, I should have said false—I did not remember at the moment—we leave all the doors open—that was the usual course, except when the chimneys smoked, and then we closed them—I should think it is nearly 90 feet from the further end of the workshop to the door in the Strand—you can see the door in the Strand from the furnace—I swear that—a person in the Strand could not see what was going on at the furnace, for the furnace is not so high as we are, but they could see the furnace alight—the shop-door leading from the street is generally kept shut—the back parlour is not used for any thing particular—any lumber is put in it—there is a counter there, on which we brush clothes or so sometimes—I never knew Mr. Solomon take a person in there for private conversation—I do not recollect that there is any fire in the workshop, except the furnaces
—there is no place for a fire in the workshop, except the furnaces—the chimneys smoke when we are burning sweep—I should think the parlour is about fifteen feet wide, but I never measured it and cannot say—I should not think the passage is quite so wide as the parlour—I do not know whether the shop is about nineteen feet wide—I cannot say whether it is wider than the parlour—it might be a foot or so longer—the shop I should say is a little longer than the parlour—( looking at the plan)—this is not correct—the furnace ought to be on this side—the furnace is opposite the door—when you enter the room the furnace is right before you—it is at the furthest end of the workshop opposite the door—there are four furnaces—they are against the wall at the end—they are built against the wall on the left hand—they join the wall on the left hand—they are not straight against the wall—they do touch the wall in one way—the side of them are against the wall, but the back is against the wall in front of you—they do not touch the wall on the right hand—there is no furnace near the door on the left hand—the furnaces come out about two or three feet from the wall—when you first enter the shop, there is a door between the shop and passage, then another door to the parlour, and then another door into the workshop—I dare say the whole of the premises, from the street door to the end of the furnaces, is between eighty and ninety feet—I was in the workshop when I saw Moses—it was my business to attend to the crucibles, but I was not looking at them at the time—if I had been attending to them, my back would have been to the front shop—I think there were some other people in the front shop at the time, but I did not see any one distinctly—I could not see all over the shop—had Mr. Moses stood against the counter, I could not have seen him, but he stood against the wall and looked over the counter—I believe there was somebody else in the shop, but I would not undertake to swear it—I cannot swear I did see any body distinctly—I cannot say positively that I saw any body else—I do not recollect seeing any body there—I cannot swear there was any body there—I have no recollection of seeing any body else there besides Mr. Moses—I have no recollection whether I saw any one else there or not.
Q. You have told us there was a mistake in your deposition, have you seen your deposition since? A. I have—Mr. Abrahams showed it to me in Coventry-street—I was there with the young Solomon—they were both there—he gave us all three our depositions—that is about a week ago—he did not point out to me any mistake—I did not hear him say any thing to any of the others, or see him point any thing out—I did not hear him say any thing to either of the others, I do not recollect it—I do not know whether he said any thing or not—I have seen him since—I saw him yesterday morning, not since—I saw him coming to the Old Bailey yesterday morning—I was sitting in the passage with Selim and Isaac Solomon—I made a statement to Mr. Abrahams before I went before the Magistrate—I do not recollect reading it over there—it was read over to me at the Magistrate's at Lambeth-street—that was the statement Mr. Abrahams took down—I signed it before the Magistrate in the private room—I am not certain of that—I do not recollect distinctly that I signed it in the Magistrate's private room, but I signed it before the Magistrate, when it was read over to me by a Mr. Gunter—I do not know one gentleman from another—I heard Mr. Gunter's name mentioned by Mr. Clarkson this morning, but I did not know the gentleman—I did not sign the same paper that was read over to me in the private room—the one
signed was at the examination that was taken down when I was examined by Mr. Clarkson—I do not know whether that was the same I signed in the private room—it is similar to that—it was read over to me before I signed it—I swore to the statement I made.
COURT. Q. Which was it you swear to? A. The statement I made before the Magistrate, and which I signed.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. And in that statement which you signed and swore to, it was stated that when you returned from Messrs. Bult you saw Mrs. Abrahams? A. It was—I asked the officer to correct that statement, and he heard me—it appears he did not do so, but I did not know it—I was sworn before he read it over to me, and when he read it over, I signed it—I believe he said, "I will alter it"—but it appears he did not—I asked him to correct it—I do not recollect whether I signed it or not—I signed it after it was read over to me, but I do not recollect whether I signed it before or after I told him to correct that statement.
Q. Did you sleep in the house of Mr. Solomon, in the Strand? A. I did—I was present when the house was searched the first time—I did not go up stairs—I have no room—I slept in the shop under the counter—I had two boxes—one on the third floor, in which I kept my clothes, which was broken open, while I was away, and another in the top of the house, where I kept some clothes which belonged to my deceased mother—it was up four pairs of stairs in an attic, which we used entirely for lumber boxes and all kind of lumber—I was not present when the officers searched that room—my other box was broken open during my absence, or I would have opened it for them—I was present the first time when they searched the lower part of the premises, but I did not go up stairs—Roe went up to search—the gold was in one of the boxes—I would have opened that box for them if I had been up stairs—it was on the fourth floor where the gold was—I received a message from Mr. Solomon to give it up—Mr. Abrahams gave me that message—I had no communication with Mr. Abrahams from the time Mr. Solomon was taken up, till he came with my deposition—I received a message from him in the presence of Lea the officer through Mr. Abrahams to give up the bars to Lea—Mr. Abrahams, Mr. Solos omon's solicitor, delivered the message to me—Mr. Abrahams' son came to the lodging, and told me his father wanted to speak to me, and when I met him in the Strand he was with Lea the officer—he told me I was to give Lea the five bars in my possession, and I accordingly did so—I was out of the way at the time—I went down to the Strand from where I was concealed, leaving the two Solomons at the place—it was in the day-time, about eleven o'clock—I considered I was safe then, as I was told Mr. Abrahams wanted to speak to me—I was staying at the house of a Mr. Mosely, No. 32, Haymarket—he is no relation of Solomon.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How long have you been in the employ of Henry Solomon. A. Between eight and nine years—I am a Christian—I was never bound to Mr. Solomon by any article or indenture of any Kind—when I saw the gold dust come in such large quantities I had very strong suspicions it was the gold dust stolen—I had some doubt certainly.
Q. In other words, you did not see it stolen and brought there, you
had as much doubt as that came to? A. That is the fact—my suspicion was certainly strong enough to make me believe so, but I had no right to believe it was so—I had strong suspicion, that is the fact—I did not ask any question of Mr. Solomon about it—I went on melting—I had never melted things before which came in under suspicious circumstances—I never had any suspicion of any thing I melted before—there are three doors to our workshop—there is no place to go out into the street, except going through the shop—one door leads into a closet—you cannot get into the street from there—you can get into the open air from that closet—you go across the yard to that closet—you come out of the workshop and go into this back-parlour—there is a door out of that into a yard, and down that yard at the end there is a closet—that closet has no door outward—you can see the Strand from the workshop—you can see any body—any one who saw a person coming could go into that closet—any body who knew it—I suppose it was not communicated to every body—I am still with Mr. Solomon.
Q. Have you, or had you any doubt that he received this gold with a guilty knowledge? A. I did not know How he received it—I did not know what was paid for it, nor How he came by it—I only know it came there—I have not heard him say any thing on the subject—I knew it when he was examined at the police office—I continue in his service still—the attorney came to us in the Haymarket, and asked us to make statements to him—he did not tell me for what purpose at that time—he told me to state the facts, as far as I was concerned—he did not tell me to what use they were to be applied—he never told me what use they were to be aplied to until they came to the police office—he did not tell me it was to corroborate Mr. Solomon, who would not be believed if he was not corroborated—he told me it was to corroborate Mr. Solomon, but he did not tell me he would not be believed without—when I went to the private audience before the Magistrate, there were two or three gentlemen there—I do not know their names—I believe it was Mr. Hardwick and the clerks, and to the best of my recollection Mr. Abrahams was there—I do not recollect that there was another Magistrate there besides Mr. Hardwick—there were three or four gentlemen, but who they were I do not know—to the best of my knowledge Mr. Abrahams was there, but it is so long ago—I did not know it was important I should recollect—I cannot say How many persons there were—I do not distinctly recollect whether Mr. Abrahams was there or not, but to the best of my belief he was there—the two young Solomons were not there when I was there—they were waiting to go in when I came out.
Q. Had Mr. Abrahams taken down in writing what you were to say before you went there? A. When he came to Moseley's, in the Hay-market, what I stated he took down in writing—he took down what the others said also—that which he so took down was put into the hands of the Magistrate's clerk, and I was examined out of it, and it was read over to me in the private room—I do not distinctly recollect whether I signed it then in the private room, but I signed it before the Magistrate—I did not sign it when Mr. Abrahams took it down—I do not recollect that I signed that paper at all—if I did not sign it in the private room I did not sign it at all—not that statement.
COURT. Q. Confine yourself to the statement you made to Mr. Abrahams at Moseley's. A. I did not sign what he put down—I am not certain whether I signed it when it was read over to me before the Magistrate.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You did not sign it at Moseley's, and if you did not sign it in the private room you did not sign it at all? A. That is correct—I believe Mr. Humphreys was in the private room, but I am not certain—I do not recollect seeing any counsel for the prosecution there that I know. Mr. Clarkson examined me at the police-office—I am not certain that he was in the private room—I am not aware that any copies were made of my statement—how many Mr. Abrahams may have made I do not know—when I came before the Magistrate in the public room, I was asked questions about this transaction—they were to exactly the same matters as I had stated in the private room, and which were contained in that paper—it had been read over in the private room.
Q. And after you had rehearsed in the private room, and given evidence in the public room, still there was a statement which was not correct, and which the clerk did not correct? A. There was—I signed it as it was—I think I was about a quarter of an hour in the private room—I was not there three hours—I had been in the public office before that, about half an hour—I should suppose it was about an hour altogether, from the time the Magistrates went out till they came into Court again.
COURT. Q. You went into the private room to make the statement; at that time had you been sworn? A. I had not been sworn—I went back again into the public room to be sworn.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. There the questions were asked you again? A. There were no questions in the private room at all—questions were asked me in the office, to which I returned the same answers as I had given before—it was taken down as my statement.
COURT. Q. When you went back into the public room to be sworn, were questions put to you? A. Then questions were put to me to which I gave answers.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. And did the questions so put to you produce answers conformable to what you had stated before? A. They did; the questions, and the answers I gave, were the same in substance as what had been read over to me in the private room, and something in the same order—I was sworn before the clerk took down what I said—I was sworn to state the whole truth—I did not know what they were going to ask me—I had no business to guess—I could not tell what they were going to ask me—I was there to give an account of the business—I never went before the Justice before—I think it was on the 7th of May that I was first examined before the Magistrate—it was in the early part of May—I was examined again about eight or ten days after to the best of my recollection—I do not recollect whether I said any thing before the Justice in the private room about Selim Solomon going to Mrs. Abrahams in consequence of the message delivered to me—it was on Sunday night, the 14th of April, that she called—on the 7th of May, when I was examined, I did not say a word about that—I had told Mr. Abrahams of it before—I went a second time to the Justice—I was ordered to attend there again, and went—Mr. Abrahams did not tell me of it—I think Selim and I went together the day I was reexamined, to the best of my recollection—I went to state that I had omitted a fact concerning Alice Abrahams—I do not know what he went to do—I did not know what would be wanted of me till I was there.
Q. Where has Mr. Solomon taken a shop now? A. There is one taken in Hanway-street—I have been there—it is not fitted up with a furnace—
I do not know what shop it is to be—it is not open yet—there is no convenience for a furnace—the lumps of gold were found in a box of mine—I had not looked into the box from the time I put them there till I went to the box with Lea and Mr. Abrahams—I put them there the day they were re-melted—that was on the 3rd of April, when they were re-melted and alloyed—I never put any valuable property in that loft before myself, nor ever knew of any being put there—Roe came to search the house when Mr. Solomon was taken into custody—that was about a week or ten days afterwards—I knew what he came to search for.
Q. How came you, being an honest young man and a Christian not to conduct them up stairs, and say, "Here is what you are searching for?" A. Because I did not think proper—I certainly do not think it would have been honest to do so—it was given into my custody, and I did not think I had a right to give it to the officers—I saw the gold dust come to the house, and I had suspicions—I saw it melted and alloyed so as to disguise it—I then took it away, and concealed it in a box in the loft—there were stairs leading to that loft—I saw the officers come to search for that gold, but told them nothing about it—afterwards, when I was requested, I told Lea where it was, and conducted him to it—I gave it up at Mr. Solomon's request—Roe was not there at that time—the box was precisely in the same situation as where I had left it—the door of the loft was not locked or secured in anyway.
COURT. Q. You say you made a mistake, and desired to be corrected, on what day was that mistake made? A. The first time I was examined, on the 7th of May, in the private room—I went there from the Haymarket, where I was concealed—Mr. Abrahams came to me there a few days previous to my going before the Magistrate, and it was then I made the statement, which he took down—Mr. Abrahams came to me a week ago to leave the deposition.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I do not understand you rightly about Mr. Abrahams; you gave him a statement? A. Yes, and told him what I could prove—that statement was taken down in writing by him—it was that statement that was read over to me in the private room at Lambeth-street office—Mr. Hardwick asked me whether that statement was true or not—I stated that it was true—I was then taken into the public office, and sworn to it, and was examined to it at the public office in the presence of all—to the best of my belief, Mr. Coombe, the Magistrate, was there also.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Now, in the private room did the magistrate or any body else put any question whatever to you, or was any thing more done than your statement handed in by Mr. Abrahams, read over to you, and you asked if it was true? A. Nothing whatever—that was the statement which came from my lips—nobody suggested a single word to me on the subject—I did not, at the time I made it, know any fact that Mr. Solomon had said or was to say—I did not know at that time whether Mr. Solomon was to be examined as a witness or not—I have never seen Mr. Solomon since that time to have any conversation with him—the plan produced does not faithfully represent the situation of the furnaces.
JAMES LEA re-examined. I have been to the house of Solomon two or three times—I should say this plan is not a faithful representation of the siuation of the furnaces—you cannot see the furnaces from the back parlour—when you are inside the shop at the door you can see the furnaces,
and even outside the street looking through the glazed doors you can if the inner door is open—you can see the brick work of two of them—I do not say you can see the furnaces, because they are lower down—when you enter the workshop the furnaces face you exactly—they are neither right nor left I should say—there are four furnaces—I rather think the furnaces do not remain now, for when I was there five weeks ago the workmen were there—it is turned into a linen-draper's shop.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Do you know Mr. Hudson, the architect? A. No—the plan is correct in every respect except the furnaces, according to the best of my belief—if the furnaces were put here it would be correct—they project out two or three feet, and are at the very end of the workshop—here is where I should consider the furnaces would be—there are benches or something on the right hand, and a lot of coals—there is no furnace on the right—( the plan was here altered)—that would be correct as far as I can judge—I went up to the trunk or box where the gold was—it is a sort of lumber room or attic—it was covered over with deal boards, old bedsteads, and things—there was a great deal of lumber in the room—I could not see it till Deane removed some of the wood—I had not been in the room before, if I had I should have found it—I imagine Roe is an active officer—I cannot say whether he went into the room—he had to search the upper part of the house—I went no further than the second floor.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I think you said you remembered going about in a cab with old Caspar? A. In a coach—I said old Caspar got out somewhere about Charing-cross or the Strand, to the best of my recollection—as near as I can recollect it might be a quarter or twenty minutes past nine o'clock—it was not ten o'clock—it might be half-past nine o'clock—I should say it was after nine o'clock by the distance we went—we went from the Swan and Two Necks to the Southampton railway station—it was a few minutes past eight o'clock when we set out—we went direct to the station near Vauxhall—it is about three miles or three miles and a half—we then came back again to the coach office in the Regent-circus, Piccadilly, which would make it six miles—that is about two miles and a half—we went from there to the Strand—it was a two-horse hackney-coach—I cannot recollect How many miles an hour we went—young Caspar told the coachman to drive as fast as he could—I dare say we went six or seven miles an hour—the coachman went as fast as he could.
Q. How do you make it an hour and a half, if the coach went at the rate of six or seven miles an hour? A. We waited some time—young Caspar got out at the coach office at the Circus—he stopped there a few minutes—we stopped eight or nine minutes at Vauxhall-bridge—as near as I can tell, old Caspar left the coach about half-past nine—young Caspar went with me from the Strand to the coffee-house in St. Paul's church-yard—he got out there, and I got out with him, and we went into the coffee-house—to the best of my recollection I finally parted with young Caspar about half-past ten o'clock—I should think we staid an hour at least at the coffee-house—we went out of our road a little at Vauxhall-bridge, but only a short distance—I do not recollect what distance—it might take us five minutes out of the way—we stopped and made inquiry when we found we were out of the road.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. To the best of your belief this is a correct plan,
with the exception of their having placed the furnaces in an entirely different position? A. Yes.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I do not know whether you know Moss's home? A. Yes, I should say it was about a mile and a half from the Royal Exchange, and perhaps two miles and a half from Charing Cross—it is not a mile, I think, from Charing Cross to St. Paul's Church-yard—it may be three miles from Charing Cross to Moss's house—I should say it is a quarter of a mile beyond Whitechapel church—I should say it is two miles and a half from Charing Cross to Whitechapel church—that is my calculation—I will not swear that it is not three miles and a half.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You speak of distance to the best of your judgment and as to time? A. Yes—I do not say whether old Caspar got out at Charing Cross or in the Strand—I should think it would take a cab about twenty-five minutes to go from Charing Cross to New-street.
Q. Supposing a man to be standing at or near two of the furnaces, could he see a person standing by the wainscot of the shop? A. Certainly, he could see into the street—I went on purpose to try if it was possible, as I thought it would be a necessary question—as the plan was first marked you could not see the furnaces at all from the door, but they can be seen from the street—the brick work can.
COURT. Q. You could see a man standing at the wainscot from the furnaces? A. Yes, certainly.
MART SOLOMON . I am the daughter of Henry Solomon. I was at home in the Strand on the morning of Easter Tuesday, the 2nd of April last—I saw the prisoner Moses at my father's house that morning, in the sitting-room up stairs, where I was sitting—my father came there with him, and I retired from the parlour.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Did you return into the room after you retired? A. No, I did during the day—it was a back-room—I went into the front-room—I cannot exactly remember How long I staid there—I remained some, time—I returned into the sitting-room in the course of the day, but not while my father was there—I do not know exactly How long—it might be about an hour—I did not hear my father go away from the room, nor any one leave the room—I returned as soon as I thought they had gone away.
GEORGE WOOD . In April last I received a £100 bank note from Dean—I took it to Ransom's, the banker's, and got sovereigns for it—I gave the sovereigns to John Dean—it was on the day Mr. Solomon was summoned to appear at the police-office—it was on Saturday—I was in Solomon's service at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Are you so still? A. Yes.
BENJAMIN WOOLF . I am a tailor, living in Regent-street On the 2nd of April, Isaac Solomon came to my house—I gave him 100 l. In a cheque, upon Ransom's, and 20 l. In money—I was repaid on the following day, the 3rd of April, by this cheque—( marked No. 10)—it looks like this—I have not the cheque I gave Isaac Solomon—I have my books with me, but I destroy my cheques—the cheque has been returned to me, but I destroyed it—I have my book, with the margin of the cheque here.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. When was this cheque destroyed? A. Soon after it came back—I destroy all my cheques—when the cheques come back I look over them to see if they are right and destroy them—I destroyed this the morning it came back—the banker's clerk can
tell you when it came back—it was before I went before the Magistrate—I have my cheques back about every month, sometimes longer, sometimes shorter—I do not sign a book when I have them back—I should think it was before I was examined before the Magistrate—I will not swear, because I cannot tell—I had not the cheque in my possession the day I went before the Magistrate—I am a nephew to Solomon's.
GEORGE GRIFFITHS . I am clerk to Messrs. Ransom and Co., bankers, in Fall Mall. Benjamin Woolf, the last witness, banks at our house, on the 2nd of last April, we paid a cheque in favour of Henry Solomon, drawn by Woolf, for 100 l.
Q. Tell me whether among the notes you paid one of £5., No. 81, 406, dated the 3rd of March, 1838? A. I paid one of that number, but the date I did not take—I also paid a£5 note, No. 71, 343—( these two notes were numbered 16 and 17.)
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. This does not tell you who it was paid to? A. No, the draft was drawn in the name of Solomon—it does not say to whom it was paid.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Do you remember to whom it was paid? A. No.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. The entry is in your hand-writing? A. Yes, I paid it with my own hands.
ARCHIBALD DUNCAN RILEY . I am the son of Mr. James Riley, a solicitor, and am his clerk. I am acquainted with the hand-writing of the prisoner Moses—( looking at note No. 17—71342)—here is Moses' hand-writing—I see his name on the back of it—I have no doubt that it is Moses' hand-writing—( looking at note No. 16—81406)—this is also his hand-writing—they are both his hand-writing.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Do you know Mr. Abrahams, the attorney? A. Yes; I cannot tell who found me out—I had no communication with him on the subject of the hand-writing—I have seen him here—I should say it was eight years since I have seen Moses write—it was at my father's chambers, No. 17, Clement's Inn.
JOHN CHURCHER HEWLET . I am deputy prothonotary of the Palace Court. I have seen the prisoner Moses write once—I have had to swear him to affidavits very often in the course of my duty—it is impossible to say How often, several times I should say—I have by those means acquired a knowledge of his hand-writing—I think I should know it if I saw it—from the slight knowledge I have of his hand-writing, I believe the words "E. Moses" on these two notes to be his hand-writing.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. How many thousand affidavits do you swear in the course of a year? A. A great many more than I could count.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Just look again at that hand-writing, and tell me if it is usual or unusual? A. It is an unusual style of writing—perhaps, I should say, when I saw these two notes I put them by the side of one or two of his signatures.
HENRY WEST . I am clerk to Mr. Abrahams. I am acquainted with the hand-writing of the prisoner Moses—I believe the hand-writing at the back of these two notes to be his.
THOMAS VINE . I am assistant officer at the Palace Court. I am acquainted with Moses' hand-writing—I believe the writing at the back of these notes to be his.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. You are employed by Mr. Abrahams? A. I have been occasionally, to serve writs—I am not a general
runner for him—I have done very little business for him lately—I have been in jail I think twice—that is all, I swear—it was once for a debt, the other time for an assault—I have certainly been locked up once or twice for being drunk—I cannot say How many times—I have never been locked up for any thing else—I was never charged with picking pockets—I have never been in Newington jail ( looking at a paper)—this is not my name, my name is Vine not Vineing—all I have been in prison for was being drunk, for debt, and for an assault—only one assault—I have seen Moses write several times—I have been in the habit of serving writs for him occasionally for the last eight years by going with officers to arrest people—he has given me instructions in writing, and I have been with him to Mr. Abrahams's office at the Palace Court.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. As assistant officer it is part of your duty to serve writs? A. Yes, I am appointed especially for that purpose—I serve writs for other attorneys besides Mr. Abrahams, all the attorneys that practise in that Court—I never went by any other name than Thomas Vine—I never in my life went by the name of James Vining—I never knew such a person as James Esdale Whittaker—there is not the slightest pretence for saying I was in custody for picking a pocket or stealing of any kind.
PERCIVAL NORTON JOHNSON . I am in partnership with Mr. Cock, as gold-refiners, in Hatton-garden. I remember on Easter Tuesday last receiving a bar of mixed gold and silver—one of the young Solomons brought it—I do not know his name—the value of the bar was 500 l. odd—I gave the person who brought it a cheque for 400 l., drawn on the bankers Williams, Deacon, & Co.—I afterwards had the gold assayed—my cheque was returned to me—( looking at it, marked "No. 19")—this is the cheque I gave young Solomon—I had paid some money to one of the Solomons before on that morning—I do not know to which—I was in my lapidary, and Corfield, my clerk, sent up to me to sign a cheque for 60 l.—this is the cheque I signed ( marked "No. 20")—that was paid first in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Was that the case with both cheques? A. A silver bar was brought in the morning, and that was paid on account—I did not see the person who brought it—the second bar I took in myself, and saw the person who brought it—that was the 500 l. bar.
WILLIAM JOHN COCK . I am in partnership with the last witness. On Thursday, the 4th of April, Henry Solomon called on me, and I gave him a cheque on Williams, Deacon, & Co., for 127 l. 17 s. 6 d.—this is it ( "No. 21")—as balance for a silver bar and a parting bar, that is a bar containing gold and silver—that was the balance of the 400 l. and the 60 l., making together 587 l. 17 s. 6 d.
JAMES GILES . I am clerk to Messrs. Williams, Deacon, & Co., bankers Cock and Johnson bank there—on the 2nd of April this cheque of theirs for 400 l. (" No. 19") was paid at our house by another clerk named Thackery—he is away from indisposition—on the 14th of April I cashed this other cheque of theirs myself ( "No. 21") for 127 l. 17 s. 6 d.—I paid it in a note for 100 l. and I believe cash.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. You have the books here where it is entered in your own hand-writing? A. Yes—I am sure I paid a £100 note with my own hand—I paid it to Solomon.
JOHN SLY . I am clerk to Mr. Adamthwaite, notary to the house of Williams, Deacon, and Labouchere. I know this note ( looking at one
for 300 l., marked " No. 15")—I should think that this note was in their banking-house—I received it at Scott's, the banker's, on the 2nd of April, for Williams, Deacon, & Co.—I brought it to my employer to our office in St. Michael's Alley, and gave it to him—at that time Mr. Friend was his partner—he received the note, and he has gone away—he did live in Cullum-street, Fenchurch-street—Mr. Adamthwaite cannot give information where he is to be found—I did not see that note afterwards in the hands of Mr. Adamthwaite—I saw it at William's, the bankers, one day last week, when I was subpoenaed—last Wednesday week I think it was—one of the cashiers showed it to me—all I can say is, I received it from Scott and paid it to Mr. Friend, Mr. Adamthwaite's partner—I do not transact any of the business at the banking-house—I made a mark on the note, "Scott & Co., 2 day 4 month"—I delivered the note to Mr. Friend on the same day.
JOHN HAWKES . I am in the pay clerk's office, in the Bank of Enggland. This 300 l. note ( marked No. 15) was paid into the Bank on the 15th of April—I did not receive it with my own band, but my colleague did—I gave twenty 10 l. notes and twenty 5 l. notes for it—among the tens were these, which I will read out, "No. 36426, 16038, 30060, 30061, 9911, 22302, 4375, 36540, 36717, 10932, 18650, 14517, 12915, all dated 11th of January, 1839; among the fives were No. 91443, dated 3rd of May, 1838; 91123, the same date—( these were the notes given up by Moss.)
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. You utter notes again in a certain time, do you? A. We re-issue fives and tens—the dates alter, but the numbers are the same—there may be more of one number out, but with a different date—we never issue the same number with the same date—I have no entry of the 300 l. In this book—it was taken off the file, and I put my initials on it—I do not exactly know the day—it was when Mr. De Mole came to the chief cashier's office, and I put my initials on it as knowing it to be a note I had given change for, but I have no memorandum of it here at present—the transaction occurred on the 2nd of April—I have it here in the other witness's writing.
CUTHBERT GEORGE BROWN . I am one of the pay clerks in the Bank. This 300 l. note was presented to me on the 2nd of April, by a person of the name of Solomon—the notes Mr. Hawkes has read from his book were the notes given in exchange for the 300 l. note.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Does it appear in that book that these notes were given for that 300 l. note? A. Yes—the 300 l. note is not down at all—I have no book in which it is entered—I posted the notes on the 2nd of April, the day they were given—I saw Mr. Hawkes pay the notes to a person calling himself Solomon.
COURT. Q. Do you know Solomon by sight? A. No.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was it on that occasion you received this 300 l. note? A. Yes.
COURT to JOHN HAWKES. Q. Who did you pay these notes to? A. A person of the name of Solomon—I did not know him, I have seen him several times since I have been here on this trial—I could swear to him now, but I could not then.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is it the custom of the Bank for the person who exchanges a note to write his name and address on it? A. Yes—I should apprehend the name and address on this note was written before—I did not see it written—these initials, "J. H." on it, are my writing, when it was taken off the file—it was on the file of the cashier's office, at the Bank,
and was applied for by Mr. De Mole—I apprehend he applied on the first day of the examination at Lambeth-street—it was produced to me, I immediately saw that it was correct according to my account of the transaction of that day, and I put my initials on it—I paid the notes to a person calling himself Solomon—I gave the value for the note received by Brown in the notes that have been read.
COURT. Q. Is there any address on it? A. Yes; "H. Solomon, Foreign Money Exchange Office, 53, Strand."
MR. PHILLIPS to MR. BROWN. Q. How long have you known Ellis Caspar? A. Since 1818—he is a watchmaker—I purchased a watch of him, for which I paid him 51 l.—I recommended one person to him—in his dealing with me, I found him fair and honourable—I believe him to be an honest tradesman.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you any knowledge at all of his character for the last ten or twelve years? A. Not for so short a time.
GEORGE RICHARD JOHNSON . I am an assayer of metals, and carry on business in Maiden-lane, Wood-street. On the 2nd of April, some assays for Henry Solomon were made at our house—there were three assays tried for gold and silver both, and one of silver only—on the 3rd of April, there were two assays of gold made for the same party—in consequence of some message I afterwards received, I sent the two last assays to Mr. Bult, in Cheapside, by the desire of John Dean, Solomon's man—the writing on these two assay papers is mine—I believe they are what were sent to Mr. Bult.
JOHN DOWN . I am one of the clerks in the banking-house of Williams, Deacon, and Co. On the 2nd of April, I received this £300 note on account of that house—I have the memorandum—I received the note from Adamthwaite and Friend, our notaries.
EDWARD CLARK . I am clerk to Mr. Bult, of Cheapside. On the 2nd of April I made a payment to some person of Solomon's establishment—it was 124 l. 1 s., in one Bank-note of 100 l., No. 1888, dated 5th of February, 1839; four notes of 5 l., dated 12th of January, 1839, and the rest in cash—on the 4th of April, I paid 156 l. 17 s., in two 20 l. notes, three 5 l. notes, and the remainder must have been in cash, as I have no entry of that—on the 8th of April, I made a further payment of 111 l. 7 s. 7 d. In cash; on the 12th, I paid 100 l. 4 s. 10 d., in one note of 50 l.; two, of 20 l.; and one, of 10 l.—I think that was paid to the elder Mr. Solomon—I cannot recollect who I paid the others to.
PERCIVAL NORTON JOHNSON re-examined.Q. You say you received the second bar yourself? A. I did—it was a mixture of gold and silver—I received it of one of the young Solomons, but I cannot say which.
WILLIAM BLAXLAND . I am a clerk in the banking-house of Messrs. Robarts, Curtis, and Co. On the 3rd of April I paid a cheque of Mr. Bult's of £1200—I paid it in eleven £100 notes and 100 sovereigns—( cheque read)—"15, Lombard-street, London, 3rd of April, 1839. Messrs. Robarts, Curtis, and Co., pay No. 2394, or bearer, 1200 l. JOSEPH BULT, SON, and Co."—the notes were, Nos. 335, 2668, 1098, 1247, 3219, 2775, 3802, 1135, 1410, 2085, 2480, all dated the 5th of February, 1839.
THOMAS FARTHING . I am cashier in Ransom's banking-house. On the 3rd of April I received, on Henry Solomon's account, three notes of £100 each, Nos. 1098, 2688, and 335, dated the 5th of February, 1839—they were placed to his credit, and on the same day I exchanged another note for Henry Solomon for £100, No. 2480, dated the 5th of
February—I gave for that a £50 note, No. 1868, dated the 23rd of May, and £50 in gold—I do not remember who brought that note—On the 10th of April there was another £100 note paid in to the credit of Solomon—it was No. 1410, dated the 5th of February—on the 11th there was another £100 note, but that was not paid in to his credit, it was to take up a bill that was discounted by our house for Solomon—the No. of that note, was 2085, dated the 5th of February—there was a considerable balance upon that bill—I gave in change a £50 note, a £20 note, and the rest in cash—it was to pay a bill of 10 l. 5 s. only—I gave a balance of 89 l. 15 s.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Was that a bill on Solomon? A. No, a bill we discounted for Solomon—I cannot say who it came from—all these entries are in my hand-writing.
PHILIP EPWARDS . I am a clerk in the house of Ransom and Co. On the 8th of April I received three £100 notes on account of Mr. Henry Solomon, Nos. 3802, 2276, and 3219, all dated the 5th of February.
GEORGE THORN . I am cashier at Ransom's. On the 6th of April I exchanged a £100 bank note on account of Mr. Solomon—it was No. 1247, dared the 5th of February—I gave sovereigns for it.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Do you know who brought it? A. I recollect that it was brought by his servant, and can swear it.
MR. BODKIN. Q. On the 9th of April did you receive another £100 note on account of Solomon? A. Yes, it was No. 2775, dated February the 5th, 1839—I am not positive who brought it—I gave notes and sovereigns in exchange for it.
—WARNER. I am an out-teller in the Bank of England. This £5 note—( looking at one)—was paid into the Bank on the 14th of May.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Have you got the entry here? A. Yes—this is the out-telling book—here is an entry in it in pencil, which I made the other day from the book in the accountant's office in the Bank—I received the note myself from Robarts, Curtis, and Co.—here it is entered in this out-teller's book—here are seventeen fives entered with a tick—this is the only entry by which I know that I received it.
Q. Is there any mark in that book at all to distinguish one note from another? A. Yes, here is Dorrien's notes distinguished by a cross, and Robarts's have a tick—here is a tick on the note—I put a tick on them if I receive a good many—if I received a few I should perhaps put down the numbers.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Looking at this entry in this book, are you able to say that that day you received seventeen £5 notes from Robarts's house? A. Yes, looking at the mark upon the note, I can say that this is one of the seventeen.
COURT. Q. How does that mark enable you to say that this is one of the seventeen? A. I marked the notes of that house that day in that style.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. And you mark them so every day if you have a large number? A. Yes.
MR. BODKIN. Q. As you always put the same mark on the notes you receive from Robarts's, How are you able to say that you received this note on that particular day, the 14th of May? A. I marked my notes that way on that day—I always mark Robarts's notes that way.
Q. But, as you always put that same mark on Robarts's notes, How are you able to say that this was received that day? A. The party came about a week after to inquire about these notes, and that directed my attention to the file, and there I saw the seventeen notes.
Q. Had you, between the 14th of May and the day the person called and directed your attention to the file, any other £5 notes from Robarts's, which you had marked in the same way? A. No, sir, I had not—after I receive the notes they are paid to the clearer's office, and go to the cash book, and from there to the accountant's—the seventeen notes I received that day would be posted in the Accountant General's Office the next morning.
Q. Have you searched the books in the Accountant General's Office, in which these notes would be entered? A. I saw the file of notes, and there I saw the notes filed which had been received by me on the 14th of May, and this one was amongst them—I searched the file on the day the person called, about a week after the 14th of May, and upon that search I found this note amongst the seventeen notes.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Did I understand you that you put in this book the marks which you put on the notes? A. Yes, invariably so, so that if there is no mark in this book J put no mark on the notes, and if there is a mark I put a mark on the notes.
Q. Then you never put the tick on any but Robarts's? A. If I had the west-end walk, and received a number of notes from Coutts's, perhaps I should put the tick on them.
Q. Is there any tick in this book on Robarts's notes? A. No, there is no tick here—here is a tick on Dorien's notes on the 15th—this tick runs through the book to other bankers who pay a large amount—here is no tick to Robarts' on the 14th of May—that must have been an error of mine—business perhaps was heavy.
Q. When you searched the file, can you tell How many £5 notes were on it, might there have been twenty or one hundred notes? A. Oh. yes, more than that—they run in rotation, according to the ledger.
COURT. Q. There might be one hundred other £5 notes as well as the seventeen? A. Yes on the file.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Though you appear to have omitted to put the tick in the book, can you recollect from memory whether you put the tick on the notes that day on Robarts? A. To the best of my belief I did—there are notes of different amounts filed on that file—if a note is stopped we have a printed form to say who stopped it, and on such a day.
Q. Is there any thing in the method by which they are filed, which would enable you to go to the file and put your hand on the notes taken on a particular day? A. Not me, sir—the accountant shows it—it is his property—he keeps the file—there is no division of days on the file.
Q. You were told the note was stopped about a week afterwards, and you went to the file and found that note with several others, did you find seventeen notes marked in the way this is? A. Yes, and this was one of them.
COURT. Q. How are you enabled to go at once to these notes? A. By the date of the month that they told me I brought it in—I got my information from the secretary's office.
GEORGE FREDERICK BULT re-examined. Q. You spoke to the occasion of the cheque being given to Solomon about the bars of gold, did you send a message to Solomon about it? A. Yes, and I received a message from him in return—I did not get my money back.
COURT. Q. On what day did you send that message? A. On the 2nd of April, the day we received the bars—within ten minutes after I gave him the cheque.
ELIZA TRYE . I live with Mr. Gurney in the Strand, he is a wine merchant. I am frequently in the habit of exchanging notes for Mr. Solomon—I cannot say whether I did so in April.
THOMAS PHYTHIAN . I am a grocer, and live in the Strand; I am in partnership with Mr. Eaves. In the month of April, John Dean applied to me to exchange a £100 note, I do not recollect the day—it was left in my hands for three or four hours, and I sent it over by my apprentice to Solomon's house.
RICHARD STONE . I am an oilman, and live in Buckingham-street, Strand. I know John Dean, servant to Solomon, he came to me with a £100 note one day about the 7th or 8th of April—I did not give him change for it as I had not sovereigns enough—he came several times with £5 and £10 notes, and I gave him change for them sometimes, and sometimes not—I had £30 or £40 worth of notes of £5 or £10 from him, which I paid away to different tradespeople.
JOSEPH LUXTON . I live in King William-street, in the Strand, and am a publican—I know Mr. Solomon's man, Dean—he came to me for change—it was on a Wednesday, and in April, I think, but I am not quite clear—he came four or five times the same morning—I had changed for him before, but on that day I noticed his coming so frequently—he wanted change for some £5 notes which I gave him—there were fives I am quite positive—I am not quite certain whether I changed a ten or not, but I am certain about the fives—he wanted change for a £100 note that day—I was not able to give it him in gold—I said I could give it him in silver if he liked—I did not change it.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. When you paid away these notes, were you sometimes asked to put your name on them? A. I have done it without being asked sometimes—I do not believe I did on these notes—if any body had asked me to have done it, I certainly should have done it—I had changed £5 notes for him before that time, but none after—if he sent, and I had change, I gave it him in a moment—I never made the slightest difference in putting my name on his notes as well as others.
JOHN HOWARD . I was employed by Lea, ever since the 27th of March, to watch certain persons with respect to this robbery—I know Crutched-friars—I was there on Good Friday last—I saw both the prisoners, Caspars, there, both together, very deeply in conversation—their heads were close together for about an hour and a half—I saw them on the 1st of April, the day Moss's house was searched—I passed the house in Mansel-street where Moss had taken apartments and saw the elder Caspar stand with his hand to his mouth, leaning his head in the younger Caspar's neck, and pointing to the house where Moss lived in Mansel-street—I know the person of Davis who has absconded—I know his house in Coventry-court—I saw him leave his house in Coventry-court on several days while I was watching there—I cannot recollect the day of the month—I know the house where the prisoner Moses lives—I have seen Davis go from Coventry-court to the house of Moses in Vinegar-yard more than once or twice—I did not go into Moses' house—I saw Davis go in, and after he went in I went to the window and saw the prisoner Moses, and Davis go into the parlour on one occasion—it is very likely I have seen him go in on some
other occasion, but I never noticed any thing besides—I have seen the prisoner Abrahams at her father's house—I know Solomon's house in the Strand—I was set to watch there and on one occasion I saw Abrahams come out of Solomon's house, and thinking her a servant girl I did not follow her—I have no recollection of the day—it was my duty when I saw Mr. Lea every morning to tell him what transpired in the day—I after that saw Abrahams come out of Solomon's house, and a man of the name of Dixon was on the opposite side of the street watching with me, and he followed her as far as Bedford-street.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long had you been acting as a spy by Mr. Lea? A.
