Ever since the 27th of March—I was settled with on one occasion for fourty-four days' service—I do not know How much they owe me—I have made no calculation—they paid me 10 l. all but a fews hillings, for the forty-four days—I calculated it was about 4 s. a day—I made a daily report to Mr. Lea, my employer—Dixon was in Mr. Lea's employ—I do not know whether Roe had any one—I saw Dixon here—he dined over the way—we had different little expenses, extra drops of half-and-half, or so on, or any thing I took a fancy to—I made no charge, it was given to me—I told him there was refreshment at different times, and the amount was given to me—I am not aware that I dined at Mr. Lea's expense—I dined at my own, considering I was serving Mr. Hartley, he paid for my services, and I paid for my own dinners—I do not remember when the Caspars were taken up—I went to the police-office—I never was examined—Mr. Lea saw me daily—I do not know that I was kept back to be sprung upon the prisoners to-day—I did not tell the prisoners I had these facts, I had no business to do it—I do not know How many examinations there were—I was always in attendance—there were six examinations or there might be a dozen—there was nothing to prevent my being examined at the police-office—I have occasionally watched the prisoners' houses from morning to night—I watched as often as I could, and this is all I have been able to discover of them—I saw them in the street as I have described—I have seen them in the Borough about five o'clock, in broad daylight—there were other persons in the street—I pointed them out to some persons who were not spies, Messrs. Lea and Roe—Lea was not in sight of the Caspars—he was just by—I saw Mr. Hartley, he attended at the examination—I believe Mr. Dixon was not examined—I should say he was not—I have no doubt he was not—he is here to-day—the last time I saw Davis was on one occasion at the examination, when Mrs. Abrahams was going to make some statement, and he was there—I saw Lea that day—he saw Davis—there was not time given for him to abscond that I know of—he has absconded—I do not know How often I have seen him in the purlieus of the police-office—I have not seen him since Moses was in custody—I swear that—I do not know bow much they owe me, I do not know How many days it is—I leave it to their honour—I mean to swear I made no agreement for each day—I do not know that it depends on How I finish the job.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You spied Alice Abrahams? A. No, I did not spy her, but I saw her cross—I did not see her make a statement, she was up there for the purpose of making a statement—I saw Davis that day—I am not aware that I saw Davis after that—I have not seen him since Moses was in custody in Bow-street—I saw him there, and Lea knew he was there—he was not taken.
JAMES DIXON . I was employed by Lea to watch some persons supposed
to be implicated in this matter—I was at one time watching Solomon's house in the Strand—I cannot exactly say the day when that was—I saw the prisoner Abrahams come out—she went up Bedford-street—I was at the office when Solomon was first summoned there, and I was examined as a witness—what I have just stated happened after that.
THOMAS PHILLIPS I am in the accountant's office in the Bank of England. It is the duty of Hawkes and other pay clerks to make entries—I produce extracts from the Bank books as to the £300 and two £5 notes—I took the extracts myself, and examined them with another clerk—( reads)—"3rd January, 1839, £300, No. 39, 836"—the ledger is headed Matthew Marshall—it is entered to his credit—he is the chief cashier of the Bank—it is posted on the 2nd of April in the Y book, page 546—that is the Whole of the entry in that book—in the cash-book there is an entry on the 2nd of April, 1839, under the head of Mr. Hawkes, the cashier—I find the note entered—( reads)—"3rd of January, 39, 886, £300"—this is all the entry in both the books on the subject of the £800 note—according to the course of business at the Bank of England, that denotes that this note of £300 came in through Mr. Hawkes, on the 2nd of April, 1839, and was posted in the ledger to the credit of Matthew Marshall, the chief cashier, on the 2nd of April—with respect to the £5 notes, here is in page 729, the 2nd of April, 1838, £5, No. 71, 312—that is the date of the note—it is posted when it came into the Bank, on the 14th of May, 1839, ✗ 146, this is headed "Matthew Marshall, chief cashier"—in the cash-book, on the 14th of May, 1839, in ✗ 146, there appears "2nd of April, 1838, No. 71, 342 £5," and at the end of it is the name of Mr. Warner, teller, which signifies it came in through him—the other £5 note in the ledger is "3rd of March, 1838, No. 81, 436"—that is the date of the note, and it is headed Matthew Marshall "13th Of May, 1839, ✗ 43," which denotes the day on which it came in—in the cash-book, in page 43 ✗ 13th of May, 1839, is entered "3rd of March, 1838, 81, 406 £5"—it came in by the London Joint Stock Bank—according to the course of business at the Bank, the two extracts I have read denote the same circumstances with respect to the £5 note as are denoted of the £300 note—the date they come in, through whose hands, the date of the note, the number of it, the value of the note, and that they were posted in the book according to the entry—the extract denotes that the £300 note came in through the hands of Mr. Hawkes—and of the two £5 notes, that one came in through the medium of Warner, and the other through the Joint Stock Bank.
MR. PHILLIPS to JOHN HOWARD Q. You stated you had been paid for forty-four days, was it during these forty-four days you saw the two Caspars? A. Yes, I do not keep dates in my memory—I put down nothing in books—I had been employed the Wednesday previous to my seeing the two Caspars in Mansel-street on the Monday, which was five or six days—I was employed on the 27th—this took place on the 1st of May.
JAMES HANDS ( police-constable K 246.) I was at Lambeth-street office on the 14th of June—there are several places at the office where persons in custody are put into—I was near that place on the morning of the 14th of June—the prisoners were at that time confined there—I heard the prisoners Lewin Caspar and Money Moses quarrelling and talking very loudly—I heard Lewin Caspar say to Moses, "Do you intend to let me have any money? I want 40 l. or 50 l. to prepare for my defence"—Moses said, "No, I want four or five fifties to pay for my own defence;" and Caspar said, "Had I had the 1400 l. or 1200 l. as you promised, it might have been different"
—Moses made some answer which I did not distinctly hear—Moses then said, "You are a b—y rogue, you did not give him even what you had"—there was something said then which I did not hear—I heard Moses say, "Did you ever give him 5 l.?"
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS Q. How long have you belonged to the Police? A. Nearly three years—I was not employed listening at the cells—there were no other policeman in the yard—the jailor was part of the time, and heard some part, and then went away—there was a man who lives in the office came down to draw some water, and the noise he made drawing prevented me hearing the whole conversation—the jailor was there when that person came down—I was requested to stay in the yard—the jailor came to me and told me to stay in the yard to keep the door shut—I do no know How long exactly I had been in the yard—when Caspar said about the 1400 l. or 1200 l. Moses said something I did not hear, on account of the noise made by the man drawing the water—he was drawing it while I was listening—I do not know the person's name—he lives at Lambeth-street office—I cannot exactly say How long the yard is—it is not so long as this room—it is six or eight feet wide—the prisoners spoke quite loud—there are no bars, so that they can look out—there are four short bars over the door—the tap is at the further end of the yard—I cannot say exactly what time it was, it was from half-past eight to half-past nine o'clock—I think they were examined about twelve o'clock—I waited in the yard a very short time after that conversation—a very few minutes—I cannot say How long I was there before the conversation began—I should say a full minute—as soon as I heard them talking, their noise attracted my attention—I went to listen to what they were saying—I cannot say How many policemen there were outside the yard at that time—I cannot say How many were outside when I went out—I do not know that there were any there—they were not all there—the door of the yard is not locked, it closes with a spring-lock, which any body can open from the inside, but nobody from the outside—I cannot say if there is a key to open it outside—the jailor went away before I did, and came again—he went away before this conversation was ended—I cannot say How how long I was listening to this conversation—it might have been five minutes—I swear it was more than one minute—I will not swear it was five minutes—I went quite close to the door, and I went up the same day to be examined—I have not seen my depositions since—no copy has been given me since—this was the last day they were examined—I have had no opportunity of reading my deposition since—it is the jailor's duty to attend at the doors—he has the keys of the cell—nobody else can do it while he retains the keys—I am not aware that there is any key to the outer door.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS Q. What were you before you were in the police? A. I was in business as a hair-dresser till I entered the police—I have been nothing else—I have had a shop, and been journeyman—when I went into the police I had not been in regular employment for three months—I have no other acquaintance with the younger Caspar than seeing him at each examination except the first—I cannot say How often he was there—he was every time in the dock, and I was in the office—I had been acquainted with Moses the same time as I had been with Caspar—I heard him examined—that is all I have heard of him—while on examination, and since he has been in custody, I frequently heard him speak, while he was in the cells, and Caspar also—it was a
good thick door that they were behind—I could not see through it, but I could hear through it—there were only the four prisoners in that cell—I did not hear either of the others speak—I swear those are the persons who spoke—I know them well enough to know the sound of their voices through a deal door—I think the door is above an inch thick—I cannot swear How much more—it was not three inches—I did not go into the office immediately they were under examination and state this story—I was called into the office, and was not aware that the solicitor for the prosecution knew that I heard this—Mr. Humphreys was there—I do not know Mr. Abrahams—I had spoken about it among some of our men—I do not know that the jailor is here—I am twenty-eight years old.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES Q. Were you employed as a police-officer? A. Yes, I was only employed at the police-office in consequence of the excitement that this examination of the prisoners caused—I was there to prevent confusion at that time—the prisoners were sometimes confined in the cell where I heard the conversation, and sometimes in the adjoining cell—they were brought from the prison there—I believe they had been brought from the prison that morning—I had not seen them come—I did not see them put into that cell that morning—I saw them brought out after when they went into the office after that conversation—I was present at the cell when they were brought out, and followed them into the office—at the time of the conversation there were no more prisoners in that cell—after that the door was opened, and one or two of Moses' daughters came—the door was opened by the jailor two or three times prior to my hearing the conversation, for the purpose of taking something in to the prisoners, I then saw who was in the cell—he took them in a pipe and tobacco, and twice he took in a light—I believe that Solomon and Moss, who were admitted as witnesses, were in the further cell at the time of the conversation—there were no prisoners confined on night charges—there were not four other prisoners confined in the same cell at the time I heard this conversation, nor after till I saw the two young women let in by the jailor—I will swear that the prisoners were the only persons there at the time I heard the conversation—I cannot say if it was two or three times that I had looked into the cell and seen who was there, before J heard this conversation—I will not say from ten minutes to half an hour How long it was before—I had remained in the yard from the time of my looking in the cell till I heard the conversation—I was in the yard all the time, so as to be able to say who went in—there was not a drunken woman confined in that cell at that time, there might be afterwards—there was a drunken woman in the yard—I cannot say How long after, it might have been an hour, or it might have been two—she fainted away in the open yard—I cannot say if four of Moses' children came to see him that morning—I saw two I think—the jailor kept the key of that cell all that time—I did not at all—it was part of my duty to keep watch over the cells at the police-office—I was stationed in the yard to keep the prisoners and to keep people from going into the yard—I went at half-past eight o'clock—I left from nine o'clock to half-past nine, but I went in and out after that—I never left the yard from half-past eight o'clock till past nine—I might have gone up the steps of the office, but never out of sight of the cell door—I did not go into the office—I do not know where the jailor was all the time—I do not recollect who I first mentioned this conversation to—I mentioned it to some of our men—there was an inspector there—I did not consider this as of any importance—I think it was very likely to be the
jailor, who was the cause of my being taken before the Magistrate as he saw me standing there—I might have said to him, "They are quarreling there"—the jailor was there, and put his ear to the door, I believe twice—he did not hear the whole of it—to the best of my belief, I did not tell the jailor what I heard pass—when I went before the Magistrate, I heard the younger Caspar make a remark—I did not pay any attention to know whether he did wholly deny my statement or not—I perfectly or partly recollect what he stated, but whether he meant it as a denial of all I stated or not I do not know—I did not understand whether he totally denied it or not—I did not hear him say that my statement was false or untrue—I believe he said he had not made use of such words as I imputed to him, and he said that he applied to Moses to either give or lend him 5 l. to defend himself—I do not precisely recollect what the words were, but I believe he said he did that because he was in great distress for want of money—I believe he said Moses had promised to give or lend him 5 l. and that he said to Moses, "If I had got 1400 l. or 1200 l. by that transaction, as you are reported to have got by it, you should not have wanted 5 l. "—the drunken woman was not in the yard at the time of this conversation.
MR. BODKIN Q. You have been asked about the door and its thickness; How high was it? A. As near as I can state, from six to seven feet high, and over it were some short bars—there was no window—it was an open space.
JOHN HARDWICK, ESQ I am one of the police Magistrates at the office in Lambeth-street. I have been so about seventeen years—the examination of the prisoners and the witnesses in this case, was conducted before me—I am the committing Magistrate—I remember some papers being presented to me, which I took into my private room—I had had no communication with the parties to whom the statement alluded before they were put into my hands—they were put in publicly in the office in the presence of the counsel and attorney—I took these statements into my private room at the request of the counsel, which was made publicly—it was publicly stated to be for the purpose of enabling me to ascertain whether, in my discretion, I should receive the testimony of Solomon as a witness for the Crown—the prisoners were present, and the counsels and attorneys on all sides, and the office full of people—I desired the clerk to accompany me to the private room—the attorney of Solomon was present in my private room—the statements were in the hands of the clerk, and I believe were read by the clerk in the presence of the young Solomon and Solomon's attorney—each of the persons who had made statements were brought into the room to me by themselves, and each of the statements were read to me without the interference of any body—the statements were read to each separately, and the others were not in the room at the time—after that had been done, and I had come to my conclusion as to the course to be taken, the persons left the private room, and were taken into the office, and there a viva voce examination by counsel took place publicly.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You say that these papers were delivered to you publicly in the public office? A. Yes—I do not know whether any of the prisoners or their counsel or solicitors had the means of ascertaining one word of their contents—all that passed was, the counsel requested the Magistrates would take certain papers into their hands and retire into their room, and there read them—none of the counsel of any of the prisoners had any means of knowing one syllable that they contained—the counsel and attorneys for the prosecution retired with me—
the counsel and solicitors for the prisoners were not allowed to be there—Mr. Abrahams, the attorney for Mr. Solomon, was allowed to be in the room—I cannot say How many persona came into the room to make statements—there was Solomon and I think two of his sons—I do not recollect whether there was any more—I do not recollect whether Mr. Dean was one.
Q. Were these statements signed by the parties? A. Yes—after they had been read over to them, and every question strictly forbidden, and they had been distinctly told that the Magistrates would only receive it as voluntary confession, which might be used against them—when the papers were read by the clerk to us, the persons making the statements were asked, if such statements were true—the papers were each signed by the parties making the statements—I cannot say into whose hands they immediately passed, but I suppose into the hands of the counsel, as notes to examine the witnesses afterwards in open court—I received the papers from the hands of the counsel for the prosecution, before I went into the room, and returned them to their hands—this private examination might have lasted an hour and a half—I believe the counsel and attorneys for the prosecution had private audiences with me several times, not every morning—we were asked for a private audience and gave it, and should have given you the same if you had asked—Alice Abrahams was examined before me before this—I cannot say if she was examined on her oath, but she was examined and made a statement as a witness—I have never been desirous of any testimony, but when I found there was evidence to implicate her—there was none excepting her own statement—the prisoners were to be kept separate in Cold Bath Fields—there were various directions from time to time—myself and my brother Magistrate were there when these statements were read over—I am not sure whether Lea or Roe Were there.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Were you there when Lewin Caspar came to give information of this robbery? A. Yes, I think it was about twelve o'clock in the day—Lea, the officer, came and asked me whether I Would give a gentleman in waiting a private audience—I found that person to be Lewin Caspar—when I saw him at the bar, I recognized him to be the person I had seen in the private room—he said he had been to the Mansion-house, and they had referred him to our office, for the purpose of detecting the thieves—in consequence of his application, I directed the officers to give the necessary assistance—in the interview young Caspar appeared agitated, and expressed great anxiety about the matter, and he put some papers into my hand, stating on these he had given an order for the delivery of some chests of gold dust.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You have told us some orders were given that the prisoners should be kept separate? A. Yes, and I believe they were kept separate, so that they should have no communication with each other, except when they came to the office—I should not have read these papers in my private room, if I had not thought it detrimental to justice to read them publicly.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. When you speak of counsel for the prisoners, do you mean any body else but Solomon? A. No.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS to JOHN ROE. Q. Did you search the upper room at all at Solomon's house? A. Yes—I did not see any boxes there to my recollection—if I had seen any I should have opened them—I searched the house generally, as particularly as I could—I searched that room as carefully as I could—I certainly should have broken open any boxes I had
found there—I will not positively swear that there were no boxes there, but to the best of my recollection there was none—there was a good deal of lumber there—I did not pull it about, but I looked, and did not find any thing there that I was looking for—the room was small—it was perfectly light.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. In the course of this investigation have you become acquainted with a person of the name of Francis Allen? A. I have—that is the clerk in Mr. Hartley's office, who has run away—he did not introduce me to a person whose name is Kean, a sailor on board the City of Limerick —he did not introduce any person to me as a sailor who had been on board the City of Limerick, or who could give any information concerning the robbery.
(William Stewart, baker, Brydges-street, Covent-garden; John Buckland, surgeon, Watling-street; Isaac Joliff, wine-merchant, Queen-street, Cheapside; William Ward, collecting clerk; Thomas Slee, clerk to Charrington and Co.; Thomas Croton, clerk to Mr. Ormes, Blackfriars-road: George Pearkes, shell-fishmonger, Vinegar-yard, Drury-lane; John Allen, tea-dealer, Great Russell-street, Covent-garden; Arnold Stead, gold and silver laceman, Drury-lane; Edward Bonney, publican, Little Shire-lane; William Ashcraft, boot-maker, Brydges-street, Covent-garden; John Gillot, Denton-street, St.'Pancras; John Hailstone, tobacco-pipemaker, Brewers-court, Bedford-bury; Nicholas M'Cann, surgeon, Parliament-street; Benjamin Grimes, engine-maker and pewterer, Osborn-street; Thomas Pearsal, sword-blade ornamenter, Little White's-alley, Chancery-lane; Thomas Hughes Headling, silversmith and polisher, Sutton-street, Clerkenwell; John Henry Nunn, brass-founder, Curtain-road; gave Moses and Abrahams a good character.)
(Dr. Solomon Hershell, Chief Rabbi of the German Jews in London; Phillip Lazarus, stationer, Rosemary-lane; Mordecai Lazarus, David-lane, Shadwell; Nathan Canstad, surgeon, Bury-street, St. Mary Axe; Morris Dyte, surgeon, Houndsditch; Hyam Ansell, watch-manufacturer, Sidney-street, Commercial-road; Thomas Chubb, tailor, Spital-square; Israel Isaacs, butcher, Duke-street, Aldgate; Aaron Simons, watchmaker and jeweller, Bishopsgate-street; Maria Everest, Cross-street, Finsbury; Ann Hall, servant of Maria Everest; William Kelly, cheesemonger, Chiswell-street; William Ebourn, clock-case maker, Great Saffron-hill; Alfred Wilson, clock-case maker, Kirby-street, Clerkenwell; Godfrey Abrahams, watchmaker, Great Prescott-street; Abraham Abrahams, watch-manufacturer, Prescott-street; deposed to that of Lewin and Ellis Caspar.)
LEWIN CASPAR — GUILTY . Aged 24.
ELLIS CASPAR — GUILTY. Aged 55.—As Accessary before and after the Fact.
MOSES — GUILTY . Aged 56.
ABRAHAMS — GUILTY . Aged 33.
Judgment Respited.

[trialAccount t18390617-1961]
1961. HENRY CHAPLIN and WILLIAM PRATT were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of May, 1 handkerchief value 2 s., the goods of Patrick Burns, from his person.
JOHN ARCHER ( police-constable G 150.) On the 21 it of May I saw the prisoners together in Greenwich fair—they went behind a gentleman and tried his pocket, but found nothing—I saw them afterwards go behind the prosecutor—Pratt felt in his pocket, then went back and spoke to Chaplin, who closed up to the prosecutor, Pratt covering him—Chaplin pat his hand into the prosecutor's left coat-tail pocket, and drew this handkerchief—I immediately took hold of him, and took it from his hand—I secured them both.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you sure you can make no mistake about this? A. Quite—there were a good many persons in the fair—I had never seen Chaplin before—I took them on the spot, before they moved a step.
PATRICK BURNS . I am a labourer, and live in Commerial-road, Lambeth. This is my handkerchief—I had it in my pocket at Greenwich fair, on Tuesday, the 21st of May—I was looking at a show—the officer tapped me on the shoulder, and asked if that handkerchief was mine, and I said it was—he had hold of the prisoners—I did not feel it taken.
(Chaplin received a good character.)
CHAPLIN — GUILTY —Aged 14. Recommended to mercy. PRATT — GUILTY —Aged 14. Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18390617-1962]
1962. WILLIAM FISHWICK was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 3 pence and 3 halfpence, the monies of Jacob Stevens, from the person of Maria Stevens.
MARIA STEVENS . I am the wife of Jacob Stevens, who is a porter in the Paragon, at Blackheath. On the 20th of May I was in Greenwich fair, about one o'clock in the day, looking at a show—I felt a hand in my pocket, turned round, and saw the prisoner standing close by the side of me—I said he had picked ray pocket—he said if I said that I should swear his life away—I said, "They are my halfpence and penny-pieces you have in your hand, and I will hold you till I feel"—I did so, and then he passed it to another as soon as I said it was mine—he got away from me, but I caught him again, and a policeman, in plain clothes, came and took him—I am quite sure he is the person who had my money—I did not see his hand come out of my pocket, but he was the closest to me—there was another standing behind, to whom I saw him pass the money—I distinctly saw the money in his hand.
JOHN ARCHER ( police-constable G 150.) I was in Greenwich fair, and saw the prosecutrix holding the prisoner—I held him while she saw what she had lost—she felt, and said, "Fourpence-halfpenny, and principally in
penny-pieces"—I found one shilling, one halfpenny, and three farthings on him—she told me she saw him pass her money to another boy—I looked round, and the other boy had got away.
Prisoners Defence. I only came to London on Saturday night to look for work—I saw a number of people going to the fair, and went too, and as I was pushing by, the lady caught hold of me, and said I had been picking her pocket—I said I had not—she said I had the halfpence in my hand—I opened it directly, and had none—she then looked on the ground, and said I had given it to somebody behind—there were three or four hundred people as close as could be.
MARIA STEVENS re-examined. He opened his hand after he had passed the money to the other one—I am sure I saw it in his hand—I did not look on the ground—I had no occasion to do so.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18390617-1963]
1963. JAMES RAYNER was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of May, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of a man whose name is unknown, from his person.
JOHN ARCHER . I am a policeman. On the 22nd of May I was at Greenwich fair, in plain clothes. I observed the prisoner there for about a quarter of an hour, and watched him—he went behind a gentleman, lifted the tail of his coat, and took this handkerchief from it—I immediately went to the gentleman, and asked if he had lost his handkerchief—he felt, and said he had—I said, "This boy has got it; come with me, and I will take it from him"—the prisoner crept under a show—I fetched him out, took the handkerchief from him, and the gentleman claimed it—he would not come to the station-house, as he said the had so much trouble in a case before—I do not know his name.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Was it this boy took it out of the pocket? A. Yes—there were two others in company with him at one time, but he was alone when he did this—I found a quantity of toys on him, which he said a boy had put into his pocket—I asked How he came by the handkerchief—he said the boy had put that in also.
GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18390617-1964]
1964. MARY DESMON was indicted for killing and slaying Emma Brasley.
ELIZABETH BRASLEY . I am fourteen years old. Emma Brasley was my sister—she was two years and six months old—I was with her in the yard behind the house, between one and two o'clock one day, standing by her side, and the prisoner, who lived in the same house, threw some hot water out of the window, which came partly on my sister—she fell down—I picked her up directly, and she was taken in doors—I had seen the window opened a few minutes before the water was thrown out, and I had seen the prisoner at the window about a quarter of an hour before—my sister was taken to the hospital the same afternoon—the prisoner came down stairs crying, and said she was very sorry for what had happened.
CHARLOTTE JOY . I saw the child directly after it was scalded, and assisted the mother in taking her clothes off—the skin came off with the clothes—the prisoner seemed very much troubled and sorry for what she had done.
ELIZABETH BRASLEY . I am the child's mother. I was sweeping my room, and heard my little girl call out—I fetched her in—the prisoner ran
down stairs in about five minutes, and was in great distress about it—she said she had thrown the water out, and appeared very sorry.
ROWLAND WILLIAMS . I am a surgeon. The child was brought to the hospital on Thursday evening, the 23rd of May, and lived till Sunday—she died from the effects of the scald.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390617-1965]
1965. JAMES PERCEVAL was indicted for feloniously assaulting John Selby Watson, on the 6th of June, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 7 keys, value 7 s.; 1 purse, value 1 s.; 6 sovereigns, 4 half-sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 2 shillings, his goods and monies; and that he immediately, before and at the time of the robbery, feloniously did beat, strike, and use other personal violence to the said John Selby Watson.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN SELBY WATSON . I now live at Maidstone, but at the time of this transaction I lived at Eltham. On the 6th of June last year, I was going home down Wellhall-lane, leading to Eltham, out of the Dover-road—it was about half-past ten o'clock by the time I got to where I was robbed—the lane is a mile and a half long—before I got to the spot I passed the witness Leopard, who is lame—I got considerably ahead of him before any thing happened—when I got about two hundred yards up the lane I was met by two soldiers, who separated in such a manner as to oblige me to go between them—the one on my right-hand said, "Good night," and the other at the same moment, or nearly so, struck me with some heavy weapon which I did not see, on the left side of my head—I was stunned by the blow and knocked down—I remained insensible for some time, and on coming to myself I found the two men over me, one rifling my pockets and the other holding me—they had turned out one of my pockets, and were proceeding to rifle the other—I attempted to cry out, but the one who held me down put sand into my mouth, and pressed my mouth to prevent my calling out—they took from me money to the amount of 8 l. odd, in six sovereigns, four half-sovereigns, and some shillings—I had no opportunity of noticing the features of either of them—I was lying on the ground at the moment they left me—they ran off as hard as they could towards Woolwich—on recovering a little I got up and walked towards Eltham, but thinking they might have left something on the spot I went back and looked about for my things, and while I was looking Leopard came up—I was bleeding very much—with his assistance I proceeded to Eltham—the clock struck eleven just as I entered Eltham—when I was lying in the lane I laid along the road, in the same direction as the road—it was rather a wide part of the lane—it was a moonlight night, but the moon was behind the' men as they came up—there is a pathway there—I was in the pathway when struck, but when I was lying down I was in the road—the path is on the level of the road.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How long was it before Leopard came up to you? A. Not more than five minutes after the outrage was committed—there is no turning between the end of the lane and the spot where I was robbed—I passed Leopard within one hundred yards before I got to the Dover-road—the end of the lane goes into the Dover-road—I was robbed about two hundred yards from the Dover-road—it might be more, but cannot be much more—I do not think it possible a person could walk along the road without seeing me lying there—I saw that the men were Marines from their dress—they had scarlet jackets—I cannot say that I noticed their trowsers—I have said to the best of my belief they had white
trowsers, but I wish to state I only say that to the best of my belief—I did not notice the lower part of their dress so much as the other.
MR. BODKIN. Q. At what pace was Leopard going when you saw him? A. At a slow place, being lame—I walked much faster—I think he must have entered Wellhall-lane by the time this occurred—there are a few houses in the lane, but the greater part is only hedges—there was nothing but the hedge to prevent any body getting out of the high-road if they thought proper.
HENRY LEOPARD . Hive at Eltham. I was going there from Wolwich-common, on the night of the 6th of June last year—I remember seeing Mr. Watson pass me—I was then about three hundred yards from the Dover-road, but I cannot say exactly—I crossed the Dover-road and entered Wellhall-lane—after I entered the lane a little distance, I met the witness Norton—he was in uniform—I have known him from his infancy—he was going in the direction of Woolwich—he was walking at a steady pace—he was alone—he said, "Good night," and I bid him the same—we called each other by name—I had got four or five hundred yards or more up the lane when I met him—I described to the constable the place where I met him, and he had the ground measured—Norton would not have an opportunity of seeing me coming some time before we met—as the road is zig-zag, winding—when I got further up the lane I got to Mr. Watson—I had not seen any other person in the interval—I found him hurt very much and assisted him to Eltham—I spoke of having seen Norton—his father and all his relations live at Eltham—I had left Eltham that day, and was returning home.
JAMES WATTS . I am high constable of Woolwich. The witnesses pointed out the places to me which they speak of—I measured the distances, and they are accurately stated in this plan ( produced)—I first saw Norton the morning after the robbery—he made a communication to me on the subject—in consequence of which the prisoner was taken into custody a few days afterwards—he was not in the barracks that day—I was present before Sir Thomas Matthew Wilson and Sir John Webb at the examination—he made a statement before them, which was taken down in writing—I saw him put his mark to it—( looking at it)—this is the hand-writing of the two Magistrates—he called a witness named Thomas Baker, who also made a statement—this is it—I cannot swear whether it was made in Perceval's presence or not, but he was called as a witness for Perceval—I cannot charge my memory whether he was present, because at one period the witnesses were out of the room.
Cross-examined. Q. Were not other witnesses called by Perceval besides Baker? A. There were witnesses called, but whether by Perceval I cannot say—I know several were called, and examined one by one out of the room—I do not recollect that Perceval called other witnesses besides Baker—I was present in December last when Baker was convicted of this offence—he was the man who was examined at the office.
CHARLES STEWART WARDEN . I was present when Baker was called as a witness before the Magistrate when Perceval was charged with this offence—he was examined in Perceval's presence.
The examination of Baker was here read, as follows. —"On Wednesday the 6th of June, I met Perceval opposite the dock-yard gate in Woolwich, about half-past nine o'clock—he and I went together on the Lower-road to Greenwich—we went to the Black Boy at first, and there we remained all night—no one but Perceval and I went in—we met some comrades there
—I am quite certain I did not leave the barracks with Perceval at six o'clock—I am sure Best did not go into the Black Boy with us—I am sure Perceval and I did not go half-way up the fair, or in it, at all—on Thursday we went to London—we walked—I had half a crown—I don't know what Perceval had."
HENRY NORTON . I am in the 22nd company of the Royal Marines. On the 6th of June last year, I went to Eltham to see my father and mother—I came, in my way home, down Wellhall-lane, about twenty minutes or half-past ten o'clock—I was alone—I met Evans, a postman, as soon as I got outside my father's house, but met nobody else till I got into the lane—I met there two soldiers—the prisoner is one—I do not know who the other was—they had round red jackets on, black trowsers, and side belts, which is the regimental dress—I pointed out to the constable the place where I met them in the lane—they were coming from Woolwich towards Eltham—I was going towards Woolwich—I said, "Holloa Perceval, where are you going this time of night?"—he said, "I am going to Footscray, will you come?"—I said, "I have no pass for Footscray, I am going to barracks," and I wished him good night—the other man said nothing—I have known Perceval three or four months—he is not in the same company as me, but in the same barracks, and I have mounted guard with him.
Q. Before you met these two men, in going down the lane did you see Mr. Watson either walking or lying down on the road? A. No, I did not—after I parted from them I met Leopard, and bid him good night—I knew him before, and he me—I then went straight to the barracks—I was out with permission that day—I had a pass—I was to return by six o'clock next morning—I was spoken to that night Bt the barracks after I was in bed about the robbery, and I stated what I knew of it—I believe inquiry was made for Perceval afterwards in the barracks—I was taken into custody, and taken before the Magistrate, and examined as a witness in Perceval's presence—I have been abroad lately on duty—I was abroad last December when Baker was tried—I returned about a month ago—I do not know at what time Perceval came into the barracks on the following day.
Cross-examined. Q. How soon after this night did you go abroad? A. I cannot tell exactly—about two months—I was present before the Magistrate when Baker was there—I never heard Baker charge me with being implicated in the robbery—he never did so in my presence at any time—I was taken up the same night—I was in custody about an hour and a half—I was sober that night—I could see very well on a moonlight night—I saw Evans and Leopard—I did not observe any gap in the hedge in any part of the lane—I swear I saw nobody lying in the road.
MR. BODKIN. Q. At what pace were you walking, slow or fast? A. I was not walking very fast, at a moderate pace—my clothes were searched at the barracks the same night, when I was charged.
GEORGE CHAPMAN . I am a sergeant-major of the Marines. I know Perceval very well—he was absent from the barracks on the night of the 7th of June last year—he had leave on the night of the 6th of June till six o'clock the following morning, but he did not return till late on the night of the 7th, or early on the morning of the 8th—he was absent a day longer than his leave permitted him—Baker was absent without leave on the night of the 6th—he had no leave on the 6th or 7th either—I cannot say whether Baker was in on the 7th—I only know he was present on the 8th—when on duty they were dressed in dark trowsers,
which we call Oxford grey cloth, almost as dark as black, and a round scarlet jacket, and with that jacket they would have a dark blue forage cap—they would be compelled to leave the barracks in that dress—they could not go out of the gate without it—a man may put on his full dress coat and cap, but cannot alter his trowsers—if he has on the fatigue jacket he must have on the forage cap—Norton has been between eighteen months or two years I believe in the regiment—he is in the same company now—his being out of England was in the performance of his duty—he went out in the Hastings —he left England the latter end of September.
Cross-examined. Q. A man on duty would have cloth trowsers on that day? A. Yes, and those not on duty would have the same—the order is given in the morning for dress—nobody had any right to put on white trowsers—there is a non-commissioned officer at the gate to see them go out—Perceval came into the regiment in April—I never heard any charge against him.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Then nobody could be allowed to have white trowsers on with that foraging cap? A. Unless they, sent out the white trowsers by somebody, or they might conceal them under their dress.
JAMES ALLEN . I am a sergeant in the Royal Marines. I was at the barracks on the night of the 6th of June when Norton came in—it had just gone a quarter after eleven o'clock—he had been drinking but was not drunk—he appeared steady—I desired him to go to bed quietly and not disturb his comrades—he went to bed directly—I do not know at what time he went out—I did not see Perceval leave that day—I do not know when he came in—one or two persons came to the barracks that night and made a communication—I did not go with them to Norton's room.
Cross-examined. Although not drunk he seemed to have sufficient to make you caution him to go to bed quietly? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Did he appear in a state to disable him from knowing a person that he met? A. I consider him quite capable of that.
JOHN SELBY WATSON re-examined. Q. During the time these men were rifling your pockets, did you notice what they had on their heads? A. They had caps—I noticed that distinctly.
Cross-examined. What kind of cap? A. A round, highish cap with a tassel.
GEORGE CHAPMAN re-examined. The caps are blue worsted, setup inside with cloth to make them stand up—they are about seven inches high—there is no tassel, but a brass in front—the persons who mind the gate see that they come in in their right dress—the officer who was on duty when they returned is on foreign service now.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Lord Chief Justice Tindal.
[trialAccount t18390617-1966]
1966. GEORGE WILLIS was indicted for the wilful murder of William Shepherd.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
ANDREW PATTON . I am acting bombardier of the fifth battalion of the Royal Artillery. William Shepherd was sergeant-major of the second battalion of the Royal Artillery—the prisoner was a gunner and driver in the same battalion—on Sunday morning, the 26th of May, I was on the parade in front of the barracks at Woolwich—the second battalion with other troops in the same barracks were being paraded previous to going to church—it was about twenty minutes to ten o'clock—they were not formed, they were all waiting scattered about together—there were between two
and three hundred—I observed the deceased standing in front of the barracks on the parade—I also observed the prisoner—I suppose he was about twenty yards from the deceased, when I first noticed him—the deceased was then standing with his back towards the prisoner—I saw the prisoner move in advance about four or five paces from the flag stones upon which he had been standing before, that brought him nearer to the deceased—I then saw him bring his musket first to the hip, as if to cock it, and then to his shoulder—he took a steady and deliberate aim at the deceased and fired—the deceased immediately fell forward on his face, and I do not think he moved afterwards—the prisoner stood for a second as if to see what effect the shot had taken, then turned to his left about, and threw his musket on the ground—I ran up and seized him by the belt and cuff of his coat—Lieutenant Bingham and Sergeant Poison came up, and I think it was Sergeant Poison asked him How he came to commit such a rash act, but the same question was put by several people at the same time—the prisoner answered, "I meant this some time"—I distinctly heard him use that phrase—he appeared quite cool and collected at the time—he was not one of those who were going to church that morning—he was going to mount guard.
Q. Would he have any authority, according to your regulations, to have his gun loaded with ball that day? A. None—the muskets are loaded with hall when the men are stationed on convict guard, but there is no convict guard on the Sunday.
Q. When the muskets are loaded for the men on convict guard, are they loaded from the ball cartridges which are regularly given out to the men, or are they supplied with ball cartridges for that particular purpose? A. They are supplied with ball cartridge for that particular purpose—there is merely one charge given for the musket—it is the duty of a man after coming off convict guard to draw the charge—it is drawn before he leaves the guard-room, and delivered over to the sergeant of the guard—the men have two packages given them for their pouches—each package contains five cartridges—I believe the prisoner has been in the battalion twelve months—the deceased had been there many years—I do not know How long.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. The men had not fallen in? A. No—the men were scattered about all over the parade when this happened, but I did not notice any one near the deceased when this happened—he was not on any military duty, standing on one spot more than another—the prisoner had no business to have the ball cartridge in his musket that day—it is delivered over to the sergeant, before they leave the guard-room—he had no business with it at all, not being on convict guard, neither in or out of his musket.
MR. BODKIN. Q. When a man goes on convict guard he has a particular ball cartridge given him to load his musket with? A. Yes, and before he leaves, it is taken out of his musket, and given back to his superior officer, and the musket is examined, so that it would be impossible to leave the room with a cartridge in it—he would have the ordinary ten in his cartridge box—every man is always served out with ten rounds, but that he is not to use.
COURT. Q. Then, has a man at all times ten in his cartridge box? A. Yes—he has no business with any loose cartridges—the cartridges in the box are the same kind as those be has on convict guard, but he has no business to use them—he has one given him for this particular service.
GEORGE WILLIAM BINGHAM . I am a Lieutenant in the first battalion of the Royal Artillery. I knew the deceased—he was Sergeant-major of the second battalion—he was not under me—on Sunday morning, the 26th May, about half-past ten o'clock, I got up from my breakfast table in my own room on the ground-floor, and looked out of window—I saw the Sergeant-major moving along towards the private parade, where his guard was falling-in—I saw the prisoner with his musket to his shoulder levelled towards the deceased—he was about twenty-five yards from me at the time, and about eight or nine yards from the Sergeant-major—before I could think, I heard the explosion of the musket which was pointed at the Sergeant-major, and I saw the Sergeant-major fall fiat on his face on the ground—his back was towards the prisoner and likewise towards me—when I saw him fall, I had not the least doubt in the world be was a dead man—the prisoner stood still and seemed to poise his musket, to hold it to his shoulder to see if he had perpetrated the act—I rushed from my room and cried out, "Seize that man," and before I got up to him he was secured by two non-commissioned officers—be was standing between Sergeants Poison and Patton when I got up—the moment I arrived I put my hand on his arm and said, "What could induce you to commit such an act?"—he said, "I have meant it some time, or I have thought of it some time"—I cannot be certain which—one or the other I am perfectly sure of—I have been in Court while Patton was examined—the account he has given of the manner in which the ball cartridges are given out for convict service, and the duty of returning them, is perfectly correct—when a man mounts duty during divine service, it is not his duty to have his musket charged at all—he should have nothing in it—there is a difference between ball cartridges and blank cartridges—ball cartridges are made up in whitey-brown paper, and blank cartridges in blue paper—it was not his duty to have any cartridge in his musket, but ten in his pouch, made up in packets of five—there is never any convict guard on Sunday—the prisoner never was on convict guard—he was once, as I have heard, warned for it, but was never on it—Her Majesty's birth-day was celebrated on the 23rd of May—the men fired the usual fen-de-joie on that occasion, which is three rounds of blank cartridges—they are delivered out for that express purpose—no more and no less than three to each individual.
Q. Did you examine the body of the unhappy man who fell? A. As soon as I left the prisoner I went towards the deceased, and made way for him to be carried into one of the barrack-rooms—he was laid on his back, and was stone dead.
Cross-examined. Q. The ten cartridges you speak of are ball cartridges? A. Yes—according to the regulations every man carries ten rounds in his pouch at all times—that is all they are to carry except when they go out to feu-de-joie —they are left at home in case of accident—the ten are for them if called suddenly on any duty—I do not know myself when the prisoner had his ten served out to him—they are put back into his pouch, I know.
JAMES M'GORRERY . I am orderly to the second detachment of the Royal Artillery. It was my duty to call the men who were to mount guard on the 26th—the prisoner was the first man for guard, and I called him first—he did not come when he was called—I called his name twice—I saw him the next instant—I saw the deceased there—I did not see him do any
thing—I saw the prisoner present his piece and fire—the Sergeant-major was walking when he fired.
Q. Had the prisoner been at all corrected by the deceased shortly before this day for any improper conduct? A. He was found fault with for being dirty on parade on the 23rd of May, the Queen's birth-day, by the Sergeant-major, and the Adjutant ordered him to have an extra parade as a punishment—it was my duty to see that he had an extra parade, and I told him to attend parade, but he did not come—it was my duty to report his absence to Sergeant-major Shepherd—I did so—the Sergeant-major told me to parade him again, and let him know How he turned out on parade the following morning—some Sergeant-majors confine a man for absence, but I was to try him again—I warned him of a second parade, but he did not come to that—I should have to report that to the Sergeant-major at the parade time the following morning, which was Sunday the 26th—I had not done it—I was just about to do it as the Sergeant-major was walking towards me, when this unfortunate affair occurred—the Sergeant-major was coming towards me at the time he was shot—the prisoner would have an opportunity of seeing that—he had been found fault with several times for slovenliness in dress—I never counted his ball cartridges.
Cross-examined. Q. The Sergeant-major did not confine him then when he did not appear on extra parade? A. No, be was a very kind man to the men—the prisoner was first for guard on this morning—I did not find him in company with the Sergeant-major, as if wanting to talk together—I called his name twice, and the men told me he was talking to the Sergeant—I did not turn round and see him with the Sergeant-major as if wishing to talk together—I saw the Sergeant-major coming away from towards him, but I did not see they had be*n talking together—I did not see them together—I think I said I did before the Coroner, but I corrected that evidence—I was under a mistake then.
Q. Recollect now, do you think they seemed as if they wished to talk together, or at least that he wished to talk to the Sergeant-major? A. No, the Sergeant-major appeared coming from towards him, but I heard something—I was too far off—I had not reported his having missed the second parade then.
GEORGE POWELL . I am a gun-driver in the Royal Artillery. I did not examine the prisoner's pouch after parade on the Queen's birth-day—I do not know who did.
ALEXANDER STEEL . I am Quarter-master Sergeant of the 2nd battalion of Royal Artillery. I did not examine the prisoner's cartouch box after parade on the Queen's birth-day—I did after the murder of the Sergeant-major—I found one bundle of the ball cartridges complete, the other with one apparently taken out—there were four left—it was not tied up as it had been when delivered—it was tied up, but not complete—the string is usually crossed, but it was only tied one way—it had evidently been opened since it was delivered—I examined the pouch immediately after the accident took place.
RICHARD QUIRK . I am a gunner and driver in the second battalion I did not examine the prisoner's pouch after parade on the Queen's birth-day, nor do I know who did—on the Sunday morning this happened, I saw the prisoner before he went on parade—he took his musket out with him—on the Saturday afternoon previous, a few minutes before six o'clock, I was at the window looking at the parade falling in—he asked me if they were
falling in, and the Sergeant-major going to inspect them—this was in the, barrack-room—I could see the parade from the window—I told him they were going to fall in, and the Sergeant-major was not there—he said he should not go down a. the Sergeant-major was not there—he asked that question about the Sergeant-major being there two-or three times over, and I always gave him the same answer, that the Sergeant-major was not there—he said he should not go down, it did not matter, he said he should see him in the morning—It was his duty to have gone on the parade then.
GEORGE POWELL I saw the prisoner in the barrack-room after the Queen's birth-day, and while he was on parade I saw his cartridge, between the rugs of his bed—he was not present when I saw them—I do not. know whether they leave their ball cartridges behind when they go to fire blank cartridge, but he had left his behind—there were five in each packet, complete and regular as made up—he had left them between the rug, of his bed—there were two parcels, five in each, perfect and regularly made up.
Cross-examined. Q. How was it you were not on parade? A. I was not a duty man—the men sleep in separate beds—I was cooking, and cleaning up the room, and saw this projecting out, and I found these package, one on the top of the other—I look the top one off, and put it down by the side, that it should not project so much. Q. Did you count the cartridges? A. I felt them with my finger—his pouch was not there—there was nothing at all remarkable in the appearance of the cartriage—I could feel them with my finger, and the other package was the same size—I caught them up in my hand to pull the top one off—all I did was to put them out of the position they were in, that the bed might be put right, but I could see there were five in each—I did not open the packages, but I counted them outside—I could see them—they presented the usual appearance-nothing induced me to notice them—I did not see any other man's cartridge in his bed—I did not untie the packages, but I saw there were five in each—there was nothing outside the package to show the number inside, that I know of—( the package was here produced.)
COURT. Q. is that the common appearance of the cartridge? A. Yes—they laid one upon the other, and I took the top one off, and put it by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. If one cartriage was out of either of those package, must you not see it immediatly? A. Yes, unless any thing else was put in.
WILLIAM BRADFORD . I am a gunner and driver in the second battalion of the royal Artillery. On the day before the Sergeant-major was killed I saw the prisoner in his room in the barracks, about ten minutes before seven o'clock in the evening—he was dressed for guard—I asked him if he was going on guard—he said no, he was not, he was going to show himself with the guard—I said, "You have plenty of guards now"—he said, "Yes, but I will not have many more"—I asked him the reason of that—he said, "You will see to-morrow"—I asked him what was to-morrow—he said, "Wait, and you will see"—he said, "There is one particular person I wish to see on the parade"—I asked him who that person was—he told me I would see to-morrow—he said, "I don't think it is the Sergeant-major's fault that was to parade to-night, for he never inspects me when I do parade; I think it is the Orderly's doing, and I will serve him out"—I then left the room, and saw no more of him—he said serve him out—I then left the room, and saw no more of him—he said
he thought the Sergeant-major had got a spite against him—I did not see him on the parade—he said nothing about going on parade that night.
Cross-examined. Q. You say you heard the prisoner declare he thought the Sergeant-major had a spite against him? A. Yes.
THOMAS MOORE . I am a bombardier in the second battalion of the Royal Artillery. After this occurrence had taken place I went into the room No. 7, and the doctor came in soon after me—I examined the prisoner's poach about eleven o'clock, and found two bundles—one appeared smaller than the other—it appeared to me that one cartridge was gone out of one of the bundles, but I did not open them—I could tell by the feel and appearance—it was tied in a different way to what it usually is—I had not the slightest doubt that one had been taken out—one could not be taken out without my knowing it.
WILLIAM RICHARDSON . I am a surgeon and medical officer. I examined the body of the deceased—I found a gun-shot wound passing through the body—that was undoubtedly the cause of death.
COURT. Q. Could you tell on which side it entered? A. On the right of the spine, between that and the lower angle of the blade bone, and came out between the centre of the breast and the right nipple—it broke one rib in front, and two ribs behind, and passed through the heart.
CHARLES JAMEISON . I am a gunner and driver in the second battalion. I know the prisoner—I saw him on Saturday afternoon, the day before this happened—he was speaking to me about his extra parade—he had to parade with the night guard, and he told me he would make it all right in the morning—he said nothing else to me that night—he said some time before that the Sergeant-major had a spite against him—I do not recollect bow lately before the Sunday that was.
GUILTY — DEATH . Aged 18.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant,

1969. RICHARD DODDERIDGE was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of June, 1 pewter-pot, value 2 s., the goods of William Grigg.
WILLIAM GRIGG . I keep the Plough, at Lewisham. The prisoner, with two more men, and two women, came to my house in company—they had two pots of beer, and while I was sitting in the bar the prisoner walked out with a pot—I followed him to the top of Blackheath-hill—I there saw
him throw it away—he walked on fast—I went after him—he abused me, and I went to the station-house—he and the two girls walked on—the policeman and I went and took the prisoner—this is my pot.
HENRY MUMFORD . I am the officer. I went to the place the prosecutor described, and found the pot—I then went and took the prisoner—he threw himself on the ground, and said he would not go—we had a great struggle.
Prisoner's Defence. While I was calling for the second pot of beer, the men went on, and they had not paid for the beer—I followed them with the pot and beer in my hand—I called to them—they did not stop—I went on till the publican came up with me—I said, "Will you take the pot?"—he said, "No"—I put it down, and went on with the females across the heath—I was not sober.
NOT GUILTY .

1974. MARY HARRIS and MARY DAVIS were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 24 yards of printed cotton, value 18 s., the goods of John Goss Fleay.
JANE SMITH . I an the wife of Saul Smith, a labourer, of Hanover-street, Lewisham. I was passing the shop of Mr. Fleay, in the Broadway, Deptford, on the afternoon of the 17th of May—the prisoners were both tending together, and Davis said to Harris, "You go in there"—Harris then went inside the lobby, and stood there with a basket on her arm—she looked first to the left and then to the right—in the mean time Davis took this print from the left-hand side of the lobby, inside, put it into her apron, and went away—Harris stopped a minute or two afterwards, and then walked away in the same direction—I did not see that she joined her—she could see what she was about, as she stood in the midway, and Davis was just inside—Harris did not offer to buy any thing, or do any thing—she walked on with the basket of eggs—I gave information, and pointed out Harris.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How many persons were about the shop? A. There might be four or five—I did not see any gentleman—Harris had a basket-full of eggs—I do not know whether she was selling them—she did not attempt to touch any thing—the whole thing took place in a minute—Harris went twenty or thirty yards—I saw the policeman take her.
GEORGE SOLE ( police-constable R 174) I was on duty in the Broadway, Deptford, on the 17th of May—I received information—I ran up the Kent-road, and overtook Davis about a mile from the shop—I found this print in her apron.
JOHN TUBB . I am assistant to Mr. John Goss Fleay—I followed Harris, and gave her into custody—she said she knew nothing of it—she did not say whether she had been in the shop—I took her back—I then went in pursuit of Davis, and found her with this print.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not ask Harris what she had got in her basket? A. I do not remember doing so—I could see what she had got—I think I told the Magistrate that she muttered something, that she had no friend, and that she was alone—she cried, and said she knew nothing about it—there was no covering over her basket.
CHRISTOPHER WHITE ( police-constable R 151.) I took Harris—Tubb said, "I give this person in charge; she has been with another and taken a piece of print, take her, and I will go after the other"—while we were going to the station-house, she said she had not been in Mr. Fleay's shop at all, and knew nothing of the robbery, that she was a stranger, and came to sell her eggs.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you examined before the Magistrate? A. Yet (Davis pleaded poverty.)
HARRIS— NOT GUILTY .
DAVIS — GUILTY . Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.

1975. WILLIAM MAYBIN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) On the 20th of May I was at Greenwich fair about a quarter past ten o'clock at night, and saw two gentlemen passing—the prisoner and another were following behind them—I saw the prisoner put his hand into a gentleman's pocket, and take out this handkerchief—I seized him, and took it out of his hand, and told the gentleman—he wanted me to give him the handkerchief back—I asked him his name and address—he would not give me his name; but his friend gave me the name of "S. Ward, Knight Ryder-street, Doctors Commons"—I have been there—his friend lives there; but he refused to give me the name of the person who was robbed—it remains unknown to me—I found another silk handkerchief in the prisoner's pocket, with the mark of "M. Y. Z." on it.
WILLIAM CHING ( police-constable N 61.) I was in the fair—I noticed the prisoner, and saw him take the handkerchief—my brother constable seized him, and took it from his hand—it was claimed by the gentleman—the prisoner said he had made a mistake, it was not his hand that was in the pocket.
Prisoner. The gentleman did not claim the handkerchief—he was intoxicated. Witness. He was sober—he said he had lost his handkerchief, and that was his.
Prisoner. It was quite dark—I was walking along—Kemp came and said, "Where did you get this handkerchief?"—I said, "It was thrown on my shoulder."
GEORGE KEMP . It was clenched in his hand—and it was no sooner out of the man's pocket than I took him.

1978. HENRY FEWTRELL and JAMES BEATON were indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of May, 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of John Riley, from his person.
JOHN RILEY . I am a compositor. I was at Greenwich-fair on the 22nd of May, looking at one of the shows with a friend—I heard a noise behind—I turned, and saw the handkerchief in the officer's hand—I think it is mine—it was in my coat pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Have you any mark on it? A. No, I had seen it about twenty minutes before.
RICHARD COLLER ( police-constable 110 B.) I was at the fair in plain clothes—I watched the prisoners together for a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes—I saw them try several person's pockets, lift them up, and weigh them, and put their hands in—I then saw Beaton go to the prosecutor and partly take his handkerchief out—then Fewtrell went and took it quite out—we took them both.
Cross-examined. Q. How many pockets did you see them try? A. Five or six—I did not take them at that time, because the Magistrates do not like to have prisoners for attempting to pick pockets.
JAMES FOWLER ( police-constable 84 B.) I was in plain clothes—I observed the prisoners try or attempt to pick several gentlemen's pockets—I then saw Beaton go to the prosecutor, take his handkerchief partly out, then Fewtrell went up and drew it quite out—we seized hold of him, and when the prosecutor turned, the handkerchief was in Coller's hand.
Cross-examined. Q. Did Coller say any thing to you, or you to him? A. I said, "These two are attempting several pockets, we had better watch them"—it is a rule that I adopt not to take persons when we see them try to pick pockets, if we do, the whole testimony rests with us.
FEWTRELL — GUILTY . Aged 18. BEATON — GUILTY . Aged 16.
Confined Six Months.

1979. HENRY JAMES GARDINER was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of May, 1 plank of wood, value 7 s. the goods of James Fyffe, and another; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
DONALD STEWART ( police-constable E 35.) I was on duty on the 31st of May at Deptford. At a quarter past ten o'clock at night, I met the prisoner carrying a plank about one hundred yards from the prosecutor's—I asked How he came by it—he said he had taken it from a beer-shop, and was taking it to Old King-street, that it belonged to Mr. Sanders, and had been kept in a house in Butcher-row—I took him.
THOMAS WALEDGE . I am a shipwright. This plank is mine—I purchased it and worked it in a barge that belonged to Mr. Fyffe, at the Pier wharf, Deptford—I left it safe at half-past eight o'clock in the evening of the 31st—I knew the prisoner, but he did not work there.
JAMES FYFFE, JUN . I am in partnership with my father—this plane belongs to us—the prisoner was not in our employ.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a man who said he would give me 6 d. to carry it to King-street—I said I would carry it for 9 d.—he said, "Who are you working for?"—I said, "Mr. Sanders"—he said, "If a police? man stops you say it is your master's, and I will give you 1 s."
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant R 15.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18390617-1980]
1980. WILLIAM SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s. 6 d. the goods of Adolphus Brome Radden, from his person.
ADOLPHUS BROME RADDEN . I am a copper-plate printer. I was at Greenwich fair on the 20th of May, facing Richardson's show—I felt a tug at my pocket—I thought there was somebody pressing me, but I took no notice—I then felt it again—I turned, and saw the prisoner close behind me—I took hold of him, and took my handkerchief from his breast—I gave him to the policeman—this is my handkerchief.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked it up, and asked a gentleman if it was his—he said "No," and then this gentleman took me. GUILTY .** Aged 14.— Transported for Seven Years.—Convict Ship.
[trialAccount t18390617-1981]
1981. MARY ROBERTS was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 1 bed, value 15 s.; 2 rugs, value 2 s.; and 3 sheets, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Jackson.—2nd COUNT stating them to be the goods of Louisa Jackson.
LOUISA JACKSON . I am the wife of Thomas Jackson, a marine-store dealer—he lives at Deptford—we let the prisoner a ready-furnished room—she had a husband living there—on the 15th of April I went out, and when I came home the prisoner was gone, and all these things—I never
permitted her to pawn them—she and her husband were with me about two months this last time, but they lived with me last summer—her husband kept her—he could earn about 4 l. a week.
Prisoner. She gave me permission to pawn the bed. Witness. I did not.
MARY ANN RUSSELL . I live in Church-street, Deptford, and keep a second-hand clothes-shop. I bought this rug of the prisoner for 9 d.—she cried, and said it was all through her husband.
GEORGE DAVIS . I am in the service of Mr. Tighe, a pawnbroker at Deptford. This bed, sheets, and one rug were pawned by the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I intended to get them out again. GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18390617-1982]
1982. JOHN WILCOCKS was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of May, 3/4 lb. weight of leather, value 1 s., the goods of Henry Crossley; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES CROSSLEY . I am the son of Henry Crossley, a currier, in Church-street, Deptford. On the 13th of May, the prisoner came and purchased some leather—my father was serving him, he had occasion to torn his back to serve another customer, and I saw the prisoner put this piece of leather into the basket which he had—he left the shop—I followed and gave him into custody of the policeman, who found the leather in his basket.
Cross-examined by MR. PATHS. Q. Was he sober? A. Yes; he did not seem as if he had been drinking—I was at the back of the desk—I was in such a situation that I could see well, I was about fife yards from him—my father went out of the shop into the adjoining room—it is a long shop—when the leather was found be offered to pay for it—it was worth 1 s.
GEORGE GOOD ( police-constable R 114.) I took the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Did he appear to be sober? A. Yes, quite.
WILLIAM SMITH . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read)—I was present at the trial—he is the person—he had three months imprisonment.
GUILTY . Aged 44.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390617-1983]
1983. ALEMINICK NEWMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of May, 26lbs. weight of iron, value 1 s. 3 d., the goods of Frank Clark Hills.
MR. BALLANTINR conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH BIMKIN . On the 29th of May I saw the prisoner coming out of Mr. Hills' premises, at Deptford—I asked what business he had been doing—he said nothing—I said, "You have got your pocket loaded with something"—he said it was nothing belonging to any one—I saw a piece of iron out of his pocket—he stood still—I told him if he did not empty his pocket I would call Mr. Walton, the clerk—he did not, and I told a person to fetch my husband—while he was gone the prisoner took out the pieces of iron from his pocket, and laid them on the ground a short distance from where I stood—he placed the iron against the gate, and walked away.
JOHN WALKER ( police-constable R 182.) I took the prisoner, on the 29th of May, in the lower part of Deptford, four or five hours after this
happened—on the road to the station-house he said he found the iron in the bed of the river.
HORTON HARRILD . I am foreman to Mr. Frank Clark Hills, manufacturing chemist. He has been constructing a steam engine, and has a great quantity of iron on his premises—the place where the prisoner was stopped, was on the premises—this iron is similar to what was on the premises.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.
[trialAccount t18390617-1984]
1984. ALEMINICK NEWMAN was again indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May, 221bs. weight of iron, value 1 s. the goods of Frank Clark Hills.
MARY ANN SMITH . HORTON HARRILD . I have examined this iron—it is my master's. GUILTY . Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.

1985. JAMES BUTT was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of May, 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of John Robson, from his person.—2nd COUNT stating it to be the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
FREDERICK BLASTON . I am a corporal in the Royal Marines. I was at Greenwich fair on the 21st of May, between five and six o'clock in the afternoon—I observed the prisoner put his hand into a gentleman's pocket and take a blue handkerchief out—I touched the gentleman, and told him—a constable by my side took the prisoner—he dropped it from under his coat—the gentleman gave his name, John Robson, in the presence of the prisoner—I am sure this is the handkerchief—I put two bits of string on it, to know it again—I read the gentleman's card—he was a hat-finisher, and lodged in Grange-walk, Bermondsey.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was there a crowd? A. Yes—I have never seen the gentleman since—he went off after he had been before the Magistrate.
JOHN JAMES BARNARD ( police-constable G 110.) I was there, and saw Blaston touch Robson—the prisoner made off from a man, and I took him—he dropped this handkerchief from under his coat on the ground—the man, in presence of the prisoner, said his name was John Robson, and this was his handkerchief—I found four other handkerchiefs on him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390617-1986]
1986. SARAH PRESTON was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of May, 1 gown, value 8 s., 1 bonnet, value 10 s., and 1 shawl, value 13 s., the goods of James Connor: and 1 gown, value 1 s., 1 petticoat, value 2 s., and 1 pair of shoes, value 2 s. the goods of John Frederick Myers.
JANE MYERS . I am the wife of John Frederick Myers, and live in New King-street, Deptford—the prisoner was four days in Mr. Connor's house, where I lodged—she slept in my apartment on the night of the 21st of March—I went out at five o'clock the next morning, leaving her in the room, and returned between eight and nine o'clock in the evening—I missed these things.
Prisoner. They were lent to me to go to the fair—I went there on Monday,
Tuesday, and Wednesday—Mrs. Connor fastened the gown on me.. Witness. I went with her on the Tuesday evening, but I did not lend her my clothes—she came on a visit to her mother, and I let her sleep in my bed, as my husband was out.
JANE CONNOR . I am the wife of James Connor—I keep the house—my husband is a police-officer in the R division—Myers lodged with me—I lost a bonnet, and a gown, and a ticket of a shawl, on Wednesday, the 22nd of May, out of Myers's room—this is the bonnet—it was new when the prisoner took it—I lent her a bonnet on the Monday—I lent her none of Myers's things.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you see her with the gown on? A. About half-past twelve o'clock—I said, "What are you doing with it on?" she said, "Mrs. Myers gave me leave to put it on till the evening"—I did not see her again till the 6th of June.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant R 15.)The prisoner was given into my custody on the 6th of June—she said she took the things without the intention of stealing them, she meant to return them, but when she got to Dover she was robbed—I asked her of what, she said three sovereigns, which a gentleman gave her—she was sent up by the Magistrate of Dover.
MART WALKER . I am female searcher at the Greenwich station-house—on the 6th of June the prisoner was brought there—I found on her the gown, petticoat, shoes, and bonnet.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Nine Months.
