[trialAccount t18390617-1988]
1988. WILLIAM LANE was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Kyle, the elder, on the 19th of April, at St. Giles's, Camberwell, and stealing therein, 1 coat, value 7 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 3 s.; and 3 gowns, value 20 s.; his goods.
MARY KYLE . I am the wife of John Kyle, and live at Surrey canal bridge. I left my house at half-past six o'clock in the morning of the 19th of April, leaving my son at home—I heard of the robbery at four
o'clock in the afternoon—I returned about nine o'clock, and found my son at home—I saw my box, which I had left secure when I went out—it was not locked—I had left the articles stated in the indictment in it—they were gone—I found them at the station-house that evening—these are the things—I do not know the prisoner—I lost a blackbird at the same time.
JOHN KYLE, JUN . On the 19th of April my mother went out and left me at home—I went out between seven and eight o'clock in the morning—I locked the street door, and put the key in my pocket—the window was shut—my mother's box was just as it used to be—I returned at four o'clock in the afternoon—I found the window wide open, the flower-pots down on the ground, and the table away from the window where it had been—I got in at the window, and went up stairs, and saw the box open, and all the things about the room—the bed was pulled to pieces, my mother's bonnet-box down, and my father's hat-box—the prisoner was a stranger.
JOHN LEDGER ( police-constable R 130.) I was on duty between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, on the 19th of April—I fell in with the prisoner in company with another sweep on the canal bridge—the prisoner had got the sack and the machine in his hand—they were standing near the bridge, I was going towards the Green Man public-house—I returned, and saw them on the other side of the way—I suspected there was something in the tack that was not soot, as they laid so light—they saw me, and went down Peckham-lane, walking at the rate of better than four miles an hour—I walked as fast as I could till they came to the canal bridge public-house—there I lost sight of them—I ran, and got sight of them again—they turned another street, and I lost sight of them—I again saw them—the prisoner was about to run, and I stopped him, and found this property in the sack which he dropped—I asked what he had got in it—he said, "Nothing"—I took him to the station-house—the prosecutor's house is in the parish of St. Giles, Camberwell.
Prisoner. Q. Did I run—did you not say if I offered to run you bad something in your pocket that would stop me? A. You ran about two yards.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the sweep—he had something in a cloth—I went down a turning, and he said, when I came up, "I have shot a load of soot in your sack"—I did not know what it was—I took it up, and was going on, but was taken.
JOHN LEDGER re-examined. If I had had any body to help me I could have taken the other one, who let the blackbird go, which they stole from the prosecutor's.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18390617-1989]
1989. WILLIAM STANFORD was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of May, 2 pails, value 2 s.; and 1 horse-cloth, value 1 s.; the goods of Joseph Mosely, his master.
DAVID BEDSON . I am a constable of Corydon—the prosecutor lives at Norwood. On the 29th of April I was on duty at a quarter to one o'clock in the morning, and stopped the prisoner about three quarters of a mile from the prosecutor's house—he was moving goods—he had as many things as you could wheel in a barrow—the two pails and the horsecloth, which were among the things, were claimed by his master.
JOSEPH MOSELY . I live at Norwood, and am a coach-proprietor. The prisoner lived with me four years as a horse-keeper—he had left me in October—I frequently missed pails and horsecloths, but I did not suspect him—I believe he was out of employ—these things are mine—they have my name on them.
Prisoner's Defence. These two pails I used while I wan with him in carrying water home—I forgot to take them back, and the cloth I chucked on my shoulder, because it rained—I forgot to take it back. GUILTY . Aged 22.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabia.
[trialAccount t18390617-1990]
1990. SAMUEL M'KEON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of May, 1 coat, value 2 l. 5 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 5 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 20 s.; 1 sovereign, and 15 shillings; the goods and monies of John Lambert, his master, in his dwelling-house.
JOHN LAMBERT . I am a carver, and live in Pitt-street, Old Kent-road. The prisoner was my journeyman for two or three months—I missed this property on the 10th of May, from a chest of drawers in the front parlour—the prisoner went away that day, without giving me notice—I paid him at the end of every week, and this was on Monday morning—I had left him at work—I found him at the station-house, with my coat and trowsers on—I examined, and missed a sovereign and fifteen shillings from the top drawer, and the clothes—he behaved very well while with me.
MARY LAMBERT . I am the prosecutor's wife. I put a sovereign and fifteen shillings into the top drawer a few weeks ago, towards our rent—I did not miss it till last Monday week.
JAMES PARRY . I am a policeman. On Tuesday evening, the 11th of May, I was on duty in Broadway, and saw the prisoner there drunk—I had received information, and took him, with these clothes on him—he said he had come from Birmingham, and denied all knowledge of Mr. Lambert. ( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18390617-1991]
1991. WILLIAM JAMES DALEY was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Daniel Godfrey, on the 25th of May, and cutting and wounding him on the head, with intent to maim and disable him.—2nd COUNT stating his intent to be to do him some grievous bodily harm.
DANIEL GODFREY . I am a corn-dealer, and live in Lower Marsh, Lambeth, at the corner of Coleman's-passage, where the prisoner lives. On the night of the 25th of May, about ten o'clock, I was in my shop, and saw Mrs. Emanuel, a neighbour, in the passage—the prisoner and her met in the passage, he took hold of her arm, gave her a shake, and then shoved her up against the corner—I thought it was done with intent to insult her—I heard her say, "You blackguard," or something, and she went in and called her husband out—the prisoner went down to his own door—Mr. Emanuel went down to him, and they had high words—I heard that while I was in my shop—I afterwards went out, and saw the prisoner standing at his own door, and using bad language—he said, "I will do for them," using a very bad word—he went into his house, and in about five minutes he came out again, with a handkerchief in his hand—I was
not aware there was any thing in the handkerchief—he repeated the same words again, and Mr. Emanuel's man stood by the side of him—I went up to him, and said, "What do you mean? who do you mean to do for?"—he said, "You, sir, if you don't mind your own business"—he used bad language—I said I thought he was a clever fellow, too much so to do any good—I said, "We had a specimen of your incivility just now in insulting Mrs. Emanuel"—I had scarcely said the words before he up with his handkerchief, and something in his hand, and hit me with it on the head—he had something in it as hard as a stone—I closed in on him, to prevent his hitting me—his wife came out and hit me on the side with a hammer, or something—I let him go—he then struck me on the head again, just over the temple—I was stunned, and recollect nothing more—when I recovered people were leading me into my house—my head was very much wounded—there were two wounds—I think I lost a pint or a pint and a half of blood—I was attended by a surgeon.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You told him he was a clever fellow, and that you thought he was too clever for himself? A. Yes—I had no quarrel with him that day—I had on the Sunday evening—he did not complain to my landlord of me, that I know of—my landlord had not given me notice to quit—I had only one quarrel with the prisoner—I did not sweep my dirt and straw up against his door—when I heard him using abusive language I went and asked him what he meant—I did not strike him, to my knowledge—I will swear I did not—he was not struck in my presence, I will swear that—his wife and he did not call out "murder," to my knowledge—I was insensible part of the time—I called out "murder"—there might be one or two hundred people there—the passage was full—I do not know Frances Harwood, nor Mary Ann White, nor Edward Skelton—Mr. Shears has two or three apprentices—I do not know their names.
MICHAEL BARNETT . I am a clothes salesman, and live with Mr. Emanuel, in Lower Marsh. I was standing at my master's door—Mr. Godfrey came out, and said some words to Daley about a woman—Daley went in-doors, brought something out in his hand, made use of a very bad expression, and said, "I will do for you"—I afterwards saw Godfrey go and speak to him, and ask what he meant—he had gone down the court three or four minutes, and I saw Daley strike him two blows, and he was in the act of striking him a third, when I seized his hand, and saw he had a mallet in it—after that his wife rushed out, and made a thrust at Godfrey with something, and struck him—she was making a second blow, when I seized her arm, and she bit me with her teeth.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you know Mr. Shears's house? A. No, I do not know such a person—I know the prisoner's house—a hatter lives next door—I do not know his name—the prisoner is a printer—I cannot say whether this mallet is used in his business—I did not set him come from his own house, and go to the door where the hatter lives—he was standing by his own door when Godfrey went up to him—I did not strike Daley at all—I cannot say whether Mr. Emanuel did—I did not see him there at all—I was standing at my employer's door when I first saw Mr. Godfrey go from his own house to the prisoner's—it is about ten or fifteen feet from Godfrey's to the prisoner's—Emanuel was serving a customer at the time Godfrey went to the prisoner—I came out, and he went in—there were others of our persuasion there—after the occurrence I saw
the mallet open in the prisoner's hand—I saw no handkerchief—I never saw him struck by any body—there is a coal-shed lower down—a person standing there might see all that happened—I do not know Patterson or Harwood.
HENEY LEUTCHFORD ( police-constable L 87.) On Saturday evening, the 25th of May, I was at the Marsh—I heard the disturbance, and saw a mob of people collected in Coleman's-place—I went to it, and saw Mr. Godfrey coming out of the mob, bleeding from the bead, and at the same time saw the prisoner in custody of my brother constable—he said, "Take this man to the station-house, for he has struck Mr. Godfrey with a mallet," producing it at the same time—I took hold of the prisoner's arm, and brought him out of the mob—he said he would not go—I said he must go—he was a little violent, and I held him pretty tight—I said I thought it a pretty weapon to strike a man with—he said he meant to do it, for he went and fetched it for the purpose.
JOHN FENN ( police-constable L 154.) On the night in question I heard the cry of police and murder in Coleman's-buildings—I ran there and saw Mr. Godfrey in the mob bleeding from wounds received in the head—I did not see the prisoner at the moment, be had the mallet in his hands, and parties standing there said, "For God's sake, policman, see what this man has got in his hand"—I turned round, and saw him with his arm up, attempting to make another stroke, I suppose at Mr. Godfrey, with the mallet in his hand—I then closed on him, and took the mallet from him—he had a handkerchief round the handle of the mallet, but the upper part of the mallet was quite clear—his wife then closed on me, and endeavoured to pull me away from him—he at the same time give his shoulders a bit of a shift, and endeavoured to slip his coat off—he got it off as far as the wrists—I had a hard struggle to take him, but I did, I brought him out of the crowd, and delivered him to my brother constable.
JOHN PETER BURY . I am a surgeon. I was called on between ten and eleven o'clock, and saw the prosecutor in his own parlour—I found two severe lacerated wounds on the left side of the temple, made by some blunt instrument—they were of a dangerous character—one branch of the temporal artery was divided—he was a week before he recovered entirely—the wounds were nearly healed then, but he was very nervous and ill after that.
MR. PHILLIPS called
JOHN PATTERSON . I am a printer. On the Saturday night in question I went to the prisoner's house, about ten o'clock, to call for Mr. Harwood, who lodges there, to go to an Odd Fellows' lodge with me—I went in at the shop-door (there is no private entrance,) and saw Mr. Daley, at work at the stone, or imposing-stone, as we call it—he is a master printer—he had a mallet in his hand, which is obliged to be used in the business—he requested me to remain at his shop-door, whilst be went to a neighbour's next door—it is Mr. Daley's own house, but Mr. Shears, a lodger, occupies the back part—he went out, taking the mallet in his hand, forgetting it—it was not wrapped in a handkerchief, but bare—I saw him knock at Shears's door—Mr. Godfrey came by me as I stood at the prisoner's door—he rushed past me in a state of fury—I heard him exclaim something about Irish as he went down the court, but what it was I cannot say—he made up to Daley at full speed, in a fighting attitude, and hit him right and
not aware there was any thing in the handkerchief—he repeated the same words again, and Mr. Emanuel's man stood by the side of him—I went up to him, and said, "What do you mean? who do you mean to do for?"—he said, "You, sir, if you don't mind your own business"—he used bad language—I said I thought he was a clever fellow, too much so to do any good—I said, "We had a specimen of your incivility just now in insulting Mrs. Emanuel"—I had scarcely said the words before he up with his handkerchief, and something in his hand, and hit me with it on the head—he had something in it as hard as a stone—I closed in on him, to prevent his hitting me—his wife came out and hit me on the side with a hammer, or something—I let him go—he then struck me on the head again, just over the temple—I was stunned, and recollect nothing more—when I recovered people were leading me into my house—my head was very much wounded—there were two wounds—I think I lost a pint or a pint and a half of blood—I was attended by a surgeon.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You told him he was a clever fellow, and that you thought he was too clever for himself? A. Yes—I had no quarrel with him that day—I had on the Sunday evening—he did not complain to my landlord of me, that I know of—my landlord had not given me notice to quit—I had only one quarrel with the prisoner—I did not sweep my dirt and straw up against his door—when I heard him using abusive language I went and asked him what he meant—I did not strike him, to my knowledge—I will swear I did not—he was not struck in my presence, I will swear that—his wife and he did not call out "murder," to my knowledge—I was insensible part of the time—I called out "murder"—there might be one or two hundred people there—the passage was full—I do not know Frances Harwood, nor Mary Ann White, nor Edward Skelton—Mr. Shears has two or three apprentices—I do not know their names.
MICHAEL BARNETT . I am a clothes salesman, and live with Mr. Emanuel, in Lower Marsh. I was standing at my master's door—Mr. Godfrey came out, and said some words to Daley about a woman—Daley went in-doors, brought something out in his hand, made use of a very bad expression, and said, "I will do for you"—I afterwards saw Godfrey go and speak to him, and ask what he meant—he had gone down the court three or four minutes, and I saw Daley strike him two blows, and he was in the act of striking him a third, when I seized his hand, and saw he had a mallet in it—after that his wife rushed out, and made a thrust at Godfrey with something, and struck him—she was making a second blow, when I seized her arm, and she bit me with her teeth.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you know Mr. Shears's house? A. No, I do not know such a person—I know the prisoner's house—a hatter lives next door—I do not know his name—the prisoner is a printer—I cannot say whether this mallet is used in his business—I did not see him come from his own house, and go to the door where the hatter lives—he was standing by his own door when Godfrey went up to him—I did not strike Daley at all—I cannot say whether Mr. Emanuel did—I did not we him there at all—I was standing at my employer's door when I first saw Mr. Godfrey go from his own house to the prisoner's—it is about ten or fifteen feet from Godfrey's to the prisoner's—Emanuel was serving a customer at the time Godfrey went to the prisoner—I came out, and he went in—there were others of our persuasion there—after the occurrence I saw
the mallet open in the prisoner's hand—I saw no handkerchief—I never saw him struck by any body—there is a coal-shed lower down—a person standing there might see all that happened—I do not know Patterson or Harwood.
HENRY LEUTCHFORD ( police-constable L 87.) On Saturday evening, the 25th of May, I was at the Marsh—I heard the disturbance, and saw a mob of people collected in Coleman's-place—I went to it, and saw Mr. Godfrey coming out of the mob, bleeding from the head, and at the same time saw the prisoner in custody of my brother constable—he said, "Take this man to the station-house, for he has struck Mr. Godfrey with a mallet," producing it at the same time—I took hold of the prisoner's arm, and brought him out of the mob—he said he would not go—I said he must go—he was a little violent, and I held him pretty tight—I said I thought it a pretty weapon to strike a man with—he said he meant to do it, for he went and fetched it for the purpose.
JOHN FENN ( police-constable L 154.) On the night in question, I heard the cry of police and murder in Coleman's-buildings—I ran there and saw Mr. Godfrey in the mob bleeding from wounds received in the head—I did not see the prisoner at the moment, he had the mallet in his hands, and parties standing there said, "For God's sake, policeman, see what this man has got in his hand"—I turned round, and saw him with his arm up, attempting to make another stroke, I suppose at Mr. Godfrey, with the mallet in his hand—I then closed on him, and took the mallet from him—he had a handkerchief round the handle of the mallet, but the upper part of the mallet was quite clear—his wife then closed on me, and endeavoured to pull me away from him—he at the same time gave his shoulders a bit of a shift, and endeavoured to slip his coat off—he got it off as far as the wrists—I had a hard struggle to take him, but I did, I brought him out of the crowd, and delivered him to my brother constable.
JOHN PETER BURY . I am a surgeon. I was called on between ten and eleven o'clock, and saw the prosecutor in his own parlour—I found two severe lacerated wounds on the left side of the temple, made by some blunt instrument—they were of a dangerous character—one branch of the temporal artery was divided—he was a week before he recovered entirely—the wounds were nearly healed then, but he was very nervous and ill after that.
MR. PHILLIPS called
JOHN PATTERSON . I am a printer. On the Saturday night in question I went to the prisoner's house, about ten o'clock, to call for Mr. Harwood, who lodges there, to go to an Odd Fellows' lodge with me—I went in at the shop-door (there is no private entrance,) and saw Mr. Daley at work at the stone, or imposing-stone, as we call it—he is a master printer—he had a mallet in his hand, which is obliged to be used in the business—he requested me to remain at his shop-door, whilst he went to a neighbour's next door—it is Mr. Daley's own house, but Mr. Shears, a lodger, occupies the back part—he went out, taking the mallet in his hand, forgetting it—it was not wrapped in a handkerchief, but bare—I saw him knock at Shears's door—Mr. Godfrey came by me as I stood at the prisoner's door—he rushed past me in a state of fury—I heard him exclaim something about Irish as he went down the court, but what it was I cannot say—he made up to Daley at full speed, in a fighting attitude, and hit him right and
left, while he stood at the door—Daley made no answer or resentment, till two Jews, Emanuel and Barnett, came down, and pitched into him there and then, as he stood, all three of them—I cannot say where Godfrey struck him—I went away upon this, for I had been held to bail to keep the peace towards Mr. Emanuel and the witness Barnett, not towards Godfrey—I called "Police," and went into the Lower Marsh—the prisoner did not strike Godfrey with the mallet before Godfrey struck him—he was struck first—the court was soon full of people.
MARY ANN WHITE . I live in Mount-row, Westminster-road. On the night in question, between ten and eleven o'clock, I was standing at the coal-shed, next door but one to Daley's house, and next door to Shears's—I saw the prisoner standing at Shears's door—I saw Godfrey come down from the end of the place, go up to Daley, and strike him in the face—after that I saw two or three men rush up, but what occurred afterwards I cannot say—a mob collected, but I did not go from where I was standing—Daley did not strike Godfrey at all, before Godfrey and the other men struck him.
EDWARD SKELTON . I am apprentice to Mr. Shears, a silk hatter. On this Saturday night, about half-past ten o'clock, I saw Daley and Godfrey struggling together, about six yards from my master's door—there were no others there at that time, but two or three minutes afterwards, two or three came from the corner, and a general struggle ensued—the confusion was so great that is all I saw.
ISAAC HARWOOD . I am a printer. I did not see any thing of this affair—this is a printer's mallet—I saw a quarrel between Daley and Godfrey the week previous.
FRANCES HARWOOD . I and my husband lodge in the prisoner's house. On the Saturday night this occurred I was at the shop between ten and eleven o'clock—I was coming out of Daley's shop-door, and saw Daley standing a few doors off, and Godfrey at the corner of the court—all of a sudden, Godfrey rushed towards Daley, apparently in a very great passion' and used the words, "You Irish b—, I will serve you out"—he then rushed on him, and hit him violently with both his hands—two or three other men then came up, and fell on him also—I screamed "Murder," being very much frightened, and in about five minutes the police came up—he was then taken to the station-house, and I went with him, at his wife's request, seeing the whole occurrence—I did not see Daley strike Godfrey at all.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy. Confined Two Months.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18390617-1992]
1992. WILLIAM DUNN was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Ann Dunn.
RICHARD THOMAS RUSSELL . I am a surgeon. On Saturday the 8th of June, I was called in to attend Ann Dunn, about two o'clock in the afternoon, she complained of a pain in her head—I did not examine her head at that time—I found her tongue was foul, and the state of the bowels confined—I sent her some medicine, and at one o'clock on Sunday morning I was sent for again—I found her in a state of insensibility, and I applied leeches to the temple—I saw her again about ten o'clock in the morning—she still remained insensible—she died on Thursday, the 13th of June—she was insensible till the Wednesday afternoon—
she was only sensible at intervals that afternoon, when she was roused—after death I examined the body—on the back of the head there were marks of violence—there was a bruise on the whole of the back of the bead, the back of the ears, and the ears them selves—I removed the integuments, and found the covering of the skull exhibited the same dark appearances, and there was a partial extravasation of blood—I removed the skull, and there was extravasation of blood at the base of the brain—that was the cause of death—I examined the rest of the body, and there was nothing else to account for death—I judge death arose from the bruise at the back of the head—it was a recent wound—on the Sunday I cut her hair off, and found the head tumified, which proved it was recent—I should judge a blow must have been given within about forty-eight hours of the Sunday morning—I mean the blow which caused death—the effusion of blood on the base of the brain would be caused by such a bruise.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I fetch you myself? A. I was sent for by one; of the lodgers at first—you fetched me at one o'clock in the morning.
CHARLOTTE FOX . I know the prisoner and his wife Ann Dunn—I lived in the same house with them, No. 10, Charles-street, Horsleydown—they occupied the down-stairs room, and I the up-stairs chamber, immediately above—I have heard quarrels between them—the last quarrel I heard was on Thursday the 6th of June—I heard a scuffle, as if somebody was fighting very desperately, and then a fall, as if somebedy was pushed against a partition—they had no children, and lived alone—I never saw him strike her—this was at seven o'clock in the evening—I had seen her at three o'clock in the afternoon—she seemed very poorly then, and complained of pain in her head—I did not observe whether she complained of any particular part of her head—I never saw her drunk—on the Friday she complained of dreadful pains in the head—I went for the surgeon on the Saturday—I met her about nine o'clock on Friday night—she had then been to the chemist to procure a powder, and she had her head tied up—she had not her head tied up before that—I did not hear any disturbance between her and her husband on the Friday or Saturday.
Prisoner. Q. Do you remember coming to me at six o'clock on Friday night when I went to the doctor's with her, and he gave her a powder? A. No doctor saw her till the Saturday.
MARY PINK . I lived in the same house as the prisoner and his wife—I never saw him ill-use her—I have heard noises in their room when they were alone at all times—the last time I heard any thing was on the Thursday, when Mrs. Fox heard it—I heard her complain of bad pains in her head about three o'clock on Thursday afternoon—that, was the first time I had heard her complain of pain in the head—at dinner time, one o'clock, I heard the scuffling as if they were fighting—there was nobody in the room but their two selves—I saw them go in—I do not know that she was in the habit of fighting with her husband—I have often heard them scuffling and fighting before—he used to use bad language to her—I never heard her do so to him.
HANNAH ALPIN . I live in the same house—I was out a good deal at work, but was at home at tea-time and at night—last Friday fortnight I heard a noise between the prisoner and his wife, about eleven o'clock at night—he used her most cruelly—I was in bed—he beat her, and I heard her scream most dreadfully—it was on the Friday night as she was taken ill on the Saturday—that was the last I heard.
Prisoner. Q. Are you sure it was Friday night? did not I sit up all night with her? A. Not the Friday night before she was taken ill—I know nothing of her being intoxicated—they were both in the habit of drinking—she went with his lunch every day at eleven o'clock, and had something to drink with him.
ANN BUCKNER . I lived in this house, but left five weeks ago—I know of nothing since the beginning of May—the deceased was drunk many times while I was in the house—she pawned her husband's things, and when he came home he would give her blows for doing so, and being drunk.
GEORGE HOOPER . I am a policeman. On Saturday morning, the 8th of June, between one and two o'clock, I was near the prisoner's house, and heard a female scream in the passage—I was on the opposite side—I do not know who it was that screamed—I went towards the door, and heard voices in the passage, and then heard apparently as if a blow was struck, and the party who received it fall down—there was then a call for the parties who live up-stairs—they were some time before they received an answer—after they received an answer the person in the first-floor could not get a light—after a short time they obtained one, and came down stairs—I did not see who it was, I only heard—I did not go into the house as there were no screams of murder—I could see the light brought from the first-floor down stairs.
Prisoner. You cannot say it was me or my wife. Witness. No, I cannot say who it was.
CHARLOTTE FOX re-examined. I live in the first-floor—there are three rooms in the house, one above another—I did not come down with a light on Friday night or Saturday morning, nor did I hear any scream—I was in bed, and in the house all night—there was no scream or noise that I heard—I am quite sure of that.
MARY PINK re-examined. I did not hear any disturbance on Friday night or Saturday morning—I did not come down with a light—I did not hear any noise or scuffling at one o'clock in the morning.
WILLIAM HUTCHINS . I live next door to this house—I knew the deceased—I have seen her drunk many times—nearly a month ago I found her lying across the street insensibly drunk—I took her up, knocked at her door, her husband came on and assisted me with her into the passage—there was very bad language between them both, and both used to get drunk—I know nothing of any blows being struck within a week of the 8th of June.
JOHN WHITBURN . I am the prisoner's cousin—I know nothing of any scuffle between them shortly before the 8th of June—they were always quarrelling and wrangling—she used to threaten him—he used to cry to me, and say she was always the instigation of her ill-treatment.
( The prisoner in a long defence, stated that his wife was constantly drunk, she pawned all his things, and never did any thing for him—that she had long before complained of her head—he had been to various doctors about it, who had said it would carry her off—and that she was constantly in the habit of falling about, being in a state of continual intoxication.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18390617-1993]
1993. RICHARD BAILEY was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Mary Chrisholm, on the 11th of June, and cutting and wounding her upon her neck, throat, and chin, with intent to kill and murder her.—2nd COUNT stating his intent to be to maim and disable her.—3rd COUNT to do her some grievous bodily harm.
MARY CHRISHOLM . I live at 168, Kent-street. I am a widow—I lived with the prisoner as his wife for two years—on Tuesday, the 11th of June, some words passed between us between two and three o'clock in the afternoon—the prisoner went out after that—I went to bed during his absence—about half-past ten o'clock—I was awoke from my sleep in the course of the night, I cannot tell about what time it was I was awoke by something cutting me across the throat—I got up, and ran down stairs and opened the door—I cannot tell who did it—I saw no one—I cannot tell whether there was any body near the bed, man, or woman—it was dark—as I was coming down stairs I received a wound in the arm—I cannot tell whether it was done with a knife, I was too frightened—it appeared to be the cut of a knife—I opened the door, and called "Murder" once—I could call no more—I fell down—a surgeon afterwards attended me—I went to the hospital on Tuesday morning, and remained till Thursday, when I was brought here—the prisoner gets his living by selling whips.
Cross-examined by MR. THOMPSON. Q. You lived for two years with this man? A. Yes, pretty decently and comfortably up to this time—I had no child by him, but I have a child, and no one can be fonder of it than he is—we had both been drinking a good deal that day—we were both drunk—there had been high words between us in the afternoon previous to going to-bed—I might have said many things to make him angry, but I cannot say—I taunted him with the child's not being his—he was particularly fond of the child—I was carrying it in my arms—it was between two and three o'clock when I went out—this happened at twelve o'clock at night—I went to bed at half-past ten.
WILLIAM GREEN . I am a policeman. About ten minutes before twelve o'clock I was called—I saw the prosecutrix outside her door, lying on her back, bleeding profusely from the throat—I inquired who did it—the prisoner came running down stairs, and said "I did it, and meant to do it, and to do for myself afterwards"—I took him to the station-house.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the prisoner very drunk? A. Not very—he could walk—he went very quietly to the station-house.
----, I am a policeman. I saw the prosecutrix lying in the manner described by Green—she was lying on her back wounded—I said, "For God's sake, who cut that woman's throat in this way?" the prisoner came down stairs without his shoes, and said, "I done it, and I meant to do it"—I told him he must go along with me to the station-house—I said, "What did you do it with?" he said, "A knife, and I threw the knife upon the floor"—I went part of the way to assist my brother constable—I gave him to another constable, and ran back to assist the woman to the hospital—I went up stairs, and found the knife underneath the bed—( produced)—that is the knife—there are marks of blood upon it—the prisoner was the worse for liquor—he was very drunk—he was able to answer me, and avow what he had done.
JAMES JACKSON . I am dresser in St. Thomas's Hospital, Borough—I was brought up to the medical profession—the woman was brought there before twelve o'clock on Tuesday—I examined her—she had two wounds upon her throat, and one on her chin—they were simple incised wounds—
they appeared to have been cut by a knife—I did not see the wound upon her arm—I did not see any marks of blood upon her when she came—she had one mark, extending from one side of her throat, and another from the other side to the centre—it was not a continued wound—it was two wounds—the wind-pipe was not cut nor the jugular vein.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Death recorded.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

LYON LEVY . 1995. MICHAEL M'CARTHY was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 149 lbs. weight of lead, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of Thomas Farncomb.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM WALL . I am clerk to Mr. Thomas Farncomb, the proprietor of Griffin's wharf, Thames-street, Southwark. About five o'clock in the afternoon of the 14th of June, I met the prisoner coming through the warehouse with this piece of lead on his shoulder—I inquired what he bad got—he said, "A piece of lead," and he was hired by the captain of a vessel to take it for him—that he did not know the name of the captain, but he would go and find him—he laid the lead down, went to the water side, and escaped round the other gateway into the street—I saw him again about two hours after, at a short distance from the premises—he tried to escape, and wrestled with me—he was employed several times at the wharf, but not that day—this was a pig of lead, about 149 lbs.—it was taken from the water side.
THOMAS HAYWARD . I saw the man coming through the wharf—I asked him what he had got—he said, a little lead, that a captain of a vessel gave him to take—he said, "I will lay it down, and fetch the captain"—he laid it down and ran away out of the other gate—I afterwards saw Wall take him—I went and assisted him.
PATRICK M'CORMACK ( police-constable M 27.) I took the prisoner, and have the lead.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390617-1996]
1996. JAMES COOK and WILLIAM REMNANT were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 2 trusses of hay, value 6 s. the goods of Edward Rayne .
EDWARD RAYNE . I live at Merton, and am a farmer. I had thirteen trusses of hay in my barn on the 13th of May, bound, ready to go out—on the 14th, I missed two trusses—I saw them afterwards, but they were made into three bundles—I know it by the rye-straw bands, which is a very unusual thing, round the bundles, but in my barn they were in trusses—I am quite sure it is my hay.
JOSEPH HAYES . I keep a beer-shop at Merton. I saw Remnant on the 14th of May at my skittle-ground—I saw some hay there—there were several other persons there—I did not see any thing done to the hay by any body—I said nothing to Remnant—I saw Allison, a man not in custody—I asked him whose hay that was—he said, "It is mine"—there were three bundles—he said, "I have got it for the races"—I said, "I don't care what you have got it for, take it off my premises"—I afterwards saw Allison and Remnant with the bundles, each going in front of the house—I saw a third man, but I do not know whether it was Cook or not.
ISAAC BENTLY . I keep a beer-shop at Merton, about twenty yards from Hayes. On the 14th of May, I saw some hay outside my premises—Allison, Cook, and Remnant took it away—I did not see it brought.
WILLIAM GROVES . I saw the hay in the corner of my master, Mr. Hayes's field, about eleven o'clock in the day.
JOHN AYTON . I am a constable at Merton. I apprehended Cook on the 19th of May—on the morning of the 20th, (I did not make any promises nor ask any questions) he told me, in going from the watch-house to Wandsworth, that he, and Allison, and Remnant went to Mr. Rayne's barn and fetched two trusses of hay, and when they were going to a beer-shop, he would not take it any further, but had no more to do with it—another man took Remnant.
Cook. He asked me if I was with them, and if I would tell him the rights of it, it would be better for me—he said he had got witnesses to prove I was there—I said, "If you have, the way is to bring them forward. Witness. I did not.
COURT. Q. Did you never tell him you had got witnesses? A. No, I believe not—I did not tell him any such thing—I swear it—I said nothing about the hay till he spoke to me—he said he brought it as far as Blind-lane, and would not take it any further—I believe I said, "Oh, did you?"—I said, "Come, Cook; it is time you had your breakfast"
COURT. Q. Do you mean to swear deliberately that you put no question? A. Yes—I asked him if there were not three men in the barn, and he said he believed there was one.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390617-1997]
1997. WILLIAM MARKS was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 3500 lbs. weight of iron, value 10., the goods of Peter Plumley, his master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
FRANCIS HARLEY . I am clerk to Peter Plumley—he is in possession of an iron-yard, where he carries on business, in Broadway, Southwark—on the 4th of May the prisoner was in his employ, and likewise Bennett and Barrett—I had twenty tons of iron to deliver at a wharf—the prisoner is a carman, and so is Bennett—each of them drove a cart on this occasion—Barrett was sent down to the wharf to see that all the iron was
put into the vessel, and to assist in putting it in—I sent down seven loads before any thing happened—when the seventh had got there the captain discovered something—I was called on by the captain about one o'clock—I asked the prisoner if he had taken away one of these loads, and he denied it—I was by when Hogarth asked him what he had done with the load which he had shot under the archway—he said he had not shot it, but Jack Barrett had—the wharf is about half a mile from the Broadway—it is called Mark Brown's Wharf—the arch is in the railway, near Crucifix-lane—that is out of the road to the wharf—they had no authority whatever to take the goods any where hut from the Broadway to the wharf—I was at the station-house where the prisoner was taken—he asked me if I had been to the King and Queen, at Rotherhithe, for the iron—I made no answer—I had not been there—I have been there since, and found some iron—it appeared to me in all respects similar to what we had lost.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. How long has the prisoner been in your master's employ? A. Ever since the winter—the load on each cart was about 30cwts.—the prisoner went home after I questioned him, and returned again—he went out with two or three loads in the morning, and one or two in the afternoon—Barrett has been in my master's service a long time—he is older than the prisoner.
JOHN HOGARTH . I am clerk to Mr. Plumley. On the 4th of May I asked the prisoner what he had done with one of the loads of iron, which I understood he had placed under the Greenwich railway—he admitted it had been shot there, but said he had not shot it—I said I thought he had, and ordered one of the men to go immediately for a policeman—I went with the officer up to the arch—the prisoner said that was the arch where the iron had been shot, and I saw the dust of old iron on the ground.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say who did shoot it? A. I was very angry, I do not remember whether he did or not—I do not remember hearing any thing pass between him and Harley—I gave him in charge.
JOHN HIGGS ( police-constable M 92.) I was sent for, and took the prisoner for stealing 31 cwts. of iron, on Saturday the 4th of May—Mr. Hogarth wished me to take him under the railway arch where the iron had been shot—we went there—it was one of the arches near Crucifix-lane—the prisoner said he was in a public-house drinking a pint of beer, and some one drove away the horse and cart—he did not know who it was—he said he did not know what public-house it was, but it was in Tooley-street, and he found the horse and cart under the railway arch, the iron shot there, and no one was with it—while at the station-house he sat down, and said he had been led astray by Jack Barrett; that Jack Barrett had come to him in Tooley-street, and they both drove the horse and cart down Dog and Bear yard, and shot the iron under the railway arch, near Crucifix-lane; That Jack Barrett was to look after the iron—he said that jack Barrett and him were colleagued together in stealing the iron, they were Going to sell it, and part the money between them; and if I would let Him go that afternoon, he would go to the King and Queen iron-foundry, Where he should find the iron and the parties.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you sure he said Jack Barrett was to look after the iron? A. Yes—and he was to go home—I did not tell him what he was to go to the station-house for—Mr. Hogarth had told him.
JOSEPH DUNN . I live in Church-street, St. John's, and keep horses
and carts for hire. On Saturday morning, the 4th of May, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I was standing near Cotton's wharf, in Tooley-street— Jack Barrett came to another man, and was engaging with him to do a job for him—I touched him twice over the back with my whip, and he turned round, and said—"Ah Joe I have got a job for you"—he took me to Dog and Bear-yard, and there was some old iron shot under the arch of the rail-road, but How it came there I do not know—it was put into my cart—I drove it to the King and Queen, where he told me—he was with me, and gave me 6 s.—he had 4 l. on account of it—I saw it in his hand.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner was not with you? A. No.
JOSEPH DYER . I am clerk to Messrs. Howard and Co., proprietors of the King and Queen iron works, Rotherhithe. On the 4th of May Joseph Dunn and another man, who I understand was Barrett, came to my master's premises with a load of iron—I gave him 4 l. for it—it was worth about 10 l.—we should have weighed it on the following Monday, and paid him the remainder—from 6 l. to 6 l. 10 s. per ton—we knew nothing of him, but we buy iron if it is of the usual description, and nothing suspicions about it.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you seen Barrett before? A. I cannot say that I had—I might have seen him.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did Hurley afterwards see the iron? and Mr. Hogarth saw it.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

1998. THOMAS GILES was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 221bs. weight of lead, value 1 s. 6 d. the goods of John Thome, his master, being fixed to a building.
GEORGE SYKES . I am a bricklayer. I work for Mr. John Thome—he is a brewer, and lives in South Lambeth—the prisoner was his undergardener, and worked by the day—on the 18th of May I left, and did not go again till the 21st—as I was going along I met Mr. James Thome, who told me there had been some lead missed—but on the 18th I had missed fifteen feet—fifteen feet from the gutter of the stable had been gone on the Saturday, and the remaining fifteen were gone on the Tuesday morning—it was all gone—the prisoner had access to the place—I saw some lead afterwards fitted to the place—it matched exactly—I have no doubt it came from that gutter.
THOMAS EMERSON ( police-constable V 142.) I met the prisoner at half-past seven o'clock on the 20th of May, carrying this lead on his arm, near to the Crown and Thistle public-house—I crossed, and asked what he had got—he said, "A bit of lead"—I took him, and on the way to the station-house he ran from me—I took him again, and then he said he had had the lead two years from Mr. Cubitt's premises—I found where it came from, and saw it fitted to the prosecutor's gutter—it tallied in all respects—about thirty feet had been taken, and this is part of it.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18390617-1999]
1999. ANTHONY GORMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 1 bag, value 1 s., and 78 lbs. weight of nails, value 2 l. 8 s., the goods of Robert Owst and another. And ELIZABETH LAWRENCE , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the statute, &c.
GORMAN pleaded GUILTY . Aged 12.— Confined Six Months.
ROBERT OWST . I am a hoop-merchant, and have one partner. We carry on business at Bermondsey. On the 20th of May we discovered 78lbs. weight of nails, but we had previously lost many other parcels—they were a peculiar sort, but there are other nails of the same sort—I went, from information, to the prisoner Lawrence's marine-store shop, in Mill-street—when I got there she and her husband were at home—I waited till the officer came—I had one of these nails in my pocket—I asked her husband if he had any nails of that kind—he said he had—I said I wished to see them—he went into the back-room, but could not find them there—he then said they were up stairs—he went up stairs, and brought down a sack containing the nails, in a bag—they were the same sort I had had stolen from me, and the bag must have been mine, as it had my initials on it—I think the prisoner Lawrence was silent, but her husband said she had bought them—there was a good deal of fretting and crying from her, but I believe she said but little—this is the bag and nails.
JOHN TAYLOR ( police-constable I 73.) I went to the house respecting the nails—the prisoner's husband said they had bought a quantity of nails of that description, and they were up stairs—I went up with him, and brought down the sack—the bag of nails was in it—I then took the husband to the station-house, the woman begged I would not take her down, but allow a person to remain with her—she was not in a fit state to go and I left a man with her—her husband said the nails were purchased on the Saturday night, but he did not buy them.
BRIDGET GORMAN . I am the mother of Anthony Gorman. I know the prisoner Lawrence by sight—I went and cautioned her three or four days to buy nothing of my boy—and the last time I went her husband told me he would not—I went to the prisoner, because she so often bought of boys—I thought my boy had got into bad company, and I told her not to buy of him—I did not take my son with me, but she knew him well—she used to take him in, and give him some meat, and he used to go in and out there—I have cautioned her not to buy of my son, when he was there himself.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You told her your son was a thief? A. Yes, I cannot keep my son from bad company—I have a husband, but he is a very drunken bad man—I spoke to the prisoner above three weeks before my son was taken—her husband followed me out, and gave me his hand and word that he would not buy any thing of him—there were two or three others concerned in this. (Lawrence received a good character.)
LAWRENCE — GUILTY . Aged 38.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18390617-2000]
2000. GEORGE BATCHELOR was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of May, 2 chisels, value 5 s.; 2 taps, value 13 s.; 1 pair of dyes, value 1 l.; 1 tap-wrench, value 2 s.; 1 mandrell, value 5 s. 1 spanner, value 3 s.; 2 files, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 plummer-block, value 1 s. and 1 pattern, value 3 s.; the goods of William Flowerdew Sadler.
WILLIAM FLOWERDEW SADLER . I live in Tooley-street, and am a millwright and engineer—the prisoner was my journeyman, and had been
my apprentice—he had the use of my tools to work on my premises—I lost tools from time to time, and obtained a warrant to search his lodging, at No. 41, Union-street—I knew his workshop and his sleeping-room—he was present when the search was made—we found a great number of tools, some of which I can speak to—he had no permission from me to carry my tools to his lodging—he never did any work for me at his lodging—when I found the tools, he pretended some of them were his, and some he acknowledged were mine.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you receive 50 l. premium with him? A. Yes—his apprenticeship expired on the 6th of May, or about then—I did not request him to stay with me as a weekly servant—it was his own wish, and I consented—James Ezekiel Clark is in my employ—I had a paper made out of the prisoner's lost time while he was my apprentice—I believe this is the statement—( looking at it)—his wages were to be 36 s. a week, as a journeyman—did not demand of him 25 l. 4 s. 3 d.—I said that was the amount of his lost time, if he felt disposed to make it up to me—he is a very fair workman, for a young man—he is fit to go into any shop—he had been in the habit of working over-hours, before his apprenticeship expired, in my shop, and very likely in his own.
Q. When you presented this statement to him did he say, "Why, 25 l. 4 s. 3 d., you might as well make me your apprentice again, you know I was never absent except I was ill?" A. He could not say so rightfully and with justice, because he was absent a great many days—I will not swear that he did not say so—I showed this paper to his father—I did not refuse to pay his wages on the first Saturday night, on account of this being due to me—I paid him at once—I did not bring the statement forward on the second Saturday night—I never pressed the "thing—there was one paper presented to him, and one to his father, I believe—I have his indentures now; but I told his father he should have them at any time—I did certainly refuse to deliver up the indentures on the day the prisoner first demanded them, when his time was out—they expired about the 6th of May, and he continued to work till about the 22nd—I know Thomas Skipper, but am not acquainted with him—he is no relation or connexion of mine—he may be a distant relation—I believe I saw his wife yesterday.
Q. Did you employ Skipper to go to the prisoner's premises before you obtained a search warrant? A. He pointed out the premises to Sadler, a nephew of mine, before I got the warrant—he is not here—I do not know that Skipper has absconded from a charge of felony—I cannot say when I saw Skipper last.
Q. Upon your oath, have you not yourself sold the prisoner tools? A. No—I never took any money for tools from him in my life—I never sold him any tools—I never sold him a lot of tools for 6 s. in the presence of a man of the name of Speechly—I never sold any to any person living—it was perhaps a month before the prisoner was taken that I saw Skipper—I never took particular notice.
Q. Upon your oath, did you not see Skipper on the very morning upon which you took the prisoner into custody? A. Yes—I saw his wife yesterday in the neighbourhood of the Borough—I met her by accident—I do not know whether it was by accident or not—I did not send for her—I do not think I saw her the day before—I have not seen her every day for the last ten days—I have seen her once or twice this week—I do not know her that Skipper has run away—I have reason to believe it, when I
see this paper (the "Hue and Cry Gazette")—I have not inquired what he had done—I care nothing about it, I have enough to do with my own business—I have been told that he had been assisting the prisoner in some carpenter's work that he was doing while in my service—I believe he assisted him to do some jobs, that is all I know—my nephew told me so—I swear I never sold the prisoner any tools—my nephew went with Clark and Skipper to the prisoner's place before the warrant was issued—the prisoner was in the habit of using my tools to work over hours in my employ during his apprenticeship—I never permitted my workmen to take home some of my tools to work with in over hours, to the best of my recollection—I believe I could safely swear that—I never permitted my apprentices to do so as well as my journeymen—I do not know of the prisoner having bought any tools of a Mr. Palmer—( examining the property)—I believe these two castings came off my pattern—I have every reason to believe they came from my shop—this is a coal chisel, or more properly, a cast-steel chisel—the name has been hammered out, but I think I can see where the name has been hammered out—it is made exactly the same as we make our chisels—this is made out of a cast-steel bar—I mean to swear to this from my belief that my name has once been on it, and is now hammered out—this other chisel has my name on it—here is another article has my name—these other tools I can swear to, but they are not all named—I have thousands of tools in my shop that have no name on them—I can swear to them by having them so many years in my use, and I can bring the remainder of the nuts and tools to fit them—they are not made of one size—these nuts would fit on the tools that I have—those tools that I can swear to, are of very little value without the other portion of the tools.
Q. Was it not on Skipper, your nephew, and Clark's information that you took the prisoner? A. My nephew pointed out the place—I took the prisoner from finding the tools—I got the warrant, as I had strong reason to believe he had been robbing me a long time—I discovered the place through my nephew, who is not here to-day, he is very particularly engaged putting up a steam-engine—Skipper did not come to my house to my knowledge after he had been to the prisoner's—I did not see him to my knowledge—I went out—I cannot say whether I saw Skipper or not—I did not employ him to go there—I did not know of his going before he went—I must know it certainly before we got to the place—I was informed he could point out to me where he thought there was something going on wrong—my nephew told me that—I did not send Skipper to the place—I did not go with him—he said he had been employed there by the prisoner—I never saw Skipper there—he told me he knew where the place was.
Q. Will you swear he did not tell you he had been there and seen some tools? A. I will not swear it—I said to the prisoner, "George, you know we have for a long time been robbed of a great many tools"—he said, "I know you have, master"—I said, "If you have no objection we will walk to your premises"—he said he had no objection, and we went part of the way—Clark and my foreman were in company—the prisoner said, "I will go with nobody but you"—he walked with me to Union-hall, that was after I got the warrant.
COURT. Q. Was it not on Skipper's information that you would find some tools at the prisoner's that you got the warrant? A. Yes.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did not the prisoner give you up the keys of his shop before you got the warrant? A. No, alter—he gave them up to the officer
—we walked together till we came to the office—I said to him, "Wait here"—Clark and Sadler saw that he did wait there—I fetched the officer out and the warrant—he waited, but I suspect the others would not have let him run away—I told them (but not in his hearing) not to let him go—I did not wish to hurt his feelings—I did not wish to lose him, nor did I wish to lose my tools, but I have lost some scores of pounds' worth—when the officer came, the prisoner took us to the place where the tools were—I do not know what has become of Skipper—I know nothing about him.
Q. Did it not occur to you that your distant relation might have put some of these tools there? A. No, I did not ask him—I have no reason to know whether he did or not—the prisoner's workshop is not above half a mile from my place.
COURT. Q. When was the last application on your part, when you told the prisoner you thought him bound to make good the time he had lost during his apprenticeship? A. I cannot say whether it was just before he was out of his time, or after—the account is 25 l. 4 s. 3 d.—it was about the 22nd, I think—that was the day I got the warrant—I do not think that I ever heard of any tools of mine supposed to be on the prisoner's premises till about the 22nd—it was the same day—I do not remember that I had heard it before—Clark did not give me any information—the prisoner said it was a hard thing for him to work to make that up, and I said it was a hard thing for me to lose it—I said to his father, "I shall give him up his indentures"—I left it to himself—I gave him a chance of working it out if he chose—he did not choose—I never demanded the money of the father—I had the account made out to show his father the time he had lost, and to see whether it was not right that he should make me some remuneration for it—I thought it good to let him know, and his father also, the time he had lost in my service, not taking into account any holidays or illness, but just working time—Clark made out this account—I certainly knew the prisoner had been absent when he first demanded his indentures—I have been losing tools for three years, but till the 22nd of May, I never took any steps to see whether he had them—I had very great reason to suspect him.
Q. Do you mean that you took any step against the prisoner till you were foiled in this application? A. I could not prove it before—he had been suspected a long time—it was about the 22nd of May that I was told I should find tools at his lodging—I did not set up any claim against him till he had asked for his indentures.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you not say, after the indentures were demanded, that you would make an example of him? A. No, I should be as ham to make that remark.
JAMES EZEKIEL CLARK . I am clerk to Mr. Sadler. It is my business to give out tools—I use them at times in the shop myself—I know this coal chisel, and these other things—we were aware that the prisoner had a lodging—I was not aware that he did any work at his shop—I know some of these other tools.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you know these by? A. I know this mandrill because it has been used for turning worms upon, and the centre has been drilled out—I do not know Tom Skipper—I am not aware of such a person.
Q. I mean Skipper, the distant relation of your master's? A. Oh, the
"nephew-in-law;" I had forgotten his name—I have no acquaintance with him—his name is Skipper, and, I think, Thomas—I saw him last about a fortnight ago, as I was going home to dinner, in Back-street, Horsleydown—I had seen him at Mr. Sadler's shop, when he came to see his brother-in-law, Mr. James Sadler—he has been there several times, where the tools were about in the shop—I went to the prisoner's shop with my master, and others—I do not know that Skipper had been to the prisoner's before—my master never told me—the journeymen and apprentices have not been in the habit of borrowing my master's tools—no such thing ever happened, to my knowledge—I know Sophia Lewis, and Mrs. Rose Cook—I do not know Mrs. Elizabeth Newland by name—I may know her—( Looking at a person)—I met her this morning in Tooley-treet—I did not know her by name—I have not know her long—I have known her not six months—I am not aware where I saw her first—I made out this account of the lost time—my employer told me to do it—he showed it to the prisoner, and asked him what arrangement he would come to—he gave him the account, and said he would expect him to make him compensation, by paying the money, or working it out, and he said he could not think of doing hat—this was on the 5th of May, and the search warrant was obtained on the 22nd.
Q. Do you remember the Saturday night after the prisoner had agreed to work at 36 s. a week, his coming to be paid? A. Yes; and he was paid—my master said to him, "George, what time are you going to allow me?"—he said he could not allow him any, and then he had his full wages paid—on the following Saturday he was paid again—I did not see my master produce the paper again—the prisoner demanded his indentures at twelve o'clock on the 6th of June—my master refused to deliver them till he was satisfied—the indentures were demanded several times—I cannot say How many—I cannot tell bow long I had seen Skipper before the prisoner was taken—I had been at home three days—I was not well—I was sent for—I saw Skipper on the day the prisoner was taken—after he was taken, not before—I saw him at Union-street, outside the hall—I am certain I had not seen him the day before—I am certain I had seen him on my master's premises, talking to his brother-in-law.
Q. Have you not acknowledged to Cook, Lewis, and Newland, or to either of them, that you were aware of the prisoner having had taps and dies and several other tools for the last three years—that you had often lent them to him, and he had brought them back, that you knew of his having some knife-shanks—and that the other apprentices and journeymen frequently had tools lent to them? A. Never.
Q. Now attend to these words, "But if we don't get Batchelor transported for fourteen years, I shall be out of employment, and Sadler will have nothing to do"—upon your solemn oath, did you ever say to any of these persons any thing of that kind? A. Never—they spoke to me on the subject of his being in prison—I said I was extremely sorry, that it was all his own fault, he had no occasion to do so, he had a good master and good employment—that was the principal conversation—I might have said something more, but not what you said—I might have said as far as this, that I was afraid he would be transported—I believe that was all I said—I believe that to be the whole conversation—I have seen the prisoner buy tools of a man—I cannot recollect what sort—I never saw him buy castings—it was merely tools for his own use—it is a long time
ago—he bought them of a man named Hooper—the castings I have sworn to as being my master's, were cast at Palmer's—I never knew the prisoner buy a lot of tools for 6 s.—I have known my master give him two or three tools in the former part of his apprenticeship—he gave him a saw, and a pair of clippers, and compasses—I am not aware of any thing else—my master never knew of his working any where but at his premises.
Q. Then what did he give him these tools for? A. These sort of wood tools the men generally find themselves—apprentices generally do so in my master's service—I did not know where I was to look for the tools when I went to the prisoner's, I had no notion where I was going—my master, and I, and his nephew, and the prisoner, all went to the police-office—my master and the prisoner had some conversation—I saw the prisoner give up his keys to the officer—we went to his place—the prisoner went through the passage to the back shop, then he returned from the back shop suddenly, as if he had made a mistake, and opened the bed-room door first—he did not wait any where before he went Co the police-office—I had nothing said to me about what I was to do—I had not the prisoner in custody—he walked to the hall voluntarily—I was never told to take care of him—nothing of the kind.
ELISHA CREASY . I am an officer—I went to the prisoner's bed-room and shop—we found some tools, which were brought away—before the Magistrate, the prisoner pointed out some tools which were his, and some he said were his master's—the warrant was obtained first, and Mr. Sadler came with the prisoner to the door—I went out with the warrant, and went to his lodging—he offered me every assistance to search his place—the taps and tools were found there—this chisel I found in his jacket pocket, with Mr. Sadler's name on it.
MR. CLARKSON called
SOPHIA LEWIS . My husband is barge-toaster to the Waterman's Company, and a fellowship-porter—a lad whom I brought up is in the prosecutor's service—I don't know the prisoner, I never saw him but once—I know Mrs. Cook and Mrs. Newland—I know the witness Clark very Well—on the 5th of June I met him and walked to the house of Mrs. Cook—Mrs. Newland was at that house—the prisoner being in custody, was the subject of conversation—Clark said he was quite aware of the prisoner haying taps and dies, that he had lent them frequently to him, and he had brought them back again—he said he was aware of his having knife-shanks, and Mr. Sadler had frequently lent him tools to work with—he said, "If we don't get Batchelor transported for fourteen years, I shall be out of employ and Sadler will have nothing to do."
ROSE COOK . I am the wife of William Cook, a lighterman—on Wednesday, the 5th of June, Sophia Lewis came to my house with Clark, about ten minutes after one o'clock—Elizabeth Newland was there—the discourse turned on a young man of the name of George Batchelor, being in prison—I never knew him nor the prosecutor, in my life—Clark said he was aware that the prisoner had had taps, and dies, and other tools, and he had frequently lent them to him, and he had brought them back—he said he was aware of his having had some knife-shanks, for Mr. Sadler lent the journeymen and apprentices tools to work with, and he said, "If we don't get him transported for fourteen years, I shall be out of employ, and Sadler will have nothing to do"—I was so shocked, that I wrote to the prisoner's father, (I did not know the address, till Clark gave it me,) and after doing that I went to Mr. Humphrey's office, and stated this.
ELIZABETH NEWLAND . I am an acquaintance of Rose Cook's—I was at her house on Wednesday, the 5th of June—Mrs. Sophia Lewis came in and Clark—I did not know Clark before, I never saw him—I do not know the prisoner, I never saw him in my life—the discourse turned on the subject of the imprisonment of a young man of the name of Batchelor—Clark said he was quite aware of his having taps, and dies, and knife-shanks, and several other tools—that they were frequently lent him, and his master was not averse to it, provided he brought them back, and that James Sadler was aware of it—he had lent them several times as well as he had, and if he was not transported out of the country, Mr. Sadler would lose his connexion, and he would be out of employment.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390617-2001]
2001. GEORGE BATCHELOR was again indicted for stealing, on the 1st of May, 1 chisel, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of William Tilby.
WILLIAM TILBY . I am a millwright and engineer, and live in Blackman-street, in the Borough—Mr. Sadler has been in the habit of using my turning-lathe—the prisoner used to come to work at it sometimes—he sometimes brought Mr. Sadler's tools to work with, and sometimes he used mine, and the tools might have got mixed unintentionally—this chisel is an article he might have used at the lathe occasionally—my tools are all marked—this other has Mr. Sadler's name on it—they might be easily taken one for the other—I am not a particular friend of Mr. Sadler's—he sent for me, and said, "There was a certain article of mine found in Batchelor's lodgings"—I don't know that he had any great desire for me to prosecute.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Are you a friend of Mr. Sadler's? A. I have known him many years, and have lent him my lathe occasionally—I have been in the Queen's Bench prison, in the Marshalsea, and Horsemonger-lane—I have not been in the Borough Compter—I had an apprentice of the name of Robert—I believe he lodged in the same house with the 'prisoner, but I heard him say that he left the lodging previously to Batchelor and him falling out about something.
Q. Did not the prisoner say that Robert had been working on the premises, and that either that tool had got mixed, or Robert had left it there? A. I do not know.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390617-2002]
2002. ANN SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of June, 2 half-crowns, 5 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Ellen Foley.
ELLEN FOLEY . I live with my mother—within these last two months the prisoner had been lodging in our house—I sell fruit in the street)—she asked on the Sunday morning to sleep in my room—her husband and herself had a row —they were fighting that day—she is not married—she told me she wished to sleep in my room—her husband was not at home that night—she laid on the ground and put her head on the foot of the bed—I had two half-crown pieces, 5 s., and one sixpence—they were put on one side of my pocket, and another side had 2 s. and 1 1/2 d., but that she left—I put my pocket under my pillow—I awoke between six and seven o'clock, she was then gone—she had given me no notice that she would get up early—I did not see her till six or seven o'clock in the afternoon—the policeman took her and found on her one of the shillings
I had—when I took it I put it into my mouth and bit it—I find the same mark on it now—the half-crowns have not been found.
THOMAS WINCH ( police-constable M 40.) I had information of this—I found the prisoner in York-street, London-road, in a public-house, rather in liquor—I found 2 s., one sixpence, and 1 1/2 d. on her, and one of the shillings the prosecutrix identified—the prisoner said what she had was 2 s., as near as she could guess, and her husband had given her half a cronw in the morning.
Prisoner's Defenee. I had gone into the house, and had a pint of beer—I had not time to drink it before I was taken—I am quite innocent—the house is open night and day.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

2003. CHARLES THRESHER LEONARD BUCK , and WILLIAM DAWSON , were indicted for a conspiracy.
( The evidence was of too indelicate a nature for publication.)
GUILTY . Fined One Shilling each, and Discharged.
